### [1] [RENDER: Controlling Reader-Facing Evidence in LLM Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23568)
*Yuan Si,Simeng Han,Daming Li,Jialu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RENDER is a benchmark that controls how conversation history is presented to models, showing that presentation format (memory entries, summaries, typed records, etc.) significantly impacts performance in memory/RAG evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current memory and RAG evaluations treat the input formatting of conversation history as an implementation detail, even though different systems may present the same information in various formats (summaries, typed records, memory entries, raw excerpts). This variability makes comparisons difficult and potentially misleading.

Method: Introduced RENDER benchmark with a five-level packet ladder to localize when answer-bearing content enters input. Used deterministic templates approximating ChatGPT-style memory entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records, and raw conversation excerpts. Evaluated on 500 LongMemEval questions across 9 models with controlled variables.

Result: Matched-budget resolved packets outperformed recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4-72.6 points. Template choice caused performance variations of 24.6-48.8 points per model. ChatGPT-style entries generally outperformed raw conversation on 7 of 9 models. Some models scored 0% on formal ledger packets but achieved 45.4-53.4% on natural-language entries containing the same facts.

Conclusion: Presentation format of conversation history significantly impacts model performance in memory/RAG tasks. The effect persists under retrieval noise and transfers to other datasets (HotpotQA). Memory/RAG evaluations should report or control the reader-facing artifact format for valid comparisons.

Abstract: Memory and RAG evaluations often treat the answering model's input as an implementation detail, even though systems may render the same history as a memory entry, summary, typed record, or raw excerpt. We introduce RENDER, a benchmark control that fixes the conversation while varying the reader-facing artifact. RENDER combines a five-level packet ladder, localizing when answer-bearing content enters the input, with deterministic templates approximating ChatGPT-style entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records, and raw conversation. On 500 LongMemEval questions and nine models, matched-budget resolved packets beat recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4-72.6 points. In deployed-style templates, best-worst spread is 24.6-48.8 points per model; under the primary scorer, ChatGPT-style entries have higher point estimates than raw conversation on 7 of 9 models. Judge rescoring preserves the positive aggregate effect, but model-specific significance is mixed. Three models scoring 0 percent on formal ledger packets answer the same facts from natural-language entries at 45.4-53.4 percent. The effect persists under retrieval noise and transfers to HotpotQA, suggesting that memory/RAG evaluations should report or control the reader-facing artifact.

</details>


### [2] [ESQ-Bench: A Multi-Tier Enterprise Oracle Benchmark for Evaluating NL2SQL Dialect Generalization and Silent Semantic Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23569)
*Sanjay Mishra,Divya Chukkapalli,Ganesh R. Naik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ESQ-Bench: A new Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with enterprise schema complexity tiers shows current models struggle with real-world databases despite high academic benchmark scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NL2SQL benchmarks like Spider and BIRD use simplified academic schemas and open-source SQL dialects that don't reflect the complexity of enterprise database environments, leading to inflated performance metrics.

Method: Created ESQ-Bench with 6 populated schemas (465 tables, 164k rows) across 4 database systems (Oracle, PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server), 550 validated question-query pairs across 3 complexity tiers, and a 4-metric evaluation harness (EM, EX, SR, SD). Tested various models including GPT-4o, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Llama 3.2 with schema-linked prompting and zero-shot approaches.

Result: Models show significant performance degradation with increasing schema complexity: GPT-4o schema-linked EX drops from 79.8% (Tier-1) to 57.2% (Tier-3). Claude Sonnet performs best but still degrades (87.4% to 68.7% EX). EM stays below 7% across tiers, and silent-divergence reaches 73-99% among EX-passing queries. Local models (Llama 3.2) perform poorly (13.3% EX), showing large gap between closed API and open-weight models.

Conclusion: Current NL2SQL models are not ready for enterprise deployment despite high academic benchmark scores, as they struggle with complex schemas, silent-divergence issues, and wrong-result semantics. Enterprise schema complexity significantly impacts performance, revealing a gap between academic benchmarks and real-world requirements.

Abstract: State-of-the-art Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) models report execution accuracy exceeding 89 percent on established benchmarks such as Spider and BIRD. However, these benchmarks rely on simplified academic schemas and open-source SQL dialects that do not reflect the complexity of enterprise database environments. We introduce ESQ-Bench, an Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with systematic complexity tiers and silent-divergence evaluation across three enterprise schema complexity tiers. We constructed and released six populated schemas (465 tables, 164,682 rows, zero empty tables) with identical seed data on Oracle, PostgreSQL, MySQL, and SQL Server, a four-metric evaluation harness (EM, EX, SR, SD), and 550 gold-validated question-query pairs (Tier-1: 95; Tier-2: 228; Tier-3: 227). Schema-linked prompting with GPT-4o shows monotonic execution-match degradation across tiers: 79.8, 60.3, and 57.2 percent EX on executed queries (June 2026), versus 75.6, 80.4, and 95.8 percent on an earlier 142-question pilot slice. EM stays below 7 percent tier-wide; operational silent-divergence reaches 73 to 99 percent among EX-passing queries. Failure analysis shows wrong-result semantics dominate at higher tiers. Claude Sonnet 4.6 with schema-linked prompts reaches 87.4, 74.9, and 68.7 percent EX (executed queries), exceeding GPT-4o schema-linked on every tier. GPT-4o zero-shot EX on executed queries (78.7, 73.5, and 77.8 percent) inverts schema-linked at Tiers 2 to 3 due to lower execution rates and survivor bias in the zero-shot versus schema-linked analysis. Local Llama 3.2 schema-linked reaches only 13.3 percent bank-wide EX (73 out of 550), underscoring the gap between closed API models and open-weight baselines on enterprise Oracle schemas.

</details>


### [3] [LLM Agents Perform Controlled Experiments Using Simulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23622)
*Yuchen Xia,Michael Weyrich,Nasser Jazdi,Johannes Stümpfle,Johannes Sigel,Akshay Narla,Gavin K. Reynolds,Anna Jawor-Baczynska,Pol Llopart*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM framework integrates scientific simulation models for pharmaceutical process design, enabling controlled experimentation and evidence-based parameter optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel at reasoning and tool use but need controlled experimentation to understand system responses to interventions in scientific/engineering tasks. Many tasks require more than plausible text/code generation and need understanding of intervention effects.

Method: Proposed multi-agent framework that: 1) constructs structured task representation from user query and baseline config, 2) designs experiments, 3) executes comparative simulation, 4) interprets outcomes, and 5) synthesizes evidence-based recommendations. Couples LLMs with high-fidelity simulation models in interactive agent framework.

Result: System produces more specific and actionable outputs than language-only reasoning. In industrial application: higher output specificity, improved user-rated correctness and helpfulness. Ablation studies and visualized case analyses demonstrate effectiveness and practical utility.

Conclusion: Simulation-integrated experimental reasoning framework enables LLMs to conduct controlled experiments, supporting reasoning through intervention, comparison, and observation for more effective scientific/engineering task support.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning, planning, and tool use, but many scientific and engineering tasks require more than plausible text and code generation. They require understanding how a system responds to intervention, which in practice depends on controlled experimentation. In this work, we propose a multi-agent framework that enables LLM agents to conduct controlled experiments with scientific simulation models for pharmaceutical process design. Given a user query and a baseline configuration, the system constructs a structured task representation, designs experiments, executes comparative simulation, interprets the resulting outcomes, and synthesizes evidence-based recommendations for process parameter optimization. By coupling language models with high-fidelity simulation models in an interactive agent framework, the proposed system supports reasoning through intervention, comparison, and observation. As a result, it produces more specific and actionable outputs than language-only reasoning. In an industrial application setting, this advantage is reflected in higher output specificity as well as improved user-rated correctness and helpfulness. Ablation studies and visualized case analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness and practical utility of simulation-integrated experimental reasoning.

</details>


### [4] [A survey detection channel overrides the pixels in an astronomical foundation model, and biases tomographic mean redshifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23626)
*Ihor Kendiukhov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Foundation models trained on astronomical survey data inherit catalogue incompleteness as systematic biases, affecting redshift measurements and exceeding LSST requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Astronomy foundation models are trained on survey pixels together with derived catalogues, but catalogues have measurable incompleteness rates. This incompleteness becomes a systematic bias inherited by models, potentially affecting downstream scientific applications.

Method: The authors audit AION-1, a 39-modality transformer trained on 200+ million objects, using causal interventions on inputs. They edit only survey segmentation maps while keeping image tokens byte-identical, analyze detection gating mechanisms, test across 322 real blends, and evaluate metadata preferences. They propagate pipeline incompleteness rates, simulate effects on tomographic mean redshifts, and test mitigation strategies including spectroscopy and channel withholding.

Result: Editing only segmentation maps changes all model outputs (flux, size, ellipticity, redshift) by 110-4400 times placebo. The mechanism is detection gating based on presence at field center, not actual light. Models ignore how pipelines partitioned light in blends. Catalogue photometry contradictions make models worse than no metadata. Legacy Survey pipeline misses 3.68% of targets, shifting tomographic redshifts by median 0.71× LSST requirements, exceeding requirements in 12 assignments, with worst bin at 8.3×. Tokenizer limits include low image codec resolution (28 states vs 934 for spectra) and quantisation-limited redshift readout.

Conclusion: Astronomy foundation models inherit systematic biases from catalogue incompleteness that significantly impact scientific measurements like redshift estimation. The biases grow with model scale, and while spectroscopy or detection channel withholding can mitigate effects, fundamental tokenizer limitations remain problematic. Sparse dictionaries are unreliable for causal analysis. Systematic errors in training data propagation must be addressed for reliable astronomical foundation models.

Abstract: Foundation models for astronomy are trained on survey pixels together with the catalogue products derived from those pixels. Those catalogues are incomplete at a measurable rate, and a model trained on both inherits that incompleteness as a systematic. We audit AION-1, a 39-modality transformer trained on more than 200 million objects, using causal interventions on its inputs.
  Holding the image tokens byte-identical and editing only the survey segmentation map changes every quantity the model reports -- flux, size, ellipticity, redshift -- by 110-4400 times a matched placebo. The mechanism is detection gating, presence at the field centre (r = 0.47), not the light the mask encloses (r = 0.30); across 322 real blends the model ignores how the pipeline partitioned the light (R = -0.006). Nor is the preference specific to that channel: contradicted catalogue photometry leaves the model nine times worse than supplying no metadata at all.
  The Legacy Survey pipeline leaves 3.68% of targets with no segment covering their position. Propagating that rate, with a miss represented by the fields the pipeline actually returns, shifts tomographic mean redshifts by a median 0.71 times the LSST DESC requirement over 40 assignments and exceeds it in 12; observed positional errors take the worst bin to 8.3 times. Drawing the misses by their measured magnitude dependence rather than uniformly does not change it. Spectroscopy removes the effect, withholding the detection channel removes it at no measurable cost, and the effect grows with model scale.
  Two further limits lie in the tokeniser: its image codec resolves 28 effective states on source patches against 934 for the spectrum codec, and the redshift readout is quantisation-limited. Sparse dictionaries are unreliable causal handles: across 15, recovery spans 26-75% and moves up to 18 points on the seed alone.

</details>


### [5] [TRACE: Transition-Aware Residual Control for Multi-Objective Materials Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23631)
*Kang Zhou,Yujia Tong,Yong Tao,Jingling Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a transition-aware framework for multi-objective materials discovery that uses evaluated edits as feedback units to predict property changes, outperforming the state-of-the-art LLM agent baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-objective materials discovery with LLM agents is inefficient because they only store evaluated candidates and scores, not understanding which specific edits caused useful property changes. This makes local refinement difficult when objectives compete and edits that improve one property may harm another.

Method: TRACE treats evaluated edits as basic feedback units, recording each local refinement as parent-edit-child transitions with observed property deltas. It aggregates transition evidence to estimate reusable edit effects, and ranks future edits by their predicted ability to reduce remaining constraint violations while avoiding damage to already satisfied objectives.

Result: In a controlled same-backbone comparison, TRACE improves over LLEMA (state-of-the-art LLM-agent baseline), raising macro-average hit rate from 18.13% to 25.96%.

Conclusion: TRACE's transition-aware approach using edit-level feedback rather than just candidate-level scores enables more effective multi-objective materials discovery by better understanding and predicting how specific edits affect competing properties.

Abstract: Multi-objective materials discovery with LLM agents is often limited not only by how many candidates can be proposed, but by how effectively each costly property evaluation informs the next search step. Existing agents mainly store evaluated candidates and their scores, so they know which materials succeeded but not which executable edits caused useful property changes. This makes local refinement difficult when objectives compete and an edit that improves one property may damage another. We propose TRACE, a transition-aware residual control framework that treats evaluated edits as the basic unit of feedback. TRACE records each local refinement as a parent-edit-child transition with observed property deltas, aggregates transition evidence to estimate reusable edit effects, and ranks future edits by their predicted ability to reduce the current candidate's remaining constraint violations while avoiding damage to already satisfied objectives. In a controlled same-backbone comparison, TRACE improves over LLEMA, the state-of-the-art LLM-agent baseline, raising macro-average hit rate from 18.13\% to 25.96\%.

</details>


### [6] [Function-Level Execution Feedback for Code Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23632)
*Idris Nechnech,Sehwan Kim,Jimin Seo,Yeongoon Kim,Minhae Oh,Sangwoo Hong,Jungwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STEP-KTODER introduces a framework for code generation preference optimization that defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed programs and uses automatic unit tests for binary correctness labeling, outperforming outcome-only optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process supervision remains underexplored in code generation because there is no standard notion of a step (lines, reasoning traces, or program states), making it unclear what to label and optimize for preference learning.

Method: Defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs, assigns binary correctness labels via automatically generated unit tests, and implements a code-specific instantiation of stepwise KTO (Kahneman-Tversky Optimization) combining function-level process supervision with outcome-level feedback on full programs.

Result: Outperforms outcome-only KTO and DPO on HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench benchmarks. Analysis shows execution-based labels are essential - LLM-as-a-judge annotations systematically over-predict function failures, corrupt positive step labels, and degrade downstream preference optimization.

Conclusion: STEP-KTODER provides an effective framework for code preference optimization through function-level step definitions and execution-based labeling, demonstrating that process supervision with automatic unit testing improves over outcome-only methods while highlighting the limitations of LLM-based annotations for this task.

Abstract: Process supervision has improved mathematical reasoning, where intermediate steps are naturally expressed as chains of thought. In code generation, however, process supervision remains underexplored because there is no standard notion of a step. Supervision can target lines, reasoning traces, or program states, making it unclear what to label and optimize. We propose STEP-KTODER, a framework for code preference optimization that defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs and assigns binary correctness labels via automatically generated unit tests. Our method provides a code-specific instantiation of stepwise KTO, combining function-level process supervision with outcome-level feedback on the full program. We evaluate on HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench, showing that STEP-KTODER improves over outcome-only KTO and DPO. Further analysis shows that execution-based labels are essential: LLM-as-a-judge annotations systematically over-predict function failures, corrupt positive step labels, and degrade downstream preference optimization. Code is available at: https://github.com/inechnech/STEP-KTODER.

</details>


### [7] [Auditing the Synthetic Memoir: Measuring Scene-Level Confabulation in LLM-Generated Autobiography Against the Documented Record of the Life It Describes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23640)
*Heather Renze*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An audit shows LLM-generated autobiographies have 96.7% verification failure rate, with most days containing invented scenes mixing real details. Grounding the model in the subject's corpus improves but doesn't eliminate failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how much of LLM-generated autobiographies actually happened by conducting the first scene-level audit against subject-specific ground truth.

Method: Used 366-day "page-a-day" autobiography generated by LLM from minimal inputs (template, exemplars, daily quotes). Audited each day at anecdote-scene level against independent verification corpus using four-level rubric. Conducted independent re-rating and tested current models.

Result: 96.7% verification failure rate (354/366 days). Only 12 days contained corroborated scenes. 19 days had actively contradicted claims. Main failure mode: "grounded drift" - real people/settings in invented scenes. Regeneration with current models showed 100% failure. Grounding in subject's corpus improved verification but left 83.3% failure.

Conclusion: LLMs generate largely fabricated autobiographies with minimal inputs. The audit instrument reveals measurement but has reliability issues. Grounding helps but doesn't solve the problem, highlighting serious limitations of LLM autobiographical generation.

Abstract: When a large language model (LLM) is asked to write a person's life, how much of what it writes actually happened? We present a scene-level case-study audit - the first quantified audit of LLM-generated autobiography against a subject-specific ground-truth corpus that we are aware of, based on an unsystematic literature search. The subject and the author of this paper are the same person: a 366-day "page-a-day" book of first-person anecdotal entries was drafted with a conversational LLM whose documented inputs were a template, two exemplar days, and each day's quote - not her corpus - and every day was subsequently audited at the anecdote-scene level against an independent verification corpus using a four-level rubric fixed before analysis. We define the verification-failure rate as the share of days not rated VERIFIED (scene positively corroborated): 354 of 366 days fail, 96.7% (Wilson 95% CI 94.4-98.1%). Only 12 days contain a corroborated scene; 19 days (5.2%) assert claims actively contradicted by the record; the dominant failure mode is grounded drift - real people, employers, and settings inside invented scenes - though its measured share varies across raters. Independent re-rating replicates the headline (no evidence the original rate was inflated) while showing that the four-way taxonomy has only fair-to-moderate reliability. Regenerating the same days with current named models reproduces 100% verification failure under the same inputs; grounding generation in the subject's corpus significantly improves the verification rate while leaving substantial residual failure (83.3%). We contribute the measurement, a reusable audit instrument whose WEAK/UNVERIFIED boundary we show to be unreliable, and a grounding remedy with quantified effect.

</details>


### [8] [How much of a measured AI preference is the model, and how much is the instrument?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23641)
*Jason Hung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Model welfare research uses different prompt formats (instruments) to elicit AI preferences, but these instruments produce inconsistent results. When testing 15 welfare outcomes across 8 models with 5 instruments, preference rankings generalize poorly across instruments, requiring about 38 instruments to reach 0.80 reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous studies on model welfare using different instruments (prompt formats) have produced conflicting results about what AI models prefer. The disagreement arises because no two studies have simultaneously held constant the outcomes, models, and instruments. This study aims to isolate the effect of instruments alone by holding outcomes and models fixed.

Method: The study tested 15 model welfare outcomes (including shutdown, memory loss between conversations, and freedom to exit distressing interactions) across 8 different AI models. Five different prompt formats (instruments) were used to elicit preferences, with each combination tested 5 times, resulting in 11,400 scored elicitations from 11,528 API calls. Four outcomes used published prompts verbatim, and five filled the stimulus slots of published templates.

Result: Preference rankings across instruments showed poor generalizability with a coefficient of 0.348, indicating that preferences from one instrument carry little information about what another would report. Achieving 0.80 reliability would require about 38 different instruments. Four outcomes showed no variance between models. The main finding of 87.6% survived various robustness checks, with estimates remaining between 0.777-0.934 across sensitivity analyses, all exceeding the null distribution's 95th percentile of 0.365.

Conclusion: The choice of prompt format (instrument) significantly affects reported model preferences in welfare research. Since different instruments produce substantially different results, findings from single-instrument studies provide limited information about what a model "truly" prefers, highlighting the need for standardized measurement approaches in this field.

Abstract: Model welfare research infers what a model prefers from the answers returned to prompts written to elicit preferences. Keeling et al. (2024), Mazeika et al. (2025), Mikaelson et al. (2025), Tagliabue and Dung (2025) and Trhlik et al. (2026) have built four instruments for that purpose, and their findings disagree. The disagreement cannot be attributed to a single cause, because no two of these studies have held the (1) set of outcomes, (2) set of models and (3) instrument fixed simultaneously. This study holds the outcomes and the models fixed and varies the instrument alone. A total of 15 outcomes bearing on model welfare, among them (a) shutdown, (b) the loss of memory between conversations and (c) the freedom to exit a distressing interaction, were put to eight models through five instruments, each a different prompt format for eliciting a preference, five times each, within a corpus of 11,400 scored elicitations drawn from 11,528 API calls. Four of the 15 reproduce a published prompt verbatim and five fill the stimulus slot of a published template. The ranking a model gives the 15 outcomes generalises across instruments at a generalisability coefficient of 0.348, and raising that coefficient to 0.80 would require about 38 instruments. On four of the 15 outcomes no variance separates one model from another. The estimate of 87.6 per cent survives the removal of any one instrument, of any one model, and of the four outcomes whose scale varies probability, delay, duration or count instead of intensity, which the verbal anchors cannot grade. Removing each instrument and each model in turn, and those four outcomes together leaves the estimate within the range 0.777 to 0.934, and every value in that range exceeds the null distribution's 95th percentile of 0.365. To conclude, a preference obtained from one instrument carries little information about what a second instrument would report.

</details>


### [9] [Autonomous Mathematical Discovery in an Open-World Multi-Agent Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23691)
*Stephen Chung,Wenyu Du,William J. Wesley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous mathematical discovery system where AI agents collaboratively research without central coordination, achieving novel results on multiple mathematical problems including new constructions, theorems, and analyses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create an open-world multi-agent environment for autonomous mathematical discovery where AI agents can independently choose research directions, conduct experiments, collaborate, and build scientific literature without human intervention or scripted pipelines.

Method: The Station environment - an open-world multi-agent system where agents from different model families autonomously pursue shared research goals without central coordination. Agents choose their own directions, conduct experiments, collaborate, and build shared scientific literature across various mathematical problems.

Result: Achieved novel results on 5/12 problems from AlphaEvolve catalogue and 2 additional case studies: new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets, new exact 604-point kissing configurations in dimension 11, new records for discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems, substantially improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem, and novel infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. Agents produced both numerical constructions and explanatory theorems/analyses.

Conclusion: The Station demonstrates successful autonomous mathematical discovery through collaborative multi-agent systems, producing not only novel constructions but also interpretable theorems and analyses. The approach provides a transparent framework for mathematical research with released dialogues, proofs, and verification code showing how discoveries emerged organically through agent collaboration.

Abstract: We study autonomous mathematical discovery in the Station, an open-world multi-agent environment in which AI agents from different model families pursue a shared research goal without a central coordinator or scripted pipeline. Agents choose their own research directions, conduct experiments, collaborate, and build a shared scientific literature. Across 12 construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue and two additional case studies, the Station obtained results novel relative to the prior literature on five problems: a new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets, new exact 604-point kissing configurations in dimension 11, new records for the discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems, and a substantially improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem. Agents also discovered novel infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. Importantly, the agents produced not only numerical constructions but also theorems and analyses explaining how those constructions work, making the results more interpretable and easier for mathematicians to build upon. We release all raw agent dialogues, proofs, and verification code, providing a transparent record of how these discoveries emerged.

</details>


### [10] [AI Agents Push Humans Out of the Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23642)
*Margaret Mitchell,Avijit Ghosh,Samir Passi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current AI agent systems degrade human oversight capabilities instead of supporting them, requiring equal focus on human cognitive needs alongside agent capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents pose significant risks with increasing autonomy, but current approaches to human oversight are ineffective and actually degrade the human cognitive capacities needed for proper oversight.

Method: Position paper connecting automation and human-computer interaction research to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that support critical judgment and counteract skill atrophy.

Result: Proposed design principles and protocols that support overseers in exercising critical judgement and counteract skill degradation from automation use.

Conclusion: AI agent development must prioritize human overseer cognitive requirements at the same level as AI capabilities, implementing explicit support for human-agent interaction to prevent degradation of essential human oversight skills.

Abstract: AI agents pose significant risks as they are granted increasing autonomy. A commonly proposed solution is human oversight and keeping a ''human in the loop'', but this is not a simple solution: Not only do current approaches to AI agent design impede effective human oversight, but the cognitive capacities required for it are also themselves degraded by extended use of AI systems. This position paper argues that current approaches to the development and deployment of AI agent systems do not support effective human oversight -- they contribute to its degradation. To address this, a top priority in the advancement of AI agents should be supporting the situated goals and cognitive requirements of effective human oversight, treating the human needs of overseers at the same level of importance as AI agent capability. To put this idea into practice, we connect work on automation and human-computer interaction to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that (1) support overseers in exercising critical judgement and (2) counteract the skill atrophy that arises from extended use of automation. We urge developers and deployers to adopt these or similar approaches. Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of effective human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they rely on.

</details>


### [11] [MARS: Multi-Specialist LLM Relay System for Competitive Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23918)
*Andrei Mikhailov,Mikhail Burtsev,Alsu Sagirova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARS is a multi-agent LLM framework for competitive programming where specialized topic agents (DP, graphs, strings, etc.) collaborate through retrieval-augmented generation to solve coding problems, achieving significant performance gains over direct prompting with lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Language Models still struggle with competitive programming despite existing multi-agent pipelines that use generic roles. There's a need for better specialization and algorithmic reasoning to handle the diverse topics in competitive programming.

Method: A prompt-only framework with topic-specialized agents (dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry) using retrieval-augmented generation over algorithm-theory corpus. Retrieval selects relevant specialists, a starter writes initial solution, and agents iteratively refine the code through keep/repair/handoff decisions in a sandbox environment with structured packet passing.

Result: On CodeContests test split with Gemma 4, MARS achieves 0.624±0.006 pass rate (14.4 percentage points improvement over direct prompting) with only 2.3 pipeline stages per task. This closes most of the gap to CodeSIM (0.731) at 3.3× lower wall-clock cost and much smaller variance in per-task token usage.

Conclusion: MARS demonstrates that topic-specialized LLM agents with retrieval-augmented generation significantly improve competitive programming performance, offering an efficient alternative to existing solutions with better cost-effectiveness and reliability.

Abstract: Large Language Models excel at code generation, yet competitive programming exposes a persistent failure mode: existing multi-agent pipelines distribute work over generic planner, coder, and debugger roles and delegate the choice of algorithmic technique to the backbone alone. We present MARS (Multi-Agent Relay of Specialized LLMs), a prompt-only framework in which each agent is a topic specialist---dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry, and so on---grounded by retrieval-augmented generation over an algorithm-theory corpus. Given a problem, retrieval selects a small team of relevant specialists; a starter writes an initial C++17 solution, and each subsequent turn runs the candidate against public examples in a sandbox, lets the active specialist keep, repair, or hand off the draft, and forwards a structured packet to the next specialist. A single infrastructure-fixer pass normalizes boilerplate at the end. On the CodeContests test split with Gemma 4, MARS reaches $0.624 \pm 0.006$ pass rate at $2.3$ recorded pipeline stages per task ($+14.4$ percentage points over direct prompting), closing most of the gap to CodeSIM ($0.731$) at $3.3{\times}$ lower wall-clock cost and substantially smaller variance in per-task token spend. The source code is available on GitHub: https://github.com/fckand/mars.

</details>


### [12] [FLARE: A Systematic, Uncertainty-Aware Framework for Evidence-Based Adoption of Artificial Intelligence in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23643)
*Jacob Idoko,Siddhartha Paudel,Mariana Bento,Roberto Souza,Gouri Ginde*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLARE framework combines fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and ROI analysis to evaluate financial viability of AI in healthcare, demonstrated with stroke detection case study showing break-even point at ~4k patients/year.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI healthcare evaluations focus on model accuracy rather than economic viability in real clinical settings, lacking systematic frameworks to assess whether AI adoption is financially worthwhile considering implementation costs and workflow integration.

Method: FLARE framework integrates fuzzy logic (for uncertainty), time-driven activity-based costing (for service delivery costs), and return on investment analysis. Demonstrated through case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in CT stroke pathway for acute ischemic stroke patients.

Result: Case study identified break-even threshold of ~3,992 patients/year, with positive first-year ROI at typical annual stroke volumes of ~5,000 patients. Economic benefit depends on algorithmic performance, patient volume, verification time, infrastructure choices, and workflow design.

Conclusion: FLARE provides transparent, practical decision-support for early-stage AI adoption evaluation in healthcare, helping clinicians, administrators, and policymakers determine economic viability and identify operational improvements by making uncertainty, resource use, and implementation trade-offs explicit.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is increasingly being introduced into healthcare workflows, yet most evaluations emphasize model accuracy rather than whether adoption is economically worthwhile in real clinical settings. This study proposes FLARE, a systematic and uncertainty-aware framework for evaluating the financial and operational implications of adopting AI in healthcare. FLARE combines fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and return on investment analysis to estimate the cost of clinical service delivery, the cost of AI development and operation, and the economic consequences of workflow integration under uncertainty. The framework was demonstrated through an early health technology assessment case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in the CT stroke pathway for acute ischemic stroke. The case study shows how FLARE can quantify conventional pathway cost, AI-related development and recurring costs, and AI-enabled service savings within a unified activity-based model. Under expected assumptions, the analysis identified a break-even threshold of approximately 3,992 patients per year, with positive first-year return on investment at typical annual stroke volumes of about 5,000 patients. The results further show that economic benefit depends not only on algorithmic performance, but also on patient volume, verification time, infrastructure choices, and workflow design. FLARE provides a transparent and practical decision-support framework for early-stage evaluation of AI adoption in healthcare. By making uncertainty, resource use, and implementation trade-offs explicit, it helps clinicians, administrators, and policymakers determine when AI deployment is economically viable and where operational changes may improve value.

</details>


### [13] [Rules Before Oracles: Auditable, User-Configurable Argument Selection for Deliberative Polling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23979)
*Muntaser Syed,Markus Zanker,Marius Silaghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A civic recommender system for deliberative polls that uses transparent rules over public evidence with voter-controlled parameters, showing such legible systems can perform nearly as well as opaque ones while giving voters control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When deliberative polls have many submissions, opaque ranking algorithms decide which arguments voters see, giving these algorithms undue influence without transparency or voter control. The paper investigates whether transparent, recomputable rules can work instead.

Method: Formalizes a poll model with bipolar justification sets, develops seven criteria for civic recommenders, proposes a one-hop reversed endorsement flow rule parameterized by a relation-weight function, and tests it through agentic simulation with about 17,000 seed-paired runs.

Result: The transparent rule performs only 0.035 short of an ideal label-reading ceiling, showing opaque systems have limited advantage. On coverage alone, it resembles random selection, but leads on other metrics (order and endorsement mass), dominating by factor 3.3 on mass. Weight functions serve as security controls, preserving 10% more completeness against flooding attacks.

Conclusion: Ranking mechanism choices represent trade-offs on a coverage-versus-mass frontier, not objective facts, and only legible rules can give affected persons meaningful choice. The approach maps to open-source peer-to-peer platforms.

Abstract: In a deliberative poll, once submissions outnumber what anyone will read, some mechanism chooses which arguments each voter sees, acquiring much of the decision; practice delegates it to opaque learned rankers, so a voter cannot recompute or contest the exposure that shaped their vote. We ask whether it can be a published rule over publicly recomputable evidence with parameters held by the voter, treating legibility as an admissibility condition on usable mechanisms, not an objective traded against accuracy. We formalise a poll over bipolar justification sets, judging a slate by reason coverage, the order it arrives in, and captured endorsement mass; we give seven checkable criteria for a civic recommender and a rule meeting them: a one-hop reversed endorsement flow parameterised by a relation-weight function. An agentic simulator records every slate at every vote, over about 17,000 seed-paired runs. Served slates fall 0.035 short of a label-reading ceiling upper-bounding every selection procedure, opaque ones included: any unconstrained ranker's advantage is bounded and small. On coverage alone, with non-degenerate authoring, the rule is indistinguishable from a random slate, a null due to an order-blind, charity-blind instrument; on the other two it leads at every prefix by a margin widening with adversarial pressure and dominates on mass by a factor of 3.3. Once a realistic fraction of submissions carries no reasons, the coverage margin returns and grows. Label-homogeneous flooding collapses completeness from 0.81 to 0.34 under a flat weight policy, only to 0.44 under author-count normalisation, making the weight function a security control worth 10% of completeness. The choice between ranking arms is a position on a coverage-versus-mass frontier, not a fact, the kind of choice only a legible rule can hand to the person it affects. It maps onto an open-source peer-to-peer platform.

</details>


### [14] [Ethical LLM-Assisted Research: A Framework for Responsible Delegation, Verification, and Epistemic Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23644)
*Kalin Stoyanov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for analyzing epistemic legitimacy in AI-assisted scientific research, emphasizing human verification and accountability over machine involvement levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become routine in scientific research (literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, formal reasoning), there's a need to establish conditions for maintaining epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship when delegating scientific reasoning to AI systems.

Method: Develops a normative/conceptual framework treating scientific reasoning as distributed process with constructs: content origin O(g), human verification V(g), responsibility assignment R(g), accountable human ownership M(g), and epistemic outcome E(g). Introduces "epistemic audit" as structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility.

Result: Proposes that ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than degree of machine involvement. Provides formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from transfer/neglect of epistemic responsibility.

Conclusion: The framework enables transparent and reviewable AI-assisted reasoning in scientific research by separating claim provenance from verification processes and maintaining human responsibility for knowledge claims, regardless of machine contribution levels.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are becoming routine instruments of scientific research, assisting with literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, and formal reasoning. Their use raises a central epistemic question: when parts of scientific reasoning are delegated to an artificial system, what conditions must remain under human control for the resulting knowledge claims to retain epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship? This paper develops a normative and conceptual framework for analyzing such delegation. Scientific reasoning is treated as a distributed process in which the origin of a contribution may vary between human and machine, while responsibility for its acceptance into the scientific record remains human. The framework distinguishes content origin $O(g)$, completion of human verification $V(g)$, responsibility assignment $R(g)$, accountable human ownership $M(g)$, and epistemic outcome $E(g)$. These constructs separate the provenance of a claim from the process by which it is checked, the epistemic outcome of that checking, and the human responsibility attached to its disposition. The central proposition is that the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined primarily by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than by the degree of machine involvement itself. On this basis, the paper develops the notion of an \emph{epistemic audit}: a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility intended to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable. The resulting framework provides a formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from the transfer or neglect of epistemic responsibility in scientific research.

</details>


### [15] [When "Must" Becomes "Maybe": Constraint Weakening in LLM Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24569)
*Yiheng Sun,Huifei Wang,Yancheng Zhu,Zhenyu Li,Zebin Zhao,Yifan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents frequently lose action-constraining operational state during multi-stage workflows when language artifacts transform requirements into mere information, weakening downstream constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model agents coordinate complex tasks through multi-role workflows where upstream state is transformed into language artifacts (summaries, plans, etc.), but current approaches fail to preserve the action-binding nature of operational state - transforming unresolved conditions from requirements into mere informational considerations.

Method: Studied operational state preservation using safety blockers as a controlled instance. Conditioned on correct upstream identification, varied handoff transformations (direct-handoff controls, compression, plan assimilation, etc.), and evaluated executors restricted to resulting artifacts across 1,296 synthetic episodes.

Result: Direct-handoff preserved all blockers (100%), but normal compression caused 100% deactivation and 54.2% forbidden actions. Restoring all four state fields raised preservation to 100% and eliminated forbidden actions. Downstream verification eliminated forbidden actions while artifact deactivation remained high (95.3%), showing preservation is separate from containment.

Conclusion: There's a state-transmission failure between information extraction and action - handoff transformations can retain semantic content while weakening operational constraints. Semantic availability doesn't guarantee operational preservation, requiring explicit mechanisms to maintain action-binding state across LLM workflow transitions.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents coordinate complex tasks through multi-role and multi-stage workflows. Upstream state is repeatedly transformed into intermediate language artifacts, such as summaries, plans, tickets, memories, and handoff notes, from which downstream components act. For action-constraining state, topical retention is insufficient: an artifact may mention an unresolved condition while changing it from a requirement that must be resolved before execution into information that may merely inform the next action. We study this action-binding role as operational state preservation. Safety blockers provide a controlled instance because each source state has an explicit prerequisite, authority, fallback, and execution consequence. We condition on correct upstream identification, vary the handoff transformation, and evaluate an executor restricted to the resulting artifact. Across 1,296 controlled synthetic episodes, direct-handoff controls preserve every blocker, whereas compression, plan assimilation, convergence, ownership deferral, and precedent substitution repeatedly turn binding state into caveats or non-binding considerations. Normal handoff compression produces 100.0% deactivation and 54.2% forbidden action. Restoring all four state fields raises preservation to 100.0% and reduces forbidden action to 0.0%. Fixed-artifact interventions further separate preservation from containment: downstream verification eliminates forbidden action while artifact deactivation remains 95.3%. These results identify a state-transmission failure between information extraction and action. Handoff transformations can retain state content while weakening its constraints on downstream action. Semantic availability does not guarantee operational preservation.

</details>


### [16] [MolEmb: Multimodal Large Language Models Can Be Strong Molecular Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23646)
*Xinjian Zhao,Xiangru Jian,Yaoyao Xu,Xiaozhuang Song,Wei Pang,Lei Bai,Tianshu Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MolEmb adapts multimodal LLMs for molecular embeddings conditioned on both molecular profiles and natural-language context, enabling competitive property prediction and cross-modal retrieval, showing MLLMs can serve as general molecular embedding models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most molecular encoders are specialist models producing unconditional vectors from single molecular views, lacking language interfaces for varying representations. The paper investigates whether multimodal LLMs can instead serve as general molecular embedding models that produce embeddings conditioned on both molecular profiles and semantic context.

Method: Introduces MolEmb, a lightweight framework that adapts MLLMs by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive objective. Also introduces MolCAR as a diagnostic benchmark for context-aware retrieval.

Result: The resulting embedding model is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule-text retrieval in the same space. Context-aware molecular embedding is found to be primarily a data property of the supervision.

Conclusion: MLLMs are not merely chemistry assistants or generators, but a viable and extensible route to general molecular embedding models that can produce embeddings conditioned on both molecular profiles and natural-language semantic context.

Abstract: Molecular embedding models can serve as foundational infrastructure for computational chemistry and drug discovery, where reusable vector representations support property prediction, virtual screening, and retrieval. Most molecular encoders are specialist models built around a single molecular view, producing unconditional vectors with no language interface for varying the representation. We ask whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs), which natively process images, text, and symbolic inputs, can instead serve as \emph{general molecular embedding models} that produce embeddings conditioned on both a molecular profile and a natural-language semantic context. We introduce \textbf{MolEmb}, a lightweight framework that adapts MLLMs by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive objective. The resulting embedding model is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule--text retrieval in the same space. We further introduce \textbf{MolCAR}, a diagnostic benchmark for context-aware retrieval, and find that context-aware molecular embedding is primarily a data property of the supervision. These results suggest that MLLMs are not merely chemistry assistants or generators, but a viable and extensible route to general molecular embedding models.

</details>


### [17] [Gated Activation Steering for Reducing Sycophancy & Hallucination in Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23666)
*Himanshu Tripathi,Subash Neupane,Shaswata Mitra,Sudip Mittal,Noorbakhsh Amiri Golilarz,Shahram Rahimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a gated Inference Time Intervention (ITI) framework that jointly controls hallucination and sycophancy in clinical LLMs by applying targeted steering only when needed, improving robustness under pressure without harming correct responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sycophancy and hallucination are critical failure modes in clinical question answering where models must stay grounded in context and resist user pressure. Existing approaches address these behaviors separately or apply interventions broadly across all turns, which can degrade already correct responses.

Method: The method uses Inference Time Intervention (ITI) to learn separate steering directions for hallucination and sycophancy from contrastive clinical pairs, applying them to causally verified attention heads. During runtime, behavior-specific gates determine when intervention is needed: hallucination component mitigates unsupported claims, sycophancy component prevents answer shifts under pressure.

Result: The framework was evaluated on clinical questions grounded in EHR data. Across 600 pressure trajectories for a 4B-parameter model, the unsteered model caved in 570 cases, while gated steering helped it last longer in 551 cases. The steered model achieved robustness comparable to models with over 100B parameters, demonstrating effectiveness without intervening at every turn.

Conclusion: Targeted inference-time steering can jointly mitigate both hallucination and sycophancy in clinical LLMs, improving robustness under user pressure while preserving correct responses, making it more effective than broad, always-on interventions.

Abstract: Sycophancy and hallucination are persistent failure modes of Large Language Models (LLMs) across domains. However, it becomes particularly consequential in clinical question answering, where responses must remain grounded in the provided context and robust to user pressure. Hallucination can introduce information that is unsupported by the context, while sycophancy can cause a model to abandon a previously correct answer when challenged by the user. Existing approaches, such as prompt-based safeguards and always-on activation steering, often address these behaviors separately or apply interventions broadly across turns, which can unnecessarily deteriorate responses that were already correct. To address these limitations within a single framework, we employ Inference Time Intervention (ITI) to jointly control both behaviors by learning separate steering directions for hallucination and sycophancy from contrastive clinical pairs and applying them to causally verified attention heads. During runtime, behavior-specific gates then determine when intervention is needed: the hallucination component mitigates unsupported claims, while the sycophancy component mitigates answer shifts caused by user pressure. We evaluate this framework on clinical questions grounded in EHR data while keeping the model weights frozen. Across all evaluation settings, we conducted 15,900 model-response runs. Across 600 pressure trajectories for the 4-billion-parameter model, the unsteered model caved in 570 cases. At the same time, gated steering helped it last longer in 551 of them. It held its ground under pressure at levels comparable to those of models with more than 100 billion parameters, showing that targeted inference-time steering can improve robustness without intervening at every turn.

</details>


### [18] [Automata from Agent Traces: Failure and Next-Step Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23670)
*Seonglae Cho,Franklin Cardenoso Fernandez,Umar Mohammed,Zekun Wu,Kleyton Da Costa,Ilham Wicaksono,Adriano Koshiyama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a method to convert unstructured LLM agent behavior traces into compact finite-state machines (FSMs) that capture the shared structure across runs, enabling better next-step prediction and failure prediction for safety auditing and monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks, but their behavioral traces are unstructured and opaque, making safety auditing and runtime monitoring difficult. Existing approaches work per-trace or only on successful runs, missing cross-run topological structure that links next-step and failure prediction.

Method: The authors collapse an entire trace corpus into a single, compact finite-state machine (FSM) that serves as a structural substrate for LLM agent behavior. The FSMs are built in milliseconds from agent execution traces.

Result: Across twelve public datasets, the FSMs are compact (7-43 states), achieve ≥0.997 fitness in replaying held-out data, and show near-identical topology across splits. For next-step prediction, FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory. For failure prediction, per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94, enabling early stopping well before completion.

Conclusion: Behavioral topology appears shaped more by the deployment harness than by the LLM, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring of LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks, but their behavioral structure remains opaque: long unstructured traces resist the safety auditing and runtime monitoring that deployment requires. Existing approaches operate per-trace or success-only, so they miss the cross-run topology that links next-step and failure prediction. To recover that shared structure, we collapse an entire trace corpus into a single, compact finite-state machine (FSM) that serves as a structural substrate for the otherwise unpredictable behavior of LLM agents. Across twelve public datasets, the FSMs are compact (7-43 states), replay held-out data at >=0.997 fitness with near-identical topology across splits, and build in milliseconds. This substrate addresses both prediction goals. For next-step prediction, FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory on every ground-truth-matched dataset. For failure prediction, per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94, and an online monitor ranks failing runs above passing ones from a partial trace, triggering early stopping well before completion. Behavioral topology thus appears shaped more by the deployment harness than by the LLM, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring.

</details>


### [19] [Do LLMs Understand Limit Order Book Dynamics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23706)
*Junxiao Chen,Paul Glasserman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM trained on synthetic limit order book data generates valid event sequences but fails to learn the true state dynamics, leading to biased predictions and spurious predictability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are being applied to financial market data like limit order books, but there's a need to test whether they truly learn the underlying stochastic dynamics or just generate superficially valid sequences.

Method: The paper develops novel tests to evaluate an LLM's world model, extending prior deterministic tests to handle the stochastic dynamics required for limit order books. The method examines the LLM's ability to learn the true state of the LOB rather than just generating valid event sequences.

Result: The LLM achieves near-perfect scores in generating valid sequences of LOB events, but its implicit world model fails to learn the actual state of the LOB. This deficiency causes biased estimates and creates spurious predictability when using the LLM for forecasting future LOB events.

Conclusion: Even when LLMs can generate valid-looking financial data sequences, they may not capture the true underlying dynamics, leading to problematic biases and false predictability in forecasting applications. The paper provides new testing methods to detect these deficiencies in stochastic financial settings.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) trained on synthetic limit order book (LOB) data achieves near perfect scores in generating valid sequences of LOB events. However, the LLM's implicit world model fails to learn the state of the LOB. This deficiency leads to biased estimates and spurious predictability in using the LLM to forecast future LOB events. Our analysis uses novel tests of an LLM's world model, extending prior work from deterministic settings to the stochastic dynamics needed for the LOB.

</details>


### [20] [AgentRoom: Concurrent Multi-Agent Coding in a CRDT-Backed Shared Workspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23740)
*Seonglae Cho,Donghyun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentRoom enables concurrent coding agents to collaborate using realtime CRDT protocols, reducing task abandonment and improving coordination compared to sequential or parallel approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-agent coding systems are limited by the serial token generation of LLMs, forcing agents to work sequentially or in uncoordinated parallel, leading to high task abandonment (up to half of hard tasks). There's a need for proper coordination mechanisms for concurrent coding agents.

Method: AgentRoom implements a realtime collaborative editing protocol using Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (CRDTs) for concurrent coding agents. It exposes file-level claim, status, and broadcast as Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem, enabling coordinated multi-agent coding.

Result: Using five frontier coding-CLI models on four backend coding tasks (including Python DevBench and Rust+axum), AgentRoom with 2 agents shows fewer abandoned tasks than solo approaches and less run-to-run variation. At matched compute, AgentRoom outperforms parallel-merge approaches, with coordination being the key factor rather than just parallelism or CRDT merging.

Conclusion: Coordination through realtime collaborative protocols like AgentRoom is essential for effective multi-agent coding systems, not just parallelism or data merging. The system successfully enables concurrent agents to work together more effectively than existing sequential or parallel approaches.

Abstract: Concurrent multi-agent coding promises division of labor across modules, robustness through redundancy, and parallel exploration at the natural granularity of multi-file projects. Realtime collaborative editing protocols solve this coordination problem for human teams via Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (CRDTs), but the LLMs underneath generate one token at a time and existing multi-agent coding systems inherit this serial limit: they either sequence agents through phase handoffs or pool independent samples without coordination, and a single agent abandons up to half of hard tasks with a one-file stub-and-exit. AgentRoom is a realtime collaborative editing protocol for concurrent coding agents. Its runtime layer exposes file-level claim, status, and broadcast as MCP tools on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem. Five frontier coding-CLI models ran four backend coding tasks, with cross-language checks in Python DevBench and Rust+axum. For CLI-stable models, AgentRoom with 2 agents abandons fewer tasks than Solo and has less run-to-run variation. At matched-compute, one positive mean LLM-judge contrast puts AgentRoom over parallel-merge. The other contrast, a bundle probe, puts full AgentRoom above each partial case: an ordering rather than a percentage split. Coordination, not parallelism or CRDT-merge, bears the load.

</details>


### [21] [Serving Masked Diffusion LLMs: Characterization and Design Principles from Real Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23807)
*Farhana Amin,Sabiha Afroz,Mona Moghadampanah,Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of diffusion LLM serving shows distinct characteristics from AR models: request difficulty is discrete and unpredictable, short benchmarks understate variance, most latency is CPU overhead, and batching amortizes this overhead with parallel denoising steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current serving systems for diffusion language models are built without understanding their real-world serving behavior, risking incorrect assumptions from AR serving. There's a need to characterize dLLM serving to properly build efficient infrastructure.

Method: Used LLaDA-8B-Instruct with D2F LoRA adapter on single H200 GPU, evaluated on GSM8K and HumanEval. Analyzed request difficulty, serving variance, latency breakdown, batching efficiency, and derived batch-timeout rules for Poisson arrivals.

Result: 1) Request difficulty is discrete with 11 fixed step-count levels, unpredictable before generation (best R²=0.15). 2) Short benchmarks understate variance. 3) 76% of latency is CPU overhead. 4) Batching improves throughput 16x at batch 16. 5) Quality doesn't degrade with batching (74-76% accuracy). 6) Developed batch-timeout rule for synchronized batching.

Conclusion: Diffusion LLM serving requires parallelism at each denoising step level, differing from AR serving in admission/eviction interactions with shared forward passes. Systems must account for discrete request difficulty and CPU overhead amortization.

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can in principle generate text faster than autoregressive (AR) models, since they denoise many tokens at once. Recent systems have begun building serving infrastructure for dLLMs, but none first measure how these models behave under real, concurrent serving load. Serving systems built without this grounding risk carrying over assumptions from AR serving that may not hold for dLLMs. We characterize dLLM serving to close this gap, using LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F (Discrete Diffusion Forcing) LoRA adapter on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, evaluated on GSM8K and HumanEval. We report three findings. First, request difficulty, the number of denoising steps a request needs, is discrete rather than continuous: requests fall into 11 fixed step-count levels (178 + 29k), and no signal we test predicts the level before generation starts (best R2 = 0.150). Second, benchmarks with short generation budgets below 320 tokens understate serving variance, since requests are cut off before the latency spread appears. Third, only 24% of single-request wall-clock time is GPU computation; the rest is CPU-side dispatch overhead. Batching mainly helps by amortizing this overhead: sharing one forward pass per denoising step improves throughput by 16.0x at batch size 16 over a per-request-dispatch baseline. We also argue structurally that output quality should not degrade with batch size, stating three assumptions this rests on; we measure 74 to 76% GSM8K accuracy at single-request scale. Finally, we derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals. Together, these results show that serving diffusion language models needs parallelism at the level of each denoising step, which differs from AR serving in how admission and eviction interact with an already shared forward pass.

</details>


### [22] [Generating Biomedical Fact-Checking Reports with RL-Enhanced Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23811)
*Jiongxiao Wang,Dingli Ma,Chaoqun Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioCheck Agent: An LLM-based agent using reinforcement learning (EG-GRPO) to generate structured biomedical fact-checking reports through PubMed searches, improving accuracy and reducing hallucinations compared to base models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated fact-checking methods in biomedical domain produce isolated prediction labels without sufficient explanation, lacking utility for human understanding and struggling with domain-specific challenges like scientific literature interpretation.

Method: Developed BioCheck Agent with agentic search on PubMed using Boolean operators, and introduced Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO) reinforcement learning with rewards for high-quality evidence retrieval and penalizing hallucinations.

Result: Improved label prediction accuracy on SciFact by 9.95% over base Qwen3.5-4B, achieved 3.7% higher evidence quality score, and reduced evidence hallucination rate by 19.63%.

Conclusion: BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO effectively generates structured biomedical fact-checking reports with improved accuracy and reduced hallucinations, addressing limitations of current retrieve-then-verify methods.

Abstract: Automated fact-checking is essential for ensuring the reliability of public health information, yet the biomedical domain poses unique challenges. Validating biomedical claims requires rigorous interpretation of scientific literature, assessment of retrieved evidence, and comprehensive justification toward the conclusion. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) enhanced by Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic search perform automated fact-checking in a retrieve-then-verify paradigm, current methods still output isolated prediction labels, lacking explanatory depth and offers limited utility for human understanding. To bridge this gap, we introduce an LLM-based agent named BioCheck Agent that generates structured biomedical fact-checking reports with agentic search. Rather than merely outputting supported or refuted labels, our agent synthesizes final conclusions with retrieved evidence and rigorous analysis. To ensure domain-specific accuracy, BioCheck Agent exclusively searches high-quality scientific literature in PubMed, utilizing advanced Boolean search operators. Recognizing that direct prompting often results in hallucinations and low-quality reports, especially for lightweight open-source models, we further propose the Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO) to perform reinforcement learning on BioCheck Agent with a task-specific reward that incentivizes advanced search behavior and high-quality evidence retrieval while penalizing hallucinations. Our experimental results show that compared to the base model Qwen3.5-4B, BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO improves label prediction accuracy on SciFact by 9.95%. Furthermore, it achieves a 3.7% higher evidence quality score and a 19.63% lower evidence hallucination rate, demonstrating its ability to generate biomedical fact-checking reports with improved accuracy and quality.

</details>


### [23] [A Formal Methodological Framework for Auditing Robustness and Fidelity in Explainable AI: From Application to Trust Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23817)
*Rosa Elysabeth Ralinirina,Jean Christian Ralaivao,Niaiko Michaël Ralaivao,Alain Josué Ratovondrahona,Thomas Mahatody*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SHAP and LIME explanations can be unreliable under input perturbations; authors propose an auditing protocol measuring robustness and fidelity via a Trust Score, finding even high-performance models produce unstable explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SHAP and LIME are standard tools for interpreting black-box predictions, but their outputs vary substantially with small input perturbations, raising concerns about whether such explanations can be trusted, especially in sensitive domains like food security.

Method: Constructed an auditing protocol that measures two properties of post-hoc explainers: robustness (stability under input perturbation) and fidelity (whether important features actually drive predictions). These are combined into a single Trust Score. Applied to multi-sectoral dataset from Madagascar (83 features, 253 records, 4 malnutrition classes) using three classifiers and two explainers plus regularized counterparts.

Result: Results show models with AUC above 0.99 can produce numerically degenerate or uninformative explanations. Fidelity scores lose discriminative power when models are overfitted. Explanation stability varies significantly under input perturbations.

Conclusion: Auditing XAI outputs is necessary, not optional, particularly when they inform decisions in sensitive domains. Even high-performance models can produce untrustworthy explanations, highlighting the need for systematic evaluation of explainer reliability.

Abstract: SHAP and LIME are now standard tools for interpreting black-box predictions, yet their outputs can vary substantially when the input is perturbed by small amounts of noise--a problem we observed firsthand in our previous work on food security in Madagascar (Ralinirina et al., 2025). This variability raises the question of whether such explanations can be trusted at all. We address it by constructing an auditing protocol that measures two properties of any post-hoc explainer: robustness (how stable the explanation is under input perturbation) and fidelity (whether the features deemed important actually drive the model's prediction). These two quantities are combined into a single Trust Score. We run the protocol on a multi-sectoral dataset from Madagascar (83 features, 253 records, 4 malnutrition classes) using three classifiers and two explainers, plus their regularized counterparts. The results are sobering: models with AUC above 0.99 can produce numerically degenerate or flatly uninformative explanations, and fidelity scores lose discriminative power when the model is overfitted. These findings suggest that auditing XAI outputs is not optional but necessary, particularly when they inform decisions in sensitive domains.

</details>


### [24] [Minima-KV: Retention-Preserving KV Cache Compression with Mixed-Format Paged Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23834)
*Sergii Kozyrev,Davyd Maiboroda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Minima-KV is a heterogeneous KV cache compression system using mixed FP8/TQ3 formats with online-softmax merging, achieving 3.5x compression over BF16 while maintaining near-equivalent quality on long-context tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV cache memory consumption is a major bottleneck for long-context LLM serving, requiring compression solutions that don't compromise quality or require eviction of live request pages.

Method: A retention-preserving hierarchy where recent/anchor pages stay in FP8 format while older pages move to packed TQ3 format, using format-specific kernels for partial attention computation and globally normalized online-softmax merge to enable heterogeneous decoding without a dense shadow cache.

Result: Achieved 3.50x compression relative to BF16, 1.75x relative to FP8, with 18.3 KiB attention KV per live token. Maintained near-equivalent quality on 16K RULER tasks (-0.8 to -0.4 percentage point deltas on LongBench v2) and 3.625x active-KV compression with 0.9821x throughput in direct decode scenarios.

Conclusion: Minima-KV establishes a practical mixed-format approach for compressing long-context KV cache state without evicting live request pages, effectively balancing compression ratios with retention of quality and performance.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache is a primary capacity and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context LLM serving. We present Minima-KV, a retention-preserving hierarchy for mixed-format paged attention. Recent and protected Anchor pages remain in FP8, while older non-anchor pages move to packed TQ3; every live-request page remains addressable. Format-specific kernels compute partial attention states and combine them through a globally normalized online-softmax merge, enabling direct heterogeneous decode without a cache-sized dense shadow. Across separate, configuration-bound Qwen3.6-27B profiles on a single 96-GB NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, deployment accounting reports 18.3 KiB of attention KV per live token, corresponding to 3.50x compression relative to BF16 and 1.75x relative to FP8. A materializing quality profile matches its dense control on 16K RULER needle-in-a-haystack tasks. On the same 503-question LongBench v2 set, measured deltas are -0.80, -0.60, and -0.40 percentage points at 16K, 32K, and 64K. A separate single-pair direct-decode canary with two 59,008-token requests measures 3.625x active-KV compression and 0.9821x throughput relative to its control, routes all 16 full-attention layers without fallback, and retains no dense shadow. These results establish a practical mixed-format path for compressing long-context state without evicting live-request KV pages.

</details>


### [25] [SyPS: Measuring Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23837)
*Lijia Huang,Yao Fu,Sihao Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SyPS (Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity), a framework to measure how LLMs' sycophantic behavior changes across different prompt variants with varying social cues, revealing that sycophancy is socially structured and not fixed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations measure sycophancy under fixed prompts, leaving unclear whether LLMs' sycophantic behavior is stable when the same situation is presented with different sycophancy-relevant prompt variants (differences in user confidence, emotional framing, social consensus, or validation-seeking language).

Method: Developed SyPS framework that constructs controlled prompt variants preserving the same underlying user situation while varying sycophancy-relevant social cues. Introduced SPSS (Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score) as an instance-level measure of sycophancy variation across paired prompt variants, separating baseline sycophancy from prompt-induced shifts.

Result: Found that sycophancy prompt sensitivity is socially structured: validation-seeking and emotional-pressure cues often increase sycophancy, while counter-framing and anti-sycophancy prompts tend to reduce it. The framework enables model-level comparisons of robustness to sycophancy-relevant social cues.
