The Adversarial Signal Preservation Score provides insights into why certain system designs are more vulnerable than others, showing how adversarial signals survive through different communication topologies.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that LLM-based multi-agent trading systems face significant security vulnerabilities through their communication channels. Since no architecture proves inherently robust, the research provides crucial insights for designing safer and more robust agentic trading systems in the future, emphasizing the need for security considerations in system design.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, in which specialized agents collaborate through structured communication to produce trading decisions, are moving rapidly from research prototypes to live deployments that control real assets. The same inter-agent communication that makes them effective also exposes them: a corrupted signal can propagate to the final decision and translate into realized financial loss. Unlike prior attacks that presume privileged access to system internals, we restrict the adversary to what is practically reachable---the source data and prompts agents consume---yielding a low-barrier, and thus democratized threat model instantiated as role-specific adversaries.
  We present the first systematic empirical study in the financial domain to characterize how an adversarial signal enters a multi-agent trading system and how far it survives toward the decision. Along the role axis, we decompose a widely-used trading pipeline into four functional roles---Analyst, Researcher, Trader, and Risk Manager---and pair each with an attack matched to its interface. Along the structural axis, we evaluate four communication topologies under data- and agent-level attacks, using the Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS) as a post-hoc lens on why some designs are more robust than others. We conduct experiments across five assets, two backbones, and two target directions. A central finding is that no architecture is inherently robust. These findings provide insights for the future design of safer and more robust agentic trading systems.

</details>


### [51] [Compression Trinity: Exploring Sparsity, Quantization, and Low-Rank Approximations for LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24070)
*Mohammad Mozaffari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified framework called "Compression Trinity" jointly applies sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations to enable efficient LLM deployment with multiple methods for pretraining acceleration and post-training compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prohibitive computational and environmental costs of scaling Large Language Models, and limitations of existing compression techniques applied in isolation hitting accuracy-efficiency walls.

Method: The Compression Trinity framework combines three pillars: sparsity (to reduce computation), quantization (to minimize memory bandwidth), and low-rank approximations (to recover accuracy). Several methods implement this framework: MKOR for pretraining acceleration via block-diagonal sparsity and low-rank inversion, SLoPe for training acceleration via double-pruned backward passes and lazy adapters, OPTIMA for post-training compression via optimal weight reconstruction, PATCH for dynamic hybrid sparsity learning, and SLiM for full joint compression.

Result: MKOR reduces curvature update complexity from O(d³) to O(d²) and accelerates convergence by up to 1.85x over KFAC. SLoPe accelerates training by up to 1.25x. OPTIMA improves zero-shot accuracy by up to 3.97%. PATCH yields up to 1.38x speedups. SLiM improves accuracy by up to 5.66% over SOTA methods and outperforms uncompressed dense models at equal parameter budgets by 0.6%.

Conclusion: Jointly applying the Compression Trinity (sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations) is essential for achieving efficient, scalable, high-performance LLMs, demonstrating that unified compression approaches can break through the limitations of isolated techniques.

Abstract: Prohibitive computational and environmental costs impede the scalable deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs). Traditional compression techniques (sparsity, quantization, low-rank approximations) are typically applied in isolation, and each hits an accuracy-efficiency wall. This thesis proposes the "Compression Trinity," a unified framework that applies the three pillars jointly: sparsity to reduce computation, quantization to minimize memory bandwidth, and low-rank approximations to recover accuracy. To accelerate pretraining, we apply the Trinity to the optimizer and model architecture. MKOR approximates curvature via block-diagonal sparsity and low-rank inversion, maintaining numerical stability for quantized states; it reduces curvature update complexity from $O(d^3)$ to $O(d^2)$ and accelerates convergence by up to 1.85x over KFAC. SLoPe accelerates training by up to 1.25x via a double-pruned backward pass for N:M sparsity, using low-rank "lazy" adapters in the final 1% of training to recover accuracy. For post-training compression, OPTIMA stabilizes static masks in a zero-training regime by formulating weight reconstruction as globally optimal column-wise quadratic programs, improving zero-shot accuracy by up to 3.97%. Given a fine-tuning budget, PATCH breaks the ceiling of static masks by learning a dynamic hybrid sparsity ratio between 0% and 50%, yielding up to 1.38x speedups. Finally, SLiM realizes the full Compression Trinity in one shot, using mathematically derived low-rank adapters to recover information lost to quantization and sparsity, improving accuracy by up to 5.66% over state-of-the-art methods and outperforming uncompressed dense models at equal parameter budgets by 0.6%. Together, these results show that jointly applying the Compression Trinity is essential for efficient, scalable, high-performance LLMs.

</details>


### [52] [AgentWorld: Personality-Aware Reliability Evaluation for Agentic Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24076)
*Gunja Agarwal,Arup Kumar Das,Arun Menon,Jitesh Chandra Mishra,Vignesh Divakaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentWorld is a simulation framework for evaluating AI agents with realistic personality diversity and adversarial testing, featuring personality-driven user populations, structured evaluation metrics, and sophisticated risk analysis tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic information retrieval evaluation is limited to scripted interactions with uniform users, missing natural personality diversity and failing to expose adversarial brittleness in AI systems.

Method: The framework combines: 1) Big Five (OCEAN) personality-driven user populations with stateful tool-use environments; 2) pass^k consistency metric with structured fault classification and dual-control verification; 3) score-thresholded training-data export in six fine-tuning formats; and 4) an adversarial Risk Analyser with Monte-Carlo rollouts, evidence fusion, and Shapley attribution.

Result: Three experiments show: conversational analytics across 10 OCEAN personas (240 judgments); customer-support across 5 tasks × 4 personas; adversarial stress-testing revealing pre-existing brittleness (V_min=0.375 without perturbation) and tool/infrastructure attacks dominating (46% system, 38% action via Shapley attribution). Personality variation exposes failures uniform testing cannot detect.

Conclusion: AgentWorld demonstrates that incorporating personality diversity and adversarial analysis in evaluation reveals critical failure modes and trajectory-level brittleness that traditional uniform testing methodologies miss, enabling more robust AI system development.

Abstract: Evaluation of agentic information retrieval remains limited to scripted interactions with uniform users, missing both natural personality diversity and adversarial brittleness. We present AgentWorld, a simulation framework combining (i)Big Five (OCEAN) personality-driven user populations with stateful tool-use environments; (ii)the pass$^k$ consistency metric with structured fault classification, partial-credit scoring, and dual-control handoff verification; (iii)score-thresholded training-data export in six fine-tuning formats; and (iv)an adversarial Risk Analyser that snapshots required-intermediate-state spines, branches Monte-Carlo rollouts under four task-aware perturbation types, and quantifies risk via $ΔP / ΔT$ scoring, Dempster--Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley attack-category attribution. Three experiments demonstrate the framework: a conversational analytics agent across 10 OCEAN personas (240 evaluator judgments); a customer-support agent across 5 tasks $\times$ 4 persona variants; and adversarial stress-testing of 5 tasks revealing pre-existing trajectory brittleness ($V_{\min}=0.375$ without perturbation) and tool/infrastructure-layer attack dominance (Shapley: 46% system, 38% action). Personality variation surfaces failure modes uniform testing cannot expose---cross-domain leakage, contextual drift, a 0.27-point quality gap, and 50% vs. 100% pass-rate across personas on the same task---while the Risk Analyser quantifies trajectory-level brittleness that pass$^k$ alone cannot measure.

</details>


### [53] [EMRB: A Multi-Level Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Reasoning over Raw Electromagnetic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24086)
*Mingxu Zhang,Ying Sun,Yuhan Li,Yang Ji,Dazhong Shen,Ke Zhang,Shan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EMRB benchmark evaluates LLMs' ability to analyze raw physical-layer I/Q data through code generation, with ReconPilot method improving performance across tested models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as code agents for scientific analysis, but their ability to analyze raw physical-layer measurements remains untested. Current benchmarks focus on preprocessed features rather than raw data analysis.

Method: Introduces EMRB benchmark with 200 problems across 5 difficulty levels and 27 question types from 11 signal types with ground truth. Also proposes ReconPilot, a structured method that separates signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification.

Result: Evaluation of 14 LLMs shows scores ranging from 24.1% to 78.9%, with mean dropping from 84.9% on basic measurement to 21.2% on system design. ReconPilot raises overall scores by 3.8 to 17.6 points and improves 13 of 15 backbone-level combinations.

Conclusion: LLMs have limited but measurable ability to analyze raw physical-layer data through code generation, with significant room for improvement in complex system design tasks. Structured methods like ReconPilot can substantially enhance performance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as code agents for scientific and engineering analysis, but their ability to analyze raw physical-layer measurements remains untested. We introduce \textbf{EMRB} (\textbf{E}lectro\textbf{m}agnetic \textbf{R}easoning \textbf{B}enchmark), which evaluates whether LLMs can analyze raw I/Q data by writing and running code. EMRB contains 200 problems across five difficulty levels and 27 question types, from signal detection to OFDM design, generated from 11 signal types with verified ground truth. Unlike benchmarks built on preprocessed features or structured tables, EMRB provides only the raw capture; the quantities each question refers to must first be discovered through code. We evaluate 14 LLMs spanning proprietary, open-weight, and reasoning-oriented families. Scores range from 24.1\% to 78.9\%, with the mean dropping from 84.9\% on basic measurement to 21.2\% on system design. We also propose \textbf{ReconPilot}, a structured method that separates signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification. Across three backbones, ReconPilot raises the overall score by 3.8 to 17.6 points and improves 13 of 15 backbone-level combinations tested. All data and code are publicly released in \href{https://github.com/mingxuZhang2/EMRB}{\textcolor{blue}{our GitHub repository}}.

</details>


### [54] [Are Android GUI Agents Robust Against Runtime Anomalies? AnTrap: Evaluating Agents in Dynamic Adversarial Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24099)
*Guo Gan,Yilun Zhao,Cong Chen,Jinbiao Wei,Tingyu Song,Zheyuan Yang,Lin Fu,Hong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnTrap benchmark systematically evaluates GUI agent robustness against dynamic runtime anomalies on Android, revealing universal vulnerabilities in leading models that persist even after adversarial training for certain complex anomaly types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GUI agent benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of agent robustness against runtime anomalies (unexpected pop-ups, action misuse, etc.) that commonly occur during real-world deployment on Android devices, creating a gap in assessing practical reliability.

Method: Introduced AnTrap benchmark with a four-layer taxonomy (State, Thinking, Action, Round) organizing 10 subcategories of real-world anomalies. Developed a construction pipeline that injects dynamic perturbations into agent execution trajectories while preserving task solvability. Evaluated 16 leading GUI models and conducted GRPO training in both original and adversarial environments.

Result: All 16 GUI models showed universal vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with significant performance degradation even for strongest models. Single-step traps at state and action layers were addressable through adversarial reinforcement learning, but deep contextual traps (like state deadlock) exposed intrinsic limitations that could not be resolved by training in environments with traps alone.

Conclusion: Dynamic anomalies present critical robustness challenges for GUI agents, requiring different mitigation strategies based on anomaly type - some are learnable through environment exposure while others require fundamental advances in reasoning capabilities to overcome intrinsic limitations.

Abstract: GUI agents often encounter dynamic anomalies when deployed on Android devices, from unexpected pop-ups to action misuse, yet existing benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of agent robustness against runtime anomalies. We introduce AnTrap, a comprehensive benchmark that injects dynamic perturbations into agent execution trajectories. We propose a taxonomy organizing real-world anomalies into four layers (State, Thinking, Action and Round) with ten fine-grained subcategories, and develop a construction pipeline that preserves task solvability while introducing realistic adversarial conditions. Evaluating 16 leading GUI models, we reveal universal vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with even the strongest models suffering significant performance degradation. Furthermore, we conduct GRPO training in both original and adversarial environments to validate our benchmark, separating environment-learnable anomalies from reasoning-bottlenecked ones. Our findings show that while single-step traps at state and action layers are largely addressable through adversarial reinforcement learning, deep contextual traps, like state deadlock, expose intrinsic limitations that cannot be resolved by training in environments with traps alone.

</details>


### [55] [ACE: A Self-Correcting Agentic Canvas Editor for Multi-Slide Presentation Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24103)
*JooYoung Jang,Taegyeong Lee,Jihyeon Park,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE is a hierarchical scene-graph editor with self-correction that outperforms HTML-based LLM agents on document editing tasks with better instruction following, speed, and cost efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial design platforms face two problems: legacy document formats expose only flat, absolutely positioned elements causing layout issues, and design lacks unique ground truth making diff-based metrics penalize valid-but-different outputs.

Method: Developed ACE (agentic canvas editor) with hierarchical scene-graph and presentation-specialized action space (98 tools), paired with CARE (content-aware router) to reduce input tokens, and a self-correction loop driven by a ground-truth-free instruction-following judge.

Result: ACE with self-correction achieves higher instruction-following scores (4.23 vs 3.81) than HTML-based agents, with 1.75x speed, ~44% lower cost, and 58.7% win-rate in blind preference tests. 66% of cases halt after one pass with strict-peak rollback preventing regressions.

Conclusion: The hierarchical scene-graph approach with self-correction via ground-truth-free judges enables reliable LLM-based document editing that overcomes limitations of flat document formats while avoiding penalties for valid design variations.

Abstract: Commercial design platforms increasingly edit documents through large language model (LLM) agents, but two practical problems block reliable deployment: legacy document formats expose only \emph{flat}, absolutely positioned elements, so agents must recompute coordinates and routinely break layouts; and design has no unique ground truth, so diff-against-reference metrics penalize valid-but-different outputs. We present \textbf{ACE}, an agentic canvas editor over a \emph{hierarchical scene-graph} with a presentation-specialized action space (98 tools), paired with \textbf{CARE}, a content-aware router that feeds the agent only the relevant slice of each deck (avg.\ $\sim$89\% input-token reduction), and a \emph{self-correction} loop driven by a \emph{ground-truth-free} instruction-following (IF) judge whose natural-language critique is fed back as the next-turn instruction. With a fixed backbone, a scene-graph editor in a \emph{single turn} already matches a same-backbone \emph{agentic} HTML pipeline that iterates internally; adding self-correction lifts ACE significantly above it on instruction following (IF 4.23 vs.\ 3.81 on the full 94-task benchmark, paired $p{=}.010$, replicated by an out-of-loop judge) at 1.75$\times$ the speed and $\sim$44\% lower cost. VQ means are statistically indistinguishable, but 26 blind raters prefer ACE overall (58.7\% decisive win-rate) and prefer the self-corrected output 81\% of the time; the ranking is invariant across three judge families, and out-of-loop judges retain two-thirds of the self-correction gain, bounding circularity. 66\% of cases halt after one pass, and a strict-peak rollback removes every observed regression.

</details>


### [56] [Scalable Question-Centric Text-to-Image Evaluation: Reliable Ranking, Fine-Grained Diagnosis, and Cost-Aware Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24112)
*Shaoan Zhao,Fang Zhao,Xueqiang Guo,Xinpei Su,Huanlin Gao,Qiang Hui,Ting Lu,Fuyuan Shi,Chao Tan,Bikun Yang,Kai Wang,Shiguo Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QC-T2I-Bench is a question-centric framework that converts text-to-image prompts into attributed atomic questions with dependency graphs, enabling fine-grained evaluation and diagnosis of T2I models beyond simple aggregate scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current T2I evaluation methods have limitations: similar total scores mask different model strengths, fine-grained benchmarks weaken attribution and ignore complexity, and requirements are either scored separately or aggregated, obscuring basic vs. compositional failures.

Method: Developed QC-T2I-Bench framework that: 1) Converts open prompts into attributed atomic questions, 2) Organizes question dependencies using Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs), 3) Uses hierarchy-constrained aggregation that excludes downstream questions after prerequisite failures, 4) Leverages DSG structure to measure joint success within prompts and compare repeated entities across prompts.

Result: Evaluation of multiple open-source T2I models shows: joint completion drops from 80.7% for components with two capabilities to 37.2% for those with seven or more. The framework enables reliable model ranking and fine-grained diagnosis. Also enables training-free routing with cost-aware router achieving ERNIE's 89.51-point estimate with 21.3% less GPU-s/MP.

Conclusion: QC-T2I-Bench provides a more nuanced evaluation framework that separates basic realization failures from compositional failures, supports better model selection, and enables efficient routing through reusing evaluation records for practical deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Modern text-to-image (T2I) models often have similar total scores but different strengths, making practical selection difficult. Fine-grained benchmarks decompose prompts into questions, yet often return them to prompt scores and fixed categories, weakening attribution and ignoring complexity. Related requirements are also scored separately or as one total, obscuring basic versus compositional failure. We present QC-T2I-Bench, a question-centric framework that converts open prompts into attributed atomic questions and organizes their dependencies with Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs). We use hierarchy-constrained question aggregation to exclude downstream questions after a prerequisite fails and to prevent simple and complex prompts from receiving the same total weight. We then use the DSG structure to measure joint success within prompts and compare repeated entities across prompts, separating basic realization failures from failures under additional requirements. We evaluate multiple open-source T2I models on English and Chinese prompts. The resulting question-level evidence supports reliable ranking and fine-grained diagnosis: joint completion falls from 80.7\% for components with two capabilities to 37.2\% for those with seven or more. Finally, we reuse the same records for training-free routing; our cost-aware router matches ERNIE's 89.51-point estimate with 21.3\% less GPU-s/MP.

</details>


### [57] [AHEAD: Adaptive Hindsight with Environment-Augmented Distillation for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24114)
*Xiaolong Jin,Dingmin Wang,Vijay Lingam,Varun Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AHEAD is a step-aware RL framework that provides asymmetric supervision: environment feedback for all steps plus LLM-generated corrective hints only for error steps, improving over trajectory-level rewards and uniform privileged information approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL methods for multi-turn LLM agents use trajectory-level rewards that assign uniform advantage to every step, failing to identify which decisions caused success/failure. While self-distillation methods provide finer-grained supervision, they apply privileged information uniformly to all steps, ignoring that routine steps need little guidance while critical error steps require corrective direction that environment feedback alone cannot provide.

Method: AHEAD matches different supervision sources to different step types: 1) Teacher receives environment feedback on all steps as grounded dense signal, and 2) Teacher additionally receives LLM-generated corrective hints specifically on error steps to supply direction lacking in environment feedback. The method introduces minimal changes to standard GRPO algorithm.

Result: Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA benchmarks, and across three model scales, AHEAD significantly improves task success (+13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B over GRPO), reaches given success rates in fewer training steps, and solves tasks within tighter interaction budgets than outcome-only RL and prior self-distillation baselines.

Conclusion: The step-aware asymmetric supervision approach of AHEAD effectively addresses limitations of uniform trajectory rewards and undifferentiated privileged information, demonstrating superior performance and efficiency in training multi-turn LLM agents across diverse environments and model scales.

Abstract: Training multi-turn LLM agents with reinforcement learning typically relies on trajectory-level rewards, which assign a uniform advantage to every step and cannot identify which decisions led to success or failure. Self-distillation methods can provide finer-grained supervision by augmenting RL with privileged information. However, existing approaches usually apply the same type of privileged information to every step in an indistinguishable manner, ignoring a key asymmetry: routine steps need little additional guidance, while critical error steps require corrective direction that environment feedback alone cannot provide. We propose AHEAD, a step-aware framework that matches different supervision sources to different step types. The teacher receives environment feedback on all steps as a grounded dense signal, and additionally receives LLM-generated corrective hints on error steps to supply the direction that environment feedback lacks. The method introduces minimal changes to the standard GRPO algorithm. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA, and across three model scales, AHEAD raises task success (+13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B over GRPO), reaches a given success rate in fewer training steps, and solves tasks within tighter interaction budgets than outcome-only RL and prior self-distillation baselines.

</details>


### [58] [Robust Code RL via Faulty-Code-Driven Test case Synthesis and Dense Reward Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24135)
*Yiwen Zhang,Xiaodong Yan,Zhenyu Huang,Deng Zhao,Liang Jiang,Qing Cui,Zujie Wen,Zhiqiang Zhang,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RobustTests framework improves RLVR for code generation by synthesizing better test cases from faulty codes and using validator agents to filter invalid tests, achieving 3% performance gain on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RLVR for code generation suffers from limitations due to insufficient test case coverage, which causes false positives and leads to reward hacking and policy degradation. The quality of automatically generated test cases is suboptimal and creates reward bias.

Method: Proposes RobustTests framework with: 1) Faulty-code-driven test case synthesis using "near correct" faulty codes to capture logical discrepancies, 2) Validator agents with behavioral feature clustering to filter invalid and redundant test cases, 3) Stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates to address false negatives from hallucination noise, and 4) Construction of high-quality dataset augmenting CodeContests test cases.

Result: The framework creates a higher quality dataset covering broader faulty code scenarios. When used for RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B on a moderately challenging subset of CodeContests problems, it achieves an absolute 3% performance gain on LiveCodeBench benchmark compared to baseline methods.

Conclusion: RobustTests effectively addresses test quality issues in RLVR for code generation, demonstrating significant performance improvements and confirming the framework's utility in advancing LLM code generation proficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a pivotal technique for enhancing the code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the efficacy of RLVR in coding implementations is fundamentally limited by the comprehensiveness of test cases, because insufficient test coverage in code validation often causes false positives, further leading to reward hacking and policy degradation. To mitigate the reward bias stemming from the suboptimal quality of current automated generation methods, we propose the RobustTests framework, which introduces a faulty-code-driven test case synthesis strategy that leverages "near correct" faulty codes to guide the model in precisely capturing latent logical discrepancies and further integrates validator agents with behavioral feature clustering to facilitate the granular filtering of invalid and redundant test cases. To address false negatives caused by inherent hallucination noise in synthetic test cases, RobustTests also incorporates a stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates, bolstering training robustness through fine-grained feedback. By employing this pipeline, we construct a high-quality dataset that augmented the test cases in CodeContests, encompassing a broader spectrum of faulty code scenarios and significantly enhances diagnostic utility. Experimental results demonstrate that, by leveraging a moderately challenging subset of problems from CodeContests for training, RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B via RobustTests achieves an absolute 3% performance gain on the LiveCodeBench benchmark compared to baseline methods, confirming the effectiveness of the RobustTests framework in advancing the code generation proficiency of LLMs.

</details>


### [59] [OmniJudge or OmniBias? Diagnosing Multimodal Judges through Balanced, Decoupled Lenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24160)
*Guangzheng Hu,Ziyue Jiang,Weixu Qiao,Lixin Zhang,Jianye Kang,Yuru Wu,Rong Bao,Niantong Li,Wei Wang,Ziyi Cheng,Xinfa Zhu,HangRui Hu,Ting He,Bing Zhao,Lin Qu,Hu Wei,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: D3-Omni is a balanced benchmark for diagnosing multimodal understanding models, covering text-to-image, text-to-video, and text-to-speech tasks with 53 orthogonal dimensions and 10,671 samples to reveal systematic blind spots in evaluation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal understanding models used as "OmniJudges" for evaluation have unclear reliability because current benchmarks overemphasize positive examples and conflate distinct failure modes, hiding capability gaps and making it unclear whether judges recognize failures.

Method: Create D3-Omni benchmark by fixing verified fully positive seeds and deriving negatives through controlled prompt rewriting and atomic, dimension-isolating perturbations. The design is Dual-balanced (alleviating negative-sample scarcity and per-dimension label imbalance), Decoupled (each error attributable to a single capability), and Dynamic (steering construction toward under-represented regions as models improve).

Result: Even strong OmniJudges struggle on modality-related dimensions, confirm satisfied requirements more reliably than detecting violated ones, and treat nominally distinct attributes as largely a single decision, revealing that aggregate accuracy hides systematic blind spots.

Conclusion: A balanced and decoupled benchmark like D3-Omni can help expose systematic blind spots in multimodal understanding models that would otherwise remain hidden by aggregate accuracy metrics, enabling better diagnosis and addressing of capability gaps.

Abstract: Multimodal understanding models that can jointly judge text-to-image (T2I), text-to-video (T2V) and text-to-speech (TTS) generation are increasingly used as "OmniJudges" for evaluation and automatic annotation. How reliably they understand what they score remains unclear, since existing benchmarks and training data tend to overemphasize positive examples and to conflate distinct failure modes, so a judge may score well without recognizing failures while its capability gaps stay hidden. Motivated by this, we introduce D3-Omni, a balanced and decoupled benchmark for diagnosing fine-grained multimodal understanding, covering 53 orthogonal binary dimensions (17/22/14) and 10,671 samples (3,526/1,998/5,147) across the three tasks. Rather than re-generating outputs, which may leak information across dimensions, we fix verified fully positive seeds and derive negatives through controlled prompt rewriting and atomic, dimension-isolating perturbations. The resulting D3 design is Dual-balanced, which helps alleviate negative-sample scarcity and per-dimension label imbalance; Decoupled, so that each error is attributable to a single capability; and Dynamic, steering construction toward under-represented regions of the label distribution as generative models improve.The suite reaches near 1:1 per-dimension parity and a uniform distribution over all total-score levels. Under this balanced view, even strong OmniJudges tend to struggle on modality-related dimensions, to confirm satisfied requirements far more reliably than they detect violated ones, and to treat nominally distinct attributes as largely a single decision, suggesting that aggregate accuracy may hide systematic blind spots that a balanced and decoupled lens can help expose and, in turn, address.

</details>


### [60] [Task-Adaptive Rubrics for GUI Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24174)
*Tao Xiong,Xavier Hu,Wenkai Wang,Qinzhuo Wu,Changqiao Wu,Pengzhi Gao,Wei Liu,Jian Luan,Shengyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaptRubric: A Coarse-to-Fine Rubrics Framework for GUI agents that constructs task-adaptive judging criteria through category-level retrieval and instance-level generation to improve outcome reward modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI reward verifiers often under-specify how success criteria should be constructed for each task instance, using generic rubrics or implicit model reasoning. Their judging criteria are not sufficiently task-adaptive, potentially transferring checks across tasks, overlooking concrete constraints, or enforcing unstated requirements.

Method: Proposes AdaptRubric with two stages: 1) Category-level coarse rubric retrieval by routing instructions to GUI task families and retrieving reusable task-family criteria, and 2) Instance-level fine rubric generation to surface compact cues for concrete values, scopes, and constraints in the current instruction.

Result: Outperforms prior reward agents across offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning optimization: improves F1 by 3.6 points over baseline average under matched image budget, and yields a 4.23-point task-success gain.

Conclusion: AdaptRubric addresses the limitation of under-specified judging criteria in existing GUI reward verifiers by providing a task-adaptive framework that constructs appropriate success criteria through coarse-to-fine rubric construction.

Abstract: Recent studies on GUI agents have increasingly focused on outcome reward modeling, which assigns outcome rewards by judging whether an executed trajectory satisfies the success criteria implied by the user instruction. Existing GUI reward verifiers, however, often under-specify how these criteria should be constructed for each task instance. Whether using generic rubric structures or implicit model reasoning, their judging criteria are not sufficiently task-adaptive: they can transfer checks across tasks, overlook concrete constraints in the current instruction, or become overly strict by enforcing unstated requirements. To address this limitation, we propose AdaptRubric, a Coarse-to-Fine Rubrics Framework that constructs task-adaptive judging criteria through a category-level coarse stage and an instance-level fine stage. AdaptRubric performs category-level coarse rubric retrieval by routing the instruction to a GUI task family and retrieving reusable task-family criteria, then conducts instance-level fine rubric generation to surface compact cues for concrete values, scopes, and constraints in the current instruction. Across offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning optimization, AdaptRubric consistently outperforms prior reward agents, improving F1 by 3.6 points over the baseline average under a matched image budget and yielding a 4.23-point task-success gain.

</details>


### [61] [Paritok-4B: Intent-Conditioned Context Compression for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24188)
*Jiayu Shi,Luzhuo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paritok-4B is a 4B parameter LoRA compressor specifically designed for coding-agent trajectories that extracts spans rather than rewriting them, achieving 4x compression (25.7% of original size) while maintaining 86.5% of uncompressed solve quality, with no per-token fees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current coding agents waste tokens by re-sending large file reads and tool outputs to LLMs every turn, and existing general-purpose prompt compressors are poorly suited for code—they paraphrase identifiers and drop exact spans needed for editing.

Method: Built on Qwen3-4B with LoRA adaptation, using two key principles: 1) extractive approach (selecting spans rather than rewriting, with 96% of identifiers/paths/numbers preserved from input), and 2) intent-conditioned compression (focusing on current task, selecting lines to survive rather than adjusting retention amount). Trained by distilling a GPT-4.1-mini teacher over 67,074 real OpenHands trajectories into 40,606 validated examples.

Result: Achieves 25.7% compression ratio on SWE-bench Lite (4x compression), 2x better than GPT-4.1-mini (50.2%) and 2.4x better than GPT-5 (61.9%). Maintains 86.5% of uncompressed solve quality. With cat -n line-numbered input, achieves 27.8% compression with 89.3% quality retention. Economic advantage: 264 MB adapter with no per-token fee vs. GPT-5 compressor being net-negative.

Conclusion: Paritok-4B provides an efficient, cost-effective solution for compressing coding-agent contexts, achieving significant compression (roughly quarter of original size) without significantly reducing solve rates, making it economically viable for self-hosting on consumer-grade GPUs.

Abstract: Coding agents re-send large file reads and tool outputs to a frontier LLM every turn, and this context dominates their token bill. General-purpose prompt compressors are trained on prose and suit code poorly: they paraphrase identifiers and drop the exact spans an agent needs to edit. We present Paritok-4B, a 4B LoRA compressor for coding-agent trajectories built on two commitments. It is extractive: it selects spans rather than rewriting them, and 96.0% of the identifiers, paths, and numbers it emits already appear in its input, holding at 96.2% on held-out SWE-bench Lite output. It is intent-conditioned: told the agent's current task, it acts chiefly inside a retained segment, selecting which lines survive (retained lines are +0.067 more intent-relevant than removed ones, paired 95% CI [+0.056, +0.078]) rather than changing how much is retained. We distil a gpt-4.1-mini teacher over 67,074 real OpenHands trajectories into 40,606 validated examples and fine-tune Qwen3-4B. On all 300 SWE-bench Lite instances, Paritok-4B compresses agent context to 25.7% of its size, 2.0x harder than a gpt-4.1-mini compressor (50.2%) and 2.4x harder than gpt-5 (61.9%), while retaining 86.5% of uncompressed single-shot solve quality. Fed the cat -n line-numbered input real agents produce, it compresses slightly less (27.8%) and retains more (89.3%); there the paired test is informative, with 30 instances solved only uncompressed and 17 only compressed, an exact McNemar p=0.079, so at this sample size compressing context to roughly a quarter of its size does not significantly reduce the solve rate. The model is a 264 MB adapter that self-hosts on one 24 GB GPU with no per-token compressor fee, which at list prices decides the economics: gpt-5 as a compressor is net-negative, costing more than the downstream tokens it saves. Weights, data, and evaluation scripts are open (Apache 2.0).

</details>


### [62] [Preference Data Selection for Mitigating the Alignment Tax in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24192)
*Minsu Kim,Jianxun Lian,Xing Xie,Steven Euijong Whang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BALIGN is a balanced data selection strategy that mitigates catastrophic forgetting in LLM alignment by filtering high-risk preference samples based on three data-centric features, preserving general capabilities without compromising alignment gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aligning LLMs to human preferences often causes catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained general capabilities. While previous works focus on optimization or architectural challenges, the inherent characteristics of preference data driving this degradation remain underexplored.

Method: BALIGN uses a balanced data selection strategy that identifies three key data-centric features from preference optimization gradient analysis: reference model's log-probability margin, token length difference between chosen/rejected responses, and TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora. These features are aggregated into a unified composite risk score to filter high-risk preference samples.

Result: Extensive experiments on standard human preference datasets show BALIGN strongly preserves foundational capabilities without compromising alignment gains, consistently achieving optimal Pareto frontier with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: BALIGN provides an effective data-centric approach to mitigate catastrophic forgetting in LLM alignment by focusing on the inherent characteristics of preference data, offering a practical solution that maintains both alignment efficacy and general capabilities.

Abstract: Aligning large language models to human preferences is crucial for real-world deployment but frequently incurs an alignment tax, leading to the catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained general capabilities. While previous works primarily frame this problem as an optimization or architectural challenge, the inherent characteristics of preference data that drive this degradation remain largely underexplored. In this paper, we propose BALIGN, a balanced data selection strategy that explicitly mitigates catastrophic forgetting while optimizing alignment efficacy. Through theoretical and empirical analyses of the preference optimization gradient, we identify three key data-centric features that dictate parameter drift: the reference model's log-probability margin, the token length difference between chosen and rejected responses, and the TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora. By aggregating these orthogonal features into a unified composite risk score, BALIGN systematically filters out high-risk preference samples that disrupt intrinsic model parameters or provide minimal alignment utility. Extensive experiments on standard human preference datasets demonstrate that BALIGN strongly preserves foundational capabilities without compromising alignment gains, consistently achieving the optimal Pareto frontier with minimal computational overhead.

</details>


### [63] [MetaRAG: Belief-Action Aligned Policy Optimization for Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24214)
*Qiuyi Qi,Tian Liang,Jiamu Wang,Jinjian Zhang,Wei Zhou,Pengcheng Zhu,Linjian Mo,Ming Kong,Jie Liu,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRAG introduces a belief-action alignment framework for agentic RAG that optimizes search decisions by aligning the model's internal belief about answerability with its search actions, improving accuracy-efficiency trade-offs without inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based methods for agentic RAG rely on external supervision and ignore the agent's internal belief about whether current evidence is sufficient, leading to suboptimal search decisions and accuracy-efficiency trade-offs.

Method: MetaRAG proposes Verify-first Action Generation (elicits explicit verification before actions) and Internal Belief Probing (estimates model's answerability belief). It uses a consistency reward gated by answer correctness to optimize policy alignment without inference-time overhead.

Result: Experiments on seven public QA benchmarks show MetaRAG consistently improves accuracy-efficiency trade-off over strong RL-based baselines, with gains that transfer to deep research settings, different optimizers, and multiple model backbones.

Conclusion: MetaRAG demonstrates that aligning internal beliefs with actions through belief-action alignment optimization significantly enhances agentic RAG performance, providing an effective framework for improving search decisions in retrieval-augmented generation systems.

Abstract: Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires language models to decide when to continue searching and when to answer. Existing RL-based methods rely on external supervision and overlook the agent's internal belief about whether the current evidence is sufficient. To address this problem, we reformulate the search decision quality as belief-action alignment and propose MetaRAG, a belief-action aligned policy optimization framework for agentic RAG. MetaRAG uses Verify-first Action Generation to elicit an explicit verification process before each actual action, and Internal Belief Probing to estimate the policy model's own answerability belief from the same question-history context. Based on these, MetaRAG derives a consistency reward that is further gated by answer correctness, avoiding reinforcement of internally consistent but incorrect trajectories. The belief probe is used only during training and introduces no inference-time overhead. Experiments on seven public QA benchmarks show that MetaRAG consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off over strong RL-based agentic RAG baselines, with gains that transfer to deep research settings, different optimizers, and multiple model backbones.

</details>


### [64] [Constraint-Guided Enterprise Data Mapping with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24218)
*Sebastian Monka,Pramod Anantharam,Thien Vo Minh,Lavdim Halilaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CGM: A neuro-symbolic entity alignment method combining LLMs with structural constraints to ensure operational validity while maintaining semantic matching capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise entity alignment faces challenges with semi-structured records, implicit attributes, and unit mismatches. Manual matching doesn't scale, while LLM-only matching can produce fluent but operationally invalid correspondences that violate structural and physical invariants.

Method: Constraint-guided mapping (CGM) with three stages: 1) schema-grounded admissibility constraints with metadata specifying constraint type and executable logic, 2) constraint-restricted candidate generation with cascade relaxation to guarantee feasible solutions, and 3) neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation restricted to the feasible set. Constraints operate as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc validators.

Result: Hard admissibility shrinks candidate space by ~480x without dropping ground truth. Constraints provide decisive lift (F1 from 0.08 to 0.66) independent of model choice. Small model with constraints matches frontier LLM without constraints at ~28x lower cost. Transfers across seven enterprise domains with macro F1 0.70, lowering expert effort by ~7x vs spreadsheet workflows.

Conclusion: Constraint-guided mapping provides a practical neuro-symbolic approach to enterprise entity alignment that ensures operational validity while maintaining efficiency. Constraints should be hard only where structural invariants are match-determining, enabling controlled degradation and auditable decisions.

Abstract: Enterprise entity alignment must handle semi-structured records, implicit attributes, and unit or granularity mismatches. Manual matching is still common in practice, but does not scale as schemas and providers evolve. LLM-only matching improves semantic recall, yet can violate structural and physical invariants, producing fluent yet operationally invalid correspondences.
  We propose constraint-guided mapping (CGM), a neuro-symbolic method with three stages: (i) schema-grounded admissibility constraints with metadata mc = <tau_c, delta_c>, where tau_c denotes the constraint type and delta_c provides executable relation and normalization logic; (ii) constraint-restricted candidate generation with cascade relaxation to guarantee a nonempty feasible set under noise; and (iii) neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation restricted to that feasible set.
  Methodologically, constraints operate as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc validators, enabling controlled degradation under relaxation and auditable, human-guidable decisions. On a controlled structural-decoy benchmark, hard admissibility shrinks the candidate space by ~480x without dropping the GT, and a layer-by-layer ablation shows this gate, not the LLM, is the decisive lift (F1 0.08 to 0.66). The benefit is model-independent and adds no extra inference cost: a small model with constraints matches a frontier LLM used without them at ~28x lower cost. The method, not a single tuned configuration, transfers across seven enterprise makes (macro F1 0.70), each under its own automatically discovered, expert-refinable constraints, and lowers expert effort by ~7x versus spreadsheet workflows. Public Valentine results add an external ranking sanity check and mark the boundary: constraints should be hard only where structural invariants are match-determining.

</details>


### [65] [Evaluating Multiple LLM Generations with Validated Task Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24228)
*Florian Le Bronnec,Rio Yokota*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VTC-Bench is a new benchmark that evaluates how many distinct useful outputs LLMs can produce in k attempts, measuring task coverage rather than just single-output quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation focuses too much on individual outputs or reduces multiple samples to single answers, missing whether models can produce several genuinely different useful results for comparison, validation, or combination purposes.

Method: Developed VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark using real-data tasks where both output quality and task-relevant distinctness can be automatically verified without model-based judges. Introduced Validated Task Coverage (VTC) metric measuring how many distinct useful results are obtained within k attempts.

Result: Configurations that appear strongest in single-draw quality are not necessarily best for coverage. Simple measures of output variation don't reliably capture task-relevant coverage. The benchmark reveals differences in model behavior not apparent from conventional per-output evaluation.

Conclusion: Finite candidate sets can be directly evaluated as objects of interest, showing that coverage-focused evaluation leads to different conclusions than conventional approaches, highlighting the importance of measuring multiple distinct useful outputs.

Abstract: Many LLM applications are most useful when they provide several candidate outputs for comparison, validation, or combination. Predominant evaluation settings, however, still focus on individual outputs or reduce multiple samples to a single success or selected answer. This can miss whether the outputs include several genuinely different useful results. We introduce VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark for this setting, together with Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as its core evaluation quantity. The benchmark is built from carefully selected real-data tasks where both output quality and task-relevant distinctness can be checked automatically and reproducibly, without model-based judges. VTC measures how many distinct useful results are obtained within $k$ attempts. Across multiple models and inference settings, the benchmark leads to different conclusions from conventional evaluation: configurations that look strongest from single-draw quality are not necessarily those with the best coverage, and simple measures of output variation do not reliably recover task-relevant coverage. These results show that finite candidate sets can be evaluated directly as objects of interest, revealing differences in model behavior that are not apparent from conventional per-output evaluation.

</details>


### [66] [TRACE: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Safety Evaluation of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24232)
*Zhenyu Wu,Siyuan Chen,Changchun Yang,Jiaqi Dong,Min Zhou,Ali Almadan,Talal Hammad,Faisal Wahbo,Aminullah Tora,Mona Alshahrani,Xin Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a new evidence-grounded safety evaluation benchmark for Large Reasoning Models that covers the entire inference pipeline (prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses) with multi-language prompts across risk categories and attack strategies, revealing that detecting unsafe content in reasoning traces is much harder than in prompts or final responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety benchmarks for Large Reasoning Models have two main limitations: 1) They focus primarily on prompts and final responses while largely ignoring intermediate reasoning traces, which may contain unsafe content even when final responses appear safe, and 2) They typically provide only binary safety labels without evidence annotations that justify the judgments.

Method: The authors introduce TRACE benchmark with prompts in two languages across nine risk categories and ten attack strategies. For each prompt, four LRMs generate reasoning traces and final responses. They annotate safety of each component and extract supporting evidence from corresponding source text, then evaluate 18 guardrail models on this benchmark.

Result: Evaluation of 18 guardrail models on TRACE reveals that: 1) Safety judgment for reasoning traces is substantially more challenging than for prompts or final responses, and 2) Current models struggle to accurately extract supporting evidence to justify safety judgments.

Conclusion: There is a critical need for guardrail models that can reliably detect and precisely localize unsafe content across the entire LRM inference pipeline (prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses), and future safety evaluation benchmarks should include comprehensive coverage with evidence annotations.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) generate intermediate reasoning traces that may contain unsafe content, even when their final responses appear safe. Guardrail models are designed to detect and block unsafe content, yet existing benchmarks for unsafe content detection focus primarily on prompts and final responses, leaving reasoning traces largely unexamined. Moreover, these benchmarks typically provide only binary safety labels, without evidence annotations that justify the judgments. To address these limitations, we introduce TRACE, an evidence-grounded safety evaluation benchmark that covers the entire LRM inference pipeline: prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses. TRACE includes prompts in two languages spanning nine risk categories and ten attack strategies. For each prompt, four LRMs generate reasoning traces and final responses, and we annotate the safety of each component and extract supporting evidence from the corresponding source text. Evaluating 18 guardrail models on TRACE reveals that safety judgment for reasoning traces is substantially more challenging than for prompts or final responses, and that current models struggle to accurately extract supporting evidence. These findings highlight the need for guardrail models that can reliably detect and precisely localize unsafe content across the LRM inference pipeline.

</details>


### [67] [STRIVE: Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for Longitudinal Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24237)
*Junyeong Maeng,Eunsong Kang,Heung-Il Suk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STRIVE is a multi-agent system for longitudinal radiology report generation that decomposes clinical reasoning into specialized agents with explicit intermediate evidence and verification stages, achieving state-of-the-art performance on temporal change detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for longitudinal radiology report generation have several limitations: they use implicit representations that cause task interference, obscure decision evidence, limit error traceability, and treat progression states as independent labels (ignoring their ordered structure and treating different types of errors equally).

Method: STRIVE decomposes clinical reasoning into three specialized agents: Diagnosis Agent, Attribute Agent, and Temporal Change Agent. The Temporal Change Agent is post-trained with Progression-Aware GRPO, a shaped reward that assigns partial credit to direction-preserving errors. The system includes two verification stages: a deterministic Consistency Gate to reconcile agent outputs, and a Validation Agent to check if generated reports are supported by aggregated clinical evidence.

Result: On the Longitudinal-MIMIC dataset, STRIVE achieves the best clinical efficacy among recent methods and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC), a measure of temporal agreement with reference reports, over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: The explicit multi-agent decomposition with specialized reasoning, combined with progression-aware training and two-stage verification, provides a more transparent, evidence-based approach to longitudinal radiology report generation that significantly improves temporal change detection while maintaining overall clinical efficacy.

Abstract: Longitudinal radiology report generation (LRRG) requires identifying both current findings and their changes relative to a prior study. Existing methods jointly model diagnosis, attribute estimation, temporal comparison, and language generation within implicit representations, which can cause task interference, obscure the evidence underlying each decision, and limit error traceability. They also model progression states as independent labels, ignoring their ordered structure and thus treating missed changes and direction reversals equally. We present STRIVE, Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for LRRG, which decomposes clinical reasoning into specialized Diagnosis, Attribute, and Temporal Change Agents that produce explicit intermediate evidence. In particular, the Temporal Change Agent is further post-trained using Progression-Aware GRPO, a verifiable, shaped reward that assigns partial credit to direction-preserving errors while scoring direction reversals lowest. STRIVE performs verification at two stages: a deterministic Consistency Gate reconciles the agent outputs before report generation, and a Validation Agent checks whether the generated report is supported by the aggregated clinical evidence. On Longitudinal-MIMIC, STRIVE attains the best clinical efficacy among recent methods and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC), a measure of temporal agreement with the reference report, over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [68] [SA-Bench: Evaluating Semantic Alignment in LLM-Based Paper Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24252)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zeli Su,Zhou Liu,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents often fail to reproduce papers accurately due to "semantic drift" - code that silently diverges from specifications. SA-Bench evaluates 30 papers with 1,491 verifiable implementation claims, finding even top-performing configurations achieve poor scores, revealing critical limitations in current code generation approaches for scientific reproduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the problem of LLM agents generating scientifically unfaithful paper reproduction code, characterized as "semantic drift" where generated code silently diverges from paper specifications. Current code generation approaches may produce executable code that doesn't accurately implement the scientific methods described in papers.

Method: The authors create SemanticAlign-Bench (SA-Bench), a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 papers from ICLR, ICML and NeurIPS 2025. They decompose paper specifications into atomic, verifiable implementation claims called Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs) and evaluate repositories across four diagnostic dimensions: numerical, methodological, protocol, and ordering drift. They evaluate 12 generator configurations (4 models × 3 scaffolds) across 1,491 SAUs spanning five ML domains.

Result: Even the strongest configuration (Claude+PaperCoder) achieves only a mean SAU score of 0.301 out of 1.0, with an overall mean of 0.221 across 360 evaluations. Failure analysis reveals agents attempt most requirements but implement them incorrectly, with implementation mismatch and stubs accounting for the majority of zero-scored claims. Scaffolds optimized for executability provide limited leverage for scientific reproduction.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents for paper reproduction suffer from significant semantic drift, producing code that is often executable but scientifically unfaithful. To narrow the gap, future work needs scaffolds that prioritize semantic specification verification rather than just executability. The benchmark and tools are publicly released to advance research in this area.

Abstract: LLM agents can generate paper reproduction code, yet often produce scientifically unfaithful implementations. We define this failure mode as semantic drift, where generated code silently diverges from the paper's specifications. We introduce SemanticAlign-Bench(SA-Bench), a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 papers from ICLR, ICML and NeurIPS 2025. For each paper, we decompose its specifications into atomic and verifiable implementation claims, which we call Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs) and evaluate repositories along four diagnostic dimensions spanning numerical, methodological, protocol and ordering drift. In total, we construct 1,491 SAUs across five ML domains and evaluate 12 generator configurations (4 models $\times$ 3 scaffolds). Even the strongest configuration (Claude+PaperCoder) achieves a mean SAU score of only 0.301 out of 1.0, with an overall mean of 0.221 across 360 evaluations. A failure taxonomy reveals that agents attempt most requirements but implement them incorrectly, with implementation mismatch and stubs accounting for the majority of zero-scored claims. Our analysis further indicates that scaffolds optimized for executability provide limited leverage for scientific reproduction; narrowing the gap requires scaffolds that prioritize semantic specification verification. The benchmark, annotations and evaluation pipeline are publicly available.

</details>


### [69] [Beyond Accuracy: A Dual-Judge Evaluation Protocol for Vision-Language Models in Legally Grounded Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24258)
*Su Myat Noe,Ha Thanh Nguyen,May Myo Zin,Ken Satoh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A dual-judge evaluation protocol for legal AI systems that pairs quality scoring with strict semantic equivalence checking, revealing systematic Type II error patterns in traffic sign interpretation tasks under varying visibility conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal AI benchmarks and evaluation protocols lack mechanisms to detect when high-quality outputs fail to achieve semantic equivalence with reference legal standards, which is critical for legally accountable AI systems where both correctness and justifiability are required.

Method: Developed a dual-judge protocol: (1) standard 0-10 quality judge for response quality, and (2) strict binary semantic-equivalence judge against human-curated references. Applied to UK traffic sign interpretation as a controlled regulatory task with 4,680 evaluations across 7 visibility levels and 2 occlusion modes.

Result: The two judges show moderate association (r = 0.644) but reveal asymmetric Type II patterns affecting 8.0% of evaluations. High visibility conditions have highest marginal error rates (14.2% at v = 0.8), but when conditioned on high-quality answers (score > 7), heavy occlusion shows highest untrustworthiness rates (54-63% at v <= 0.3). Human validation confirms the quality judge aligns well with human judgement (r = 0.81).

Conclusion: The dual-judge protocol reveals systematic evaluation blind spots not captured by single-judge approaches, particularly showing that high quality scores become least trustworthy under degraded input conditions. This provides an additional evaluation signal for legally accountable AI systems.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly evaluated for legally accountable settings, where correct outputs must also be justifiable against an applicable legal standard. Existing legal-AI benchmarks and LLM-as-judge protocols provide important infrastructure for measuring task performance and open-ended response quality. We contribute one additional evaluation signal: a dual-judge protocol that pairs a standard 0-10 quality judge with a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge against a human-curated reference.
  We study a controlled, visually grounded regulatory task - UK traffic-sign interpretation, whose meaning is a codified question with a known reference for every input - and measure not merely whether the two judges disagree (by construction they must) but how much and where. On 4,680 evaluations under seven visibility levels and two occlusion modes, the two judges are moderately associated (point-biserial r = 0.644), while revealing an asymmetric Type II pattern affecting 8.0% of all evaluations.
  Its distribution is instructive: the marginal rate peaks at high visibility (14.2% at v = 0.8) simply because high-scoring answers are common there, but conditioned on the answer already scoring above 7, the rate is highest under heavy occlusion (54-63% at v <= 0.3), so a high quality score is least trustworthy when the input is most degraded.
  We are explicit that the signal is a property of this judge and reference: a 49-row human check shows the 0-10 judge aligns closely with everyday-reader judgement (Pearson r = 0.81; r = 0.80 with the LLM accuracy sub-score), while the equivalence judge is fairly but one-directionally stricter. The protocol adds one LLM call per evaluation and surfaces a signal single-judge protocols do not report. We release the prompt template, occluded variants, and full evaluation results.

</details>


### [70] [Real-World Knowledge-Guided Change Data Synthesis for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24263)
*Yaoyi Qi,Xingxing Weng,Chao Pang,Yongkang Cui,Xiangyu Hao,Xiaokang Zhang,Guibo Zhu,Gui-Song Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowChange is a knowledge-guided change data synthesis framework that uses pretrained vision-language models to reason about plausible changes from pre-change scenes and desired change types, enabling more diverse and flexible synthetic change detection data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing change data synthesis methods rely on handcrafted rules with limited coverage of class transitions, restricting diversity and flexibility. They struggle to accommodate varied change types and have predefined transition designs that limit their effectiveness.

Method: KnowChange leverages pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions from pre-change scenes and desired change types. It integrates knowledge-guided change simulation with generalizable synthesis models within a unified framework.

Result: KnowChange-generated data consistently outperforms existing synthetic datasets in both synthetic-to-real transfer and synthetic data augmentation, despite being generated at a compact scale. The framework can be seamlessly integrated into existing synthesis pipelines and enhances downstream utility.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided change simulation using pretrained vision-language models provides a more flexible and diverse approach to change data synthesis, overcoming limitations of rule-based methods and improving the performance of change detection models.

Abstract: Change data synthesis provides a cost-effective solution for expanding training data and improving the performance of change detection models. However, existing synthesis methods typically rely on handcrafted rules to simulate changes, where limited coverage of class transitions restricts the diversity of synthesized data, while predefined transition designs limit their flexibility in accommodating varied change types. In this work, we introduce KnowChange, a knowledge-guided change data synthesis framework that leverages pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions from pre-change scenes and desired change types. By integrating knowledge-guided change simulation with generalizable synthesis models, KnowChange enables flexible synthesis of diverse change types within a unified framework. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KnowChange-generated data consistently outperforms existing synthetic datasets in both synthetic-to-real transfer and synthetic data augmentation, despite being generated at a compact scale. Further analyses show that the knowledge-guided change simulation can be seamlessly integrated into existing synthesis pipelines and enhance the downstream utility of synthesized data.

</details>


### [71] [Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization for Candidate-Set Interference in Continual Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24273)
*Hao Ren,Junbin Gao,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEOR regularization method reduces rank degradation in continual knowledge graph embedding when new entities are added, by comparing newcomer competition against structurally matched old references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continual knowledge graph embedding methods focus on catastrophic forgetting but overlook that entity admission changes the candidate universe for queries, causing historical answers to lose rank even when their scores are preserved - an effect called candidate-set interference.

Method: Introduces Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR), a host-level objective that compares smooth answer-relative newcomer pressure with score-blind, structurally matched old references. It uses a one-sided penalty that only activates when newcomer competition exceeds the matched reference.

Result: MEOR improves historical current-universe mean reciprocal rank (MRR) by 0.0057 over replay and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055. It preserves old-universe ranking while supporting newcomer acquisition, outperforming persistent calibration, MMR, and UOR across multiple datasets and settings.

Conclusion: Candidate admission is a distinct source of continual rank loss that can be controlled without changing the underlying embedding architecture or continual learner. MEOR effectively addresses candidate-set interference while maintaining performance on both old and new entities.

Abstract: Continual knowledge graph embedding updates entity and relation representations as a graph grows. Existing methods primarily address catastrophic forgetting, but entity admission also changes the candidate universe of every compatible query. A historical answer can therefore lose rank even when its score and its ordering among old entities are preserved. We formalize this effect as candidate-set interference and introduce Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR), a host-level objective that compares smooth answer-relative newcomer pressure with score-blind, structurally matched old references. Its one-sided penalty acts only when newcomer competition exceeds the matched reference, preserving the host learner's signal for legitimate new entities. Across eight paired runs on ENTITY-ComplEx, MEOR improves historical current-universe mean reciprocal rank (MRR) by 0.0057 over replay and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055, with one-sided 95% lower bounds of 0.0052 and 0.0051, respectively. It satisfies the preservation criteria for old-universe ranking and newcomer acquisition and improves historical current-universe MRR over persistent calibration, matched maximum regularizer (MMR), and unmatched old regularizer (UOR). Direct ablations support each component of its reference construction and aggregation. Adding MEOR also improves historical ranking in all ten reported FBInc-S and FBInc-L host and backbone settings, with every paired 95% confidence interval excluding zero. These results establish candidate admission as a distinct source of continual rank loss and show that it can be controlled without replacing the underlying embedding architecture or continual learner.

</details>


### [72] [Eating for a Sustainable Planet: Personalized Sustainable Diet Recommendation via Constraint-Aware Decision-Making Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24274)
*Ying Jin,Weiqing Min,Mingyu Huang,Shuqiang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A personalized sustainable diet recommendation model that incorporates sustainability as learnable constraints rather than user preferences, enabling individual adoption while meeting sustainability requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While population-level sustainability modeling exists, practical implementation fails at individual level due to heterogeneity in preferences, creating challenges in aligning sustainable diet requirements with individual choices.

Method: Personalized sustainable diet recommendation model using constraint-aware decision-making mechanism, where sustainability is incorporated through learnable constraints rather than modeled as user preferences. Evaluated using SusDiet dataset with 150k recipes and comprehensive sustainability indicators.

Result: Method promotes more sustainable choices without compromising individual preferences, establishing quantitative evidence for aligning individual dietary choices with planetary health.

Conclusion: Provides a framework for sustainable diet adoption at individual level, offering guidance for future interventions and policy-making for sustainable development.

Abstract: A sustainable diet represents a multi-dimensional synergy among four essential pillars: nutrition adequacy, economic affordability, cultural acceptability, and environmental respect. Despite the prevalence of population-level sustainability modeling, practical implementation relies on effective individual-level adoption. This transition is often hindered by inter-individual heterogeneity, posing a formidable challenge in aligning sustainable diet requirements with individual preferences. To address this issue, we propose a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model based on a constraint-aware decision-making mechanism, where sustainability is incorporated through learnable constraints rather than modeled as user preferences. To systematically evaluate the proposed approach, we construct a sustainable diet dataset named SusDiet with about 150k recipes, characterized by broad coverage of sustainability indicators. Experimental results on this dataset show that our method promotes more sustainable choices without compromising individual preference. This work establishes a framework for aligning individual dietary choices with planetary health, offering quantitative evidence to guide future sustainable diet interventions and policy-making for sustainable development.

</details>


### [73] [RePolicy: Reinforcement Learning for Safety-Policy Invocation in Agent Safeguards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24275)
*Houcheng Jiang,Boxuan Zhang,Qiyong Zhong,Junfeng Fang,Xiang Wang,Xiangnan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RePolicy: A reinforcement learning-based agent safeguard that invokes dynamic safety policies from a policy library to produce policy-grounded rationales and safety judgments for language model agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing policy-aware safeguards for language model agents mainly rely on prompting or supervised fine-tuning, which limits their ability to adapt to unseen trajectories and changing policy contexts. Current approaches lack robust adaptation to dynamic safety policies.

Method: RePolicy learns safety-policy invocation through reinforcement learning. It takes an agent trajectory and dynamic policy library as input, invokes the applicable policy, and uses its content to produce policy-grounded rationale and safety judgment. The method includes supervised initialization using PolicyTraj-20K dataset, followed by GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation.

Result: Experiments across six agent safety benchmarks show that RePolicy achieves strong overall safety-detection performance and robust policy invocation under varying policy contexts, outperforming existing approaches.

Conclusion: RePolicy demonstrates that reinforcement learning-based policy invocation can effectively adapt to dynamic safety policies and unseen trajectories, providing a more robust safeguard framework for language model agents compared to traditional prompting or supervised fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Safeguarding language model agents requires assessing complete execution trajectories under context-dependent safety policies. Existing policy-aware safeguards mainly rely on prompting or supervised fine-tuning, limiting their ability to adapt to unseen trajectories and changing policy contexts. We propose RePolicy, an agent safeguard that learns safety-policy invocation through reinforcement learning. Given an agent trajectory and a dynamic policy library, RePolicy invokes the applicable policy and uses its content to produce a policy-grounded rationale and safety judgment. We construct PolicyTraj-20K to support supervised initialization, followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation. Experiments across six agent safety benchmarks show that RePolicy achieves strong overall safety-detection performance and robust policy invocation under varying policy contexts.

</details>


### [74] [ReproAgent: Contract-Guided Paper-to-Code Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24291)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zhongyuan Wang,Zhou Liu,Zeli Su,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReproAgent: A four-stage pipeline that helps AI agents reproduce research papers into executable code repositories by maintaining a persistent implementation contract with two channels for explicit paper requirements and implicit contextual evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Paper-to-code reproduction is difficult because explicit paper content gets lost across long agent trajectories, while implicit details like framework defaults and conventions are absent from papers, leading to incomplete or incorrect implementations.

Method: ReproAgent uses a four-stage Prepare-Plan-Generate-Repair pipeline built around a persistent implementation contract with two channels: 1) implementation-requirement channel that turns paper snippets into code obligations, and 2) reference-evidence channel that retrieves content and structure evidence from related repositories. Both are bound to work packages and projected into file-level contracts.

Result: On PaperBench Code-Dev benchmark, ReproAgent achieves the highest mean score among same-backbone scaffolds under both Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and Gemini-3-Flash. End-to-end channel ablations and per-paper cases demonstrate the contribution of both channels to successful reproduction.

Conclusion: ReproAgent's dual-channel approach effectively addresses the split specification problem in paper-to-code reproduction by maintaining persistent contracts that capture both explicit paper requirements and implicit contextual evidence, leading to more accurate and complete code implementations.
