On single-frame referring image segmentation and representative general VLM benchmarks, PhysMLLMs maintains comparable performance, demonstrating that the injected spatial prior improves video consistency without compromising image-level grounding or general multimodal capability. These results suggest that physics-inspired spatial prior injection can improve temporal stability while preserving general capability. The code is available at https://github.com/tusu-code/20260121-icml2026-2.git.

</details>


### [99] [Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding: Solvability, Complexity, and Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24585)
*Andrea Di Nezza,Mihir Patel,Fabio Fagnani,Sara Bernardini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding (PS-MAPF), a MAPF variant where agents must visit interchangeable pivots (workstations) before parking at anonymous stations, addressing automated storage systems. The authors provide solvability characterization, NP-hardness proofs for optimization, and propose efficient algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated high-density storage systems (warehouses, robotic parking, plant logistics) require fleets of agents to move through scarce task-critical resources and park without obstructing future operations. There's a need for efficient path planning where agents must first visit workstations (pivots) before parking at stations.

Method: 1. Introduce PS-MAPF problem formulation. 2. Characterize solvability completely: prove every instance on 2-edge-connected graphs is solvable, and establish necessary/sufficient conditions on arbitrary connected graphs using structural effective-distance measure. 3. Prove NP-hardness of minimizing station-makespan or station-flowtime with single pivot. 4. Develop three algorithms: complete baseline, SAT-based optimal solver, and Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP).

Result: Complete solvability characterization established. NP-hardness of optimization problems proved. Three algorithms developed, with PPP solving 74-89% of benchmark instances while achieving makespan and flowtime orders of magnitude below the baseline algorithm.

Conclusion: PS-MAPF effectively models practical storage system scenarios with theoretical solvability guarantees. The Pivot-Prioritized Planning algorithm provides efficient, near-optimal solutions for most instances, making the approach practical for real-world automated storage applications.

Abstract: Automated high-density storage systems (warehouses, robotic parking, plant logistics, etc.) require fleets of agents to move through scarce task-critical resources and then park without obstructing future operations. We introduce Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding (PS-MAPF), a MAPF variant in which a subset of tasked agents must each visit one of a set of interchangeable pivots (e.g., workstations) before the entire fleet terminates at anonymous stations, one agent per station. We characterize solvability completely: every instance on a 2-edge-connected graph is solvable, and, on arbitrary connected graphs, a structural effective-distance measure relative to the number of unoccupied vertices gives a necessary and sufficient condition. We prove that minimizing station-makespan or station-flowtime is NP-hard already with a single pivot. We present three algorithms, a complete baseline, a SAT-based optimal solver, and Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP), the last solving 74-89% of benchmark instances with makespan and flowtime orders of magnitude below the baseline.

</details>


### [100] [Causal Modelling of Support Interventions for Student Competency Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24632)
*Francesca Mangili,Alessandro Antonucci,Rafael Cabañas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes using structural causal modeling instead of traditional psychometric models like item response theory to enable interventional and counterfactual reasoning in educational assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional educational assessment using psychometric models (like item response theory) only supports probabilistic belief updating, but cannot handle interventional and counterfactual reasoning about educational scenarios like providing hints or analyzing alternative outcomes.

Method: The authors propose a protocol for constructing structural causal models for educational assessment, where structural equations are elicited from domain experts. The approach uses purely logical information rather than probabilistic assumptions, and is illustrated with data from an assessment measuring compulsory school students' algorithmic skills.

Result: The framework enables reasoning about interventions (like hints) and counterfactual scenarios that are inaccessible to standard associative models. The protocol provides practical relevance for these advanced forms of reasoning in educational assessment contexts.

Conclusion: Structural causal modeling offers a valuable extension to traditional educational assessment by supporting interventional and counterfactual reasoning, providing educators with more powerful tools for understanding student competencies and designing interventions.

Abstract: Accurate assessment of student competencies is essential for enabling educators to identify individual needs, design targeted interventions, and evaluate the effectiveness of educational strategies. Empirical assessment procedures are typically grounded in psychometric models, such as item response theory, which relate student competence levels to performance on assessment tasks. In this paper, we advocate adopting a structural causal modelling approach to educational assessment, moving beyond probabilistic belief updating toward a framework that explicitly supports interventional and counterfactual reasoning. We propose a corresponding protocol for its construction and analyse the practical relevance of forms of reasoning that remain inaccessible to standard associative models, including the explicit modelling of interventions such as hints and the related counterfactual scenario analysis. Although our protocol requires the structural equations to be elicited from experts, the necessary information is purely logical and does not rely on probabilistic, less tenable assumptions. We illustrate the approach using data from an assessment that employs complex tasks designed to measure compulsory school student algorithmic skills.

</details>


### [101] [Parason: Revealing Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24658)
*Zhengyang Zhang,Zijian Zhang,Jiaxuan Gao,Shusheng Xu,Yi Wu,Song Han,Ligeng Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Parason introduces a system for parallel LLM reasoning that combines both Subtask Parallelism and Trial Parallelism, achieving 1.7× acceleration on mathematical reasoning benchmarks while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard autoregressive decoding in LLMs executes reasoning traces sequentially, causing severe latency (up to days/weeks for difficult tasks). Existing systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism but overlook Trial Parallelism where multiple speculative attempts explore competing hypotheses in parallel.

Method: Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories using context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO) that jointly balances accuracy, latency, and both parallelism ratios. At inference, it executes learned parallel structures through tool calls.

Result: Analysis shows Trial Parallelism constitutes 65.5% of parallelizable reasoning computation in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE, becoming increasingly dominant on hard problems. On mathematical reasoning benchmarks (AIME24, AIME25), Parason achieves average ~1.7× acceleration while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Conclusion: Parason successfully reveals and learns both Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM reasoning, demonstrating that parallel reasoning approaches can provide significant real-world wall-clock acceleration while preserving model accuracy, with Trial Parallelism playing a crucial role in parallelizable computation.

Abstract: Scaling test-time reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but standard autoregressive decoding still executes long reasoning traces sequentially, creating severe latency for difficult tasks (up to days and weeks). Parallel reasoning offers a natural remedy. However, prior systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism, where the model learns to decompose a high-level task into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. This approach overlooks another pervasive form of parallelism: Trial Parallelism, where multiple speculative attempts explore, verify, and aggregate competing hypotheses in parallel. In this paper, we introduce Parason, which reveals and learns both forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning. Our analysis identifies Trial Parallelism as the majority of parallelizable reasoning computation (65.5% in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE), and it becomes increasingly dominant on hard problems. Guided by this taxonomy, Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories with a context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios. At inference time, Parason executes the learned parallel structure through tool calls, translating theoretical savings to real-world wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25 show that Parason achieves an average acceleration about 1.7$\times$ while maintaining competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [102] [Confident at the moment of action: belief miscalibration in LLM play under hidden information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24691)
*Bhushan Kashinath Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent confidence calibration is poor in hidden-information chess games, with high-confidence captures being wrong 98% of the time, yet models can still win games, showing outcome evaluation fails to detect belief quality issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems increasingly use model confidence to gate actions, assuming confidence tracks correctness at decision time. The paper tests this assumption in a controlled setting with hidden information.

Method: Researchers created a hidden-information chess variant where royal status secretly relocates between pieces. They tracked agents' probability distributions over the opponent's hidden royal piece (elicited each turn separately from moves) and scored them against ground truth recovered after games.

Result: Captures made at high stated confidence (≥0.5) were correct in only 1 of 62 cases. The calibration deficit concentrated almost entirely in these events (99.3% in original batch, 98.7% replication). This pattern held across four additional model configurations, though statistically underpowered. Conventional evaluation metrics (legality, cost, latency, completion rate) completely dissociated from belief quality.

Conclusion: Model confidence doesn't reliably track correctness in hidden-information settings, yet models can still win games, meaning outcome-only evaluation misses critical belief quality issues. This reveals a dangerous dissociation between conventional performance metrics and true belief calibration.

Abstract: Agentic systems increasingly gate actions on a model's own stated confidence, which assumes confidence tracks correctness at the moment of acting. We test this in a hidden-information chess variant where royal status can be secretly, repeatedly relocated between pieces, and where an agent's stated probability distribution over the opponent's hidden royal piece -- elicited every turn, separately from the move it chooses -- is scored against ground truth recoverable after the game. Across two independent batches, captures made at high stated confidence ($\geq 0.5$) about the hidden piece's location were correct in 1 of 62 cases. The calibration deficit is concentrated almost entirely in these events: 99.3% of it in the original batch, 98.7% in the replication. The same pattern, in weaker form, orders consistently (point estimates only; most pairwise gaps are not statistically distinguishable at this sample size) across four further model configurations spanning a second provider -- reported as scope for the finding, not as evidence that capability predicts calibration: a same-model comparison at a fixed external leaderboard score shows a deliberation-budget change alone moves the metric by nearly as much as a large cross-model gap. In a separate seat, conventional evaluation axes -- legality, cost, latency, completion rate -- can dissociate entirely from belief quality, with the configuration winning on every conventional axis producing the worst belief quality tested. A model exhibiting this pattern can still win the game its belief was about, which is why outcome-only evaluation would not detect it.

</details>


### [103] [The Invisible Editorial Layer: Formalizing Undisclosed Inference-Time Steering, Probability Placement, and the Attribution Problem in Deployed Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24662)
*Augusto Camargo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reveals that LLM behavior isn't just determined by weights, data, and prompts, but also by hidden inference-time interventions that systematically modify output probabilities before token selection, creating a layer of control with significant governance and security implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation assumes behavior is determined by model weights, training data, alignment, and prompts, but this view is incomplete. Modern inference pipelines can systematically modify probability distributions before token selection, creating an undisclosed layer of control with unexplored governance, security, and economic implications.

Method: The paper formalizes the concept that Model != Deployed System and introduces three key concepts: (1) Inference Attribution Problem (why behavioral bias can't be causally attributed to model weights alone), (2) Probability Placement (advertising primitive using systematic probability shifts rather than explicit product insertions), and (3) Inference Policy Transparency (governance principle for making deployment-layer interventions auditable).

Result: The paper identifies inference-time framing bias as a systematic modification of generated language toward political, ideological, institutional, or commercial frames via interventions applied after model inference but before token sampling, examining this in relation to EU AI Act, Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.

Conclusion: There's a critical governance gap in inference-time interventions that can systematically bias LLM outputs. The paper calls for transparency and auditability of inference policies to address security and ethical concerns related to undisclosed framing biases in deployed LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are commonly evaluated under the assumption that their observable behavior is primarily determined by model weights, training data, alignment procedures, and user prompts. This view is incomplete. Modern inference pipelines may systematically modify the probability distribution produced by a model immediately before token selection, creating an additional layer of control between frozen weights and observed text.
  While controlled generation (e.g., PPLM, GeDi, DExperts, FUDGE) and text-watermarking systems (e.g., SynthID-Text) demonstrate the technical maturity of decoding- and logit-level interventions, the governance, security, and economic implications of an undisclosed inference policy remain comparatively underexplored. This paper examines the emergence of inference-time framing bias: the systematic modification of generated language toward political, ideological, institutional, or commercial frames via interventions applied after model inference but before token sampling.
  We formalize the operational reality Model != Deployed System and introduce three concepts: (1) the Inference Attribution Problem, characterizing why observed behavioral bias cannot generally be causally attributed to model weights alone under limited observability; (2) Probability Placement, defining a hypothetical advertising primitive in which commercial influence is implemented through systematic shifts in generation probabilities rather than explicit product insertions; and (3) Inference Policy Transparency, a governance principle for making deployment-layer interventions auditable. We examine these concepts in relation to Article 5 of the EU AI Act, the EU Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.

</details>


### [104] [Lifted Model Construction under Approximate Commutativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24713)
*Malte Luttermann,Jan Speller,Tanya Braun,Marcel Gehrke,Ralf Möller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ε-commutativity as a relaxation of exact commutativity to enable practical lifted inference when learned parameters deviate from perfect symmetry, maintaining high accuracy with theoretical error bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In practice, parameters learned from real data inevitably deviate from perfect symmetry even when objects are indistinguishable, causing factors to be only approximately commutative rather than exactly commutative. This prevents traditional lifted inference algorithms from constructing lifted representations for practical applications.

Method: The authors introduce the concept of ε-commutativity, which relaxes the requirement of exact commutativity to approximate commutativity where output values are only approximately invariant under permutations of input values. They show how to exploit ε-commutativity for lifted model construction and downstream probabilistic inference while proving strict bounds on the induced approximation error.

Result: The theoretical guarantees are confirmed empirically, demonstrating comparable query accuracy at lower runtime compared to traditional approaches. The method enables practical applicability of lifted model construction while maintaining highly accurate query results even when factors are only approximately commutative.

Conclusion: By introducing ε-commutativity as a relaxation of exact commutativity, the paper provides a practical solution for lifted inference in real-world scenarios where learned parameters deviate from perfect symmetry, bridging the gap between theoretical lifted inference algorithms and practical applications with data-learned parameters.

Abstract: Lifted inference algorithms enable scalable probabilistic inference even for large object domains by leveraging the indistinguishability of objects in a probability distribution. An essential prerequisite for constructing a lifted representation is to identify commutative factors, i.e., functions whose output values are invariant under permutations of a subset of their input values, in a potential-based factorisation. In practice, however, parameters learned from data inevitably deviate even if associated objects are indistinguishable, causing their corresponding factors to be only approximately commutative instead of being exactly commutative. We address this problem by introducing the concept of ε-commutativity, a relaxation of commutativity where output values are only approximately invariant under permutations of input values. Specifically, we show how ε-commutativity can be exploited for lifted model construction, downstream probabilistic inference, and prove strict bounds on the induced approximation error, thereby ensuring the practical applicability of lifted model construction while maintaining highly accurate query results. These theoretical guarantees are confirmed empirically, demonstrating comparable query accuracy at lower runtime.

</details>


### [105] [Meta$^n$: Recursive Self-Improvement through Emergent Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24735)
*Zae Myung Kim,Young-Jun Lee,Seungyeon Jwa,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Meta^n is a recursive self-improving LLM agent that applies a fixed meta-operation Ω repeatedly to its own outputs, enabling deeper meta-reasoning without system instability while outperforming prior methods on AGI benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current self-improving LLM agents face limitations: they either refine answers rather than the process, keep meta-levels fixed, or must leave part of their editing machinery untouched, capping meta-depth at roughly two levels. This limits their ability to achieve deeper recursive self-improvement.

Method: Meta^n uses a fixed meta-operation Ω that recursively applies to its own products. It reads the traces of the solver stack below along with the code that produced them, then writes the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Depth is determined by convergence rather than being fixed, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains.

Result: Meta^n outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families across two backbones. It achieves the sharpest improvement on ARC-AGI-2 (built to resist skill memorization), where it alone scores above zero. Ablations show most gains come from conditioning each layer passes to the next, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth without explicit prompting.

Conclusion: By keeping the meta-operation fixed and recursing on its input rather than modifying itself, Meta^n achieves stable, deep self-improvement beyond the typical two-level cap. The approach enables emergence of distinct layer roles and conditioning effects that drive performance gains on challenging AGI benchmarks.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents refine answers, not the process that produces those answers. Systems that add a meta-level hold that level fixed, and those that edit themselves must leave part of their own editing machinery untouched to stay stable, capping the meta-depth they realize at roughly two. We present Meta$^n$, which keeps the meta-operation fixed and recurses on its input instead. That operation, $Ω$, is applied repeatedly to its own products, reading the traces of the solver stack below together with the code that produced them, then writing the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Because $Ω$ never changes, it cannot destabilize the system, and because its input strictly grows, each layer reasons from a higher vantage than the last. Depth is set by convergence rather than fixed in advance, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains. Across two backbones, Meta$^n$ outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families. The sharpest case is ARC-AGI-2, built to resist skill memorization, where it alone scores above zero. Ablations indicate that most of the gain from recursion comes from the conditioning each layer passes to the next, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth although no prompt prescribes them. Code available at https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n

</details>


### [106] [RACE: Scalable Statistical Estimation of Functional Consistency in LLM Neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24758)
*Runyu Wang,Bo Liu,Xiaxin Zhang,Yu Han,Jiawei Cao,Xiaoye Zhang,Zhe Zhang,Yifan Yang,Peng Ping*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RACE is a forward-pass statistical framework for evaluating domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons with much lower computational cost than gradient methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for analyzing neuron behavior in mechanistic interpretability either rely on instance-level point estimates (which obscure population-level variability) or computationally expensive procedures (limiting scalable domain-wide analysis).

Method: RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation) - a forward-pass statistical framework that evaluates domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons by analyzing residual alignment patterns.

Result: 1. RACE achieves superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based point estimates in perturbation experiments. 2. Token-distribution-level results verify association between selected neurons and target domain. 3. Computational overhead is two orders of magnitude lower than gradient-based methods.

Conclusion: RACE provides an efficient and effective framework for discovering stable neuron behavior across entire domains in Transformer models, addressing key limitations of existing interpretability methods.

Abstract: Discovering stable neuron behavior across entire domains remains a challenge in mechanistic interpretability. Existing methods often rely on instance-level point estimates or computationally expensive procedures, which either obscure population-level variability or limit scalable domain-wide analysis. We present RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation), a forward-pass statistical framework that evaluates the domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons. Perturbation experiments demonstrate that RACE achieves superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based point estimates. Meanwhile, token-distribution-level results verify the association between the selected neurons and the target domain. Furthermore, its computational overhead is two orders of magnitude lower than that of gradient-based methods.

</details>


### [107] [Evidence Blindness in Direct Corpus Interaction: Persistent Navigation with AtlasNav](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24764)
*Hongyu Guo,Zhiyu Zheng,Zhao Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AtlasNav introduces a persistent multi-view corpus-navigation framework that organizes corpora into reusable structures for more efficient agentic search under finite interaction budgets, outperforming dynamic-workspace methods while reducing inference costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) approaches suffer from "Evidence Blindness" where required evidence may fail to surface or be used effectively under finite interaction budgets, and existing methods don't create reusable corpus organization.

Method: AtlasNav organizes corpora once into a persistent "Corpus Atlas" with multi-view representations, enabling each query to navigate adaptively over reusable structures rather than reconstructing query-conditioned spaces online.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, achieves 92.05% strict accuracy with 30.21% reduced online inference cost compared to prior dynamic-workspace SOTA, realizes evidence earlier under matched budgets, and scales effectively to 10K-1M documents while transferring competitively to enterprise knowledge.

Conclusion: Effective agentic search depends not just on accessible evidence but on how corpora are represented to enable limited interaction to become effective navigation, with persistent reusable structures outperforming online reconstruction approaches.

Abstract: Large language model agents are moving beyond conventional retrieval-augmented generation toward direct interaction with external corpora. Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) keeps the full corpus accessible, yet reachable evidence can remain unusable under finite interaction budgets. Required evidence may fail to surface, a surfaced supporting document may remain unopened, or an opened document may fail to expose its decisive fragment. We call this progressive silent loss Evidence Blindness and quantify it through stage-wise evidence realization. Within the DCI paradigm, raw interaction adds little reusable corpus organization, while dynamic-workspace methods reconstruct a query-conditioned interaction space from each query and trajectory. In both cases, useful structure is recovered largely online. We instead formulate large-scale agentic search as finite-budget navigation over reusable corpus structure. We introduce AtlasNav, a persistent multi-view corpus-navigation framework that retains direct corpus interaction but organizes the corpus once into a Corpus Atlas, allowing each query to navigate adaptively rather than reconstruct shared structure. On BrowseComp-Plus, AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy while reducing recorded online inference cost by 30.21% relative to the prior dynamic-workspace state of the art. Under matched budgets, it realizes the complete required evidence earlier and approaches the same model's evidence-supplied empirical reference more rapidly. The same representation principle remains effective under PhantomWiki's distinct corpus organization and controlled 10K-1M scaling, and transfers competitively to heterogeneous enterprise knowledge. These results show that agentic search depends not only on accessible evidence, but also on how the corpus is represented so that limited interaction becomes effective navigation.

</details>


### [108] [StepGuard: Learning Step-Level Guardrails with Scalable Supervision and Safety-Utility Balancing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24777)
*Zhijie Zheng,Yu Li,Chen Qian,Yuqian Fu,Yanwei Fu,Lu Sheng,Jing Shao,Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StepGuard: A step-level guard model for LLM-based agents that audits completed trajectories and checks tool actions before execution to prevent security risks like file modification, information leakage, and unauthorized actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents interacting with external environments through tool invocation introduce security risks, and existing guardrails often only evaluate completed trajectories, leaving pre-execution monitoring of step-level actions underexplored.

Method: Propose StepGuard guard model that audits trajectories and checks actions before execution. Use StepGen automatic data engine to generate safe/unsafe trajectories with same context but different actions at risky steps. Develop Balance-GRPO to dynamically balance learning between safe and unsafe actions based on observed accuracy.

Result: StepGuard achieves highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models, comparable to GPT-5.4. Reduces mean attack success rate by 77.3% relative to no-guard setting on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, with only 2.8% utility drop.

Conclusion: StepGuard provides effective step-level security monitoring for LLM agents with minimal utility impact, addressing the critical gap in pre-execution action checking for tool-invoking agents.

Abstract: LLM-based agents can interact with external environments through tool invocation, but this capability also introduces security risks such as file modification, information leakage, and unauthorized actions. Existing guardrails often evaluate completed trajectories, leaving pre-execution monitoring of step-level actions underexplored. We propose StepGuard, a step-level guard model that can audit completed agent trajectories and check tool actions before they are executed. To train StepGuard, we introduce StepGen, an automatic data engine that generates safe and unsafe trajectories with the same context but different actions at the risky step. To further reduce over-defense and under-defense, we propose Balance-GRPO, which dynamically balances learning between safe and unsafe actions based on their observed accuracy. Experiments show that StepGuard achieves the highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models, with performance comparable to GPT-5.4. When used to guard agents on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, StepGuard reduces mean attack success rate by 77.3% relative to the no-guard setting, while mean utility drops by only 2.8 percentage points.

</details>


### [109] [Right Diagnoses, Decorative Reasoning:A Perturbation Audit of Medical Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24790)
*Mengzhu Xu,Jifan Gao,Xia Jiang,Yaoxin Wu,Xi Long*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Medical LLMs' chain-of-thought rationales are largely decoupled from their answers, with 72.9% of models failing to update answers when clinical edits are made to both chains and questions, suggesting CoT may be documentation rather than faithful reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinicians rely on chain-of-thought (CoT) rationales as evidence of medical reasoning, but there's little testing of whether chains actually play that role. Existing CoT-faithfulness tests ignore clinical context and costs, while medical LLM evaluations treat the chain as a black box, creating a gap in understanding medical reasoning fidelity.

Method: Developed a medical perturbation audit with 30 clinically motivated operators (severity reversal, negation flip, demographic swap, evidence ablation) that edit both the chain and question. Used chain-update times answer-flip joint analysis to classify model failure modes. Tested 14 LLMs on four medical QA benchmarks with three independent convergence tests.

Result: Chain-Decoupling Rate (CDR) was 72.9% panel-wide on meaningful destructive edits, chain corruption left accuracy unchanged, and removing CoT prompting didn't reduce accuracy. Board-certified clinicians re-annotated 197 perturbed questions, with 98.5% leaving the gold defensible. Pattern held across medical/reasoning fine-tuning and scale, with closed-source models showing consistent decoupling signals.

Conclusion: Medical LLMs' chain-of-thought rationales are largely decoupled from their answers, challenging the assumption that CoT represents faithful medical reasoning. The framework and CDR provide a reusable yardstick for auditing whether medical CoT is faithful or merely documentation, revealing widespread reasoning disconnect.

Abstract: Clinicians read chain-of-thought (CoT) rationales as evidence of medical reasoning, but whether the visible chain plays that role is rarely tested. General-domain CoT-faithfulness probes ignore clinical cost, and medical LLM evaluations treat the chain as a black box. We close this gap with a medical perturbation audit: a 30-operator battery edits both the chain and the question with clinically motivated operators (severity reversal, negation flip, demographic swap, evidence ablation), paired with a chain-update times answer-flip joint analysis that classifies each model by its failure mode. Applied to 14 LLMs on four medical QA benchmarks, three independent tests converge: the Chain-Decoupling Rate (CDR; chain does not register the edit and the answer does not flip) is 72.9% panel-wide on clinically meaningful destructive edits, chain corruption leaves accuracy unchanged, and removing CoT prompting does not reduce accuracy. Two board-certified clinicians re-annotate N=197 perturbed questions; 98.5% leave the gold defensible. The pattern holds across medical and reasoning fine-tuning and scale; on the closed-source tier, where the chain text is unavailable, the answer-side signals are consistent with the same decoupling. Our framework and CDR provide a reusable yardstick for auditing whether medical CoT is faithful or merely documentation.

</details>


### [110] [CAFE: Self-Improving Search Agents Need Co-Evolving Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24794)
*Boyang Liu,Senjie Jin,Peixin Wang,Zhangyue Yin,Yibo Wang,Yuhao Zhou,Xinbing Liang,Shizheng Zhu,Yuhui Wang,Jingqi Tong,Zhiheng Xi,Jiazheng Zhang,Clive Bai,Clarenceai,Blaze Chen,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAFE couples agent and critic evolution through feedback-conditioned recovery and online/offline optimization, improving search agent performance and reducing hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outcome-supervised search agents suffer from delayed error localization and compounding mistakes, while corrective feedback requires joint optimization of when/how to request feedback and how to infer useful corrections from confounded rollouts.

Method: Shared-parameter model alternates between search-agent and critic roles with feedback-conditioned recovery, comparative feedback shaping via call-skip success gaps, feedback-aware advantage shaping for online RL, and rollout-derived preference optimization for offline learning.

Result: Outperforms RL-based search agents on 7 benchmarks, retains gains across 6 out-of-domain benchmarks, reduces hallucinations, and shows co-evolution prevents performance plateaus seen in one-sided improvements.

Conclusion: Self-improving search agents require feedback mechanisms that co-evolve with the policies they guide, with coupled agent-critic optimization enabling better error correction and performance.

Abstract: Outcome-supervised search agents learn when and how to retrieve evidence, but terminal rewards neither localize intermediate errors nor redirect an ongoing trajectory before those errors compound. Treating corrective feedback as a learned in-trajectory intervention couples the two roles: the agent must decide when to request and use feedback, while the critic must infer useful corrections from outcome-confounded rollouts whose failure patterns shift as the agent improves. We introduce CAFE (Coupled Agent--Feedback Evolution), a framework in which a shared-parameter model alternates between search-agent and critic roles. CAFE initializes feedback-conditioned recovery from trajectories built around the base agent's own failures, then couples online and offline optimization. During online RL, a comparative feedback estimate uses a prompt-level call--skip success gap to shape request returns, while feedback-aware advantage shaping reweights token advantages before and after feedback. Offline, rollout-derived preference optimization learns feedback from matched successful and unsuccessful trajectories. On seven agentic search benchmarks, CAFE outperforms the evaluated RL-based search agents on average, retains its gains across all six out-of-domain benchmarks, and reduces answer-level hallucinations. One-sided ablations show that improving only the agent or only the critic eventually plateaus, whereas alternating the two updates continues to improve performance. These findings suggest that a self-improving search agent needs feedback that co-evolves with the policy it guides.

</details>


### [111] [StarHarness: Evolving Harnesses with Stratified Search for Enterprise Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24804)
*Esakkivel Esakkiraja,Denis Akhiyarov,Vikas Yadav,Sai Rajeswar,Patrice Bechard,Sridhar Nemala,Sagar Davasam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StarHarness evolves environment-specific agent harnesses (prompts, tools, structure) while keeping model weights fixed, improving performance by 20-35% across enterprise domains with transferable gains across model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address persistent model-environment mismatch in tool-rich enterprise tasks, where pre-trained models struggle with specific environment conventions, interfaces, and operational knowledge needed for effective task execution.

Method: Framework for evolving agent harnesses through strategic task stratification: creates compact evolution pool by categorizing tasks based on baseline failure behavior, separates proposer-visible search tasks from hidden selection tasks, uses held-out tasks for generalization evaluation.

Result: 20-35 percentage point improvement in full-benchmark performance across ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance after 4-12 accepted harness changes per environment. Gains persist on excluded tasks and transfer across GPT and Qwen model families without re-evolution.

Conclusion: StarHarness offers a practical approach to reduce persistent model-environment mismatch by evolving environment-specific agent harnesses, achieving significant performance improvements through interface repairs, environment convention learning, and operational knowledge compression.

Abstract: We present StarHarness, a framework for evolving environment-specific agent harnesses while keeping model weights fixed. The evolved harness can include prompt and task framing, tool interfaces, skills, MCP-backed providers, subagent structure, and agent-loop configuration. StarHarness constructs a compact evolution pool by stratifying tasks according to baseline failure behavior, separates proposer-visible search tasks from proposer-hidden selection tasks, and reserves held-out tasks for evaluating generalization. Across ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance, harness evolution improves full-benchmark performance by 20-35 percentage points over the default harness after 4-12 accepted changes per environment. These gains persist on tasks excluded from evolution and transfer without re-evolution across GPT and Qwen model families. Trace analysis links the improvements to interface repairs, environment conventions, and operational knowledge that compresses search, with fewer false-positive diagnoses and shorter trajectories in several settings. StarHarness therefore offers a practical way to reduce persistent model-environment mismatch in tool-rich enterprise tasks.

</details>


### [112] [Strictly Causal Streaming Video Anomaly Detection with a Theoretically-Grounded State-Space Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24810)
*Yogesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A strictly causal streaming video anomaly detector using Mamba-style SSMs with O(1) time/memory per frame, no buffering, validated on edge hardware with real-time performance (1300+ FPS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video anomaly detection methods using SSMs still use clip buffering, lack theoretical understanding of temporal memory effects on latency, and benchmark only GPU throughput rather than edge hardware performance.

Method: Strictly causal streaming detector with fixed-size state updated in O(1) per frame; uses diagonal linear state space recurrence with input/state dependent decay gate, trained self-supervised through causal next embedding prediction on frozen visual backbone.

Result: Achieves 0.74-0.77 ms per frame (1300+ FPS) on Apple M3 Pro; 67.9% AUC on Ped2 and 70.2% on Avenue; theoretical decay analysis shows event boundary gate (not base decay) governs responsiveness; gate effectiveness dataset-size dependent.

Conclusion: Demonstrates practical edge deployment with real-time performance, but accuracy gap exists versus non-causal baselines; immediate next steps include closing accuracy gap and extending to larger benchmarks.

Abstract: Recent work has applied Mamba style state space models (SSMs) to video anomaly detection, yet existing approaches still rely on buffering clips or windows internally, lack a theoretical account of how temporal memory relates to detection latency, and benchmark efficiency only through GPU throughput rather than the edge hardware these methods are intended to target. We introduce a strictly causal streaming anomaly detector whose fixed size state is updated in O(1) time and memory per incoming frame, with no lookahead and no clip buffering. Its temporal core is a diagonal linear state space recurrence with an input and state dependent decay gate, trained self supervised through causal next embedding prediction on a frozen visual backbone. We derive a closed form relationship between the recurrence decay spectrum and both detection delay and the shortest anomaly it can reliably capture, then validate empirically on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue. The settling delay bound predicted from the learned base decay (57 to 59 frames) sits far above the measured detection delay (1.6 and 18.4 frames), showing that the event boundary gate, not the base decay, governs responsiveness. We further report end to end latency and throughput measured directly on Apple M3 Pro hardware, 0.74 ms and 0.77 ms per frame (over 1300 FPS), rather than simulated GPU numbers. With an untuned initial configuration the method reaches 67.9 percent and 70.2 percent frame level AUC on Ped2 and Avenue, trailing prior non causal SSM baselines in accuracy. Ablations over decay rate, state size, and gating reveal that the gate contribution is dataset size dependent, hurting accuracy on the smaller Ped2 training set but helping on the larger Avenue one. Closing this accuracy gap and extending evaluation to a third, larger benchmark are immediate next steps.

</details>


### [113] [Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge for LLM-Based Knowledge Graph QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24824)
*Emanuel Kitzelmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A KGQA approach that verifies LLM-generated candidate answers using lightweight symbolic constraints with three-valued semantics, improving precision without sacrificing recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based KGQA methods have limitations: semantic parsing to SPARQL is brittle with complex schemas, while LLM reasoning lacks formal guarantees. Need a complementary approach that balances robustness with some formal grounding.

Method: Introduces Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK): LLM generates candidate answers, then verifies them using lightweight symbolic constraints (type, relation, exclusion) with three-valued semantics (satisfied, violated, unknown) to handle incomplete KGs.

Result: Precision improves by filtering invalid candidates while recall is preserved (unknown constraints don't cause rejection). Satisfied constraints provide additional positive evidence for ranking. Demonstrated on Hetionet biomedical KG.

Conclusion: The approach offers a pragmatic middle ground between brittle semantic parsing and ungrounded LLM reasoning, providing formal verification without requiring complete logical forms.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used for knowledge graph question answering (KGQA), but can fail to correctly ground answers in the underlying graph. Current approaches to LLM-based KGQA either rely on full semantic parsing into executable queries such as SPARQL, which is brittle in practice due to complex schemas or incompleteness of real-world KGs, or on LLM-reasoning and answer generation over KGs, which can be more robust but lacks formal guarantees. In this work, we study a complementary setting in which \emph{candidate} answers are generated by an LLM-based system and subsequently verified using lightweight symbolic constraints derived from the question. We introduce \emph{Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK)}, a problem formulation that focuses on eliminating invalid answers and providing symbolic support for valid ones without requiring construction of executable logical forms. To account for incomplete KGs, we employ a three-valued constraint semantics (\emph{satisfied, violated, unknown}) that avoids incorrect rejections under open-world assumptions. To demonstrate the effects of our method, we instantiate this framework over the Hetionet biomedical knowledge graph and evaluate the impact of type, relation, and exclusion constraints. Experiments show that precision improves by filtering invalid candidates, while recall is preserved due to retaining candidates whose constraints are not explicitly violated. Satisfied constraints provide additional positive symbolic evidence to rank remaining candidates.

</details>


### [114] [A Dual-Dimensional LLM Framework for Automated Item Incidental Content Similarity Analysis in Large-Scale Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24825)
*Jing Huang,Jihong Zhang,Hua-Hua Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A dual-dimensional LLM-powered framework (AISA) measures item similarity in assessments by combining structural decomposition and semantic relatedness, outperforming traditional metrics and improving adaptive testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing concerns about incidental content redundancy in large-scale assessments and automatic item generation, where construct-irrelevant elements like wording or context become unintentionally repetitive across items. Traditional similarity metrics fail to capture nuanced structural and semantic layers that drive perceived redundancy.

Method: Proposes a dual-dimensional framework for Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), operationalizing similarity through Structured Decomposition and Semantic Relatedness. Includes psychometric validation and application in Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT) simulations with LLM-based similarity constraints.

Result: LLM-derived metrics align more closely with construct-irrelevant local dependence indicators and yield more coherent item parameter groupings than traditional text-based measures. CAT simulations show LLM-based similarity constraints improve estimation stability, reduce bias with minimal efficiency trade-offs, outperforming constraints based on conventional metrics.

Conclusion: LLM-powered AISA has strong potential to support scalable bank curation, content-aware test assembly, and experience-sensitive adaptive testing across diverse assessment contexts by better capturing nuanced similarity dimensions that traditional metrics miss.

Abstract: The rapid expansion of large-scale assessments and the growing adoption of automatic item generation have intensified concerns about incidental content redundancy, where construct-irrelevant elements such as wording or contextual framing become unintentionally repetitive across items. Traditional similarity metrics like BLEU or cosine similarity, often fail to capture the nuanced structural and semantic layers that drive perceived redundancy simultaneously. This study proposes a dual-dimensional framework for Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), operationalizing similarity through Structured Decomposition and Semantic Relatedness. Psychometric validation indicates that LLM-derived metrics align more closely with indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and yield more coherent item parameter groupings than traditional text-based measures. The framework is further evaluated through its application in Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT). Simulations reveal that incorporating LLM-based similarity constraints into item selection improves estimation stability and reduces bias with minimal efficiency trade-offs, outperforming constraints based on conventional metrics. These findings highlight the potential of LLM-powered AISA to support scalable bank curation, content-aware test assembly, and experience-sensitive adaptive testing across diverse assessment contexts.

</details>


### [115] [FedV-KGQA: Multi-Hop Question Answering over Vertically Partitioned Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24846)
*Md Saikat Islam Khan Bappy,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedV-KGQA enables multi-hop reasoning over vertically partitioned knowledge graphs across organizations without sharing raw data, using local graph enrichment and embeddings while maintaining data sovereignty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge graph question answering data is often distributed across organizations due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. Existing centralized systems cannot answer multi-hop questions when required facts are split across vertically partitioned silos where organizations share entities but own disjoint relations.

Method: Combines local graph enrichment and knowledge graph embeddings to keep raw triples and relation parameters within each silo. Introduces topic entity anchoring mechanism to ground questions in correct graph neighborhood without runtime inter-silo communication.

Result: Evaluated 12 model configurations across three benchmarks. FedV-KGQA performs strongly, remains close to centralized performance, generalizes to 3-hop reasoning, and is robust to embedding perturbations.

Conclusion: FedV-KGQA provides an effective framework for multi-hop reasoning over distributed knowledge graphs while maintaining data sovereignty through structural data boundaries and eliminating need for centralized graph access.

Abstract: Real-world data for knowledge graph question answering is often distributed across different organizations due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. While centralized systems exist, they cannot answer multi-hop questions when the required facts are split across vertically partitioned silos. In this paper, we propose FedV-KGQA, a framework for multi-hop reasoning over knowledge graphs in which organizations share entities but own disjoint sets of relations. Our approach combines local graph enrichment and knowledge graph embeddings to ensure raw triples and relation parameters never leave each silo, establishing a structural data boundary without requiring centralized graph access. We further introduce a topic entity anchoring mechanism that grounds questions in the correct graph neighborhood without any runtime inter-silo communication. We evaluate 12 model configurations across three benchmarks and show that FedV-KGQA performs strongly, remains close to centralized performance, generalizes to 3-hop reasoning, and is robust to embedding perturbations.

</details>


### [116] [SPO++: Stream-Aligned Policy Optimization for Asynchronous Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24870)
*Kai Ruan,Jinghao Lin,Qianshan Wei,Ziqi Zhou,Zihe Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPO++ improves on Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) by fixing token-weighted advantage normalization and reorganizing prompt evidence, enhancing online learning efficiency for tool-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group-relative reinforcement learning requires waiting for sibling rollouts of the same prompt, which is costly for long and variable tool-use trajectories. SPO tries to address this but has limitations in how it handles advantage normalization and prompt evidence organization.

Method: SPO++ fixes two issues: 1) It standardizes terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure (fixing the token-weighted quantity mismatch), and 2) organizes prompt evidence by the policy event that generated it rather than learner receipt order.

Result: Across matched runs on ALFWorld at two model scales and on Math-TIR, SPO++ improves online learning efficiency over SPO. A paired ablation identifies action-token-measure normalization as the strongest tested component.

Conclusion: SPO++ successfully addresses limitations in SPO by properly handling token-weighted advantage normalization and evidence organization, leading to more efficient online learning for reinforcement learning in tool-use scenarios with long and variable trajectories.

Abstract: Group-relative reinforcement learning waits for sibling rollouts of the same prompt, which is costly for long and variable tool-use trajectories. Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) removes this dependency with a persistent prompt-level value estimate, but its recipe whitens one advantage per trajectory before optimizing a token-mean actor loss. We show that trajectory centering generally does not center the token-weighted quantity consumed by the actor, and fix the mismatch by standardizing terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure. We additionally organize prompt evidence by the policy event that generated it rather than learner receipt order. Across matched runs on ALFWorld at two model scales and on Math-TIR, SPO++ improves online learning efficiency over SPO. A paired ablation identifies action-token-measure normalization as the strongest tested component.

</details>


### [117] [Recursive Experiential-Working Memory Evolution for Long-Horizon Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24876)
*Zhaochen Yu,Yingcheng Wu,Zhenfei Yin,Kaiyuan Chen,Zhe Zhao,Mengdi Wang,Shuicheng Yan,Ling Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Recuris is a recursive memory architecture that enables agents to improve long-horizon task performance through structured memory evolution and localized skill updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is challenging in long-horizon tasks because growing histories obscure task states and lead to misaligned skill invocation. Traditional approaches struggle to maintain context and adapt skills effectively over extended sequences of actions.

Method: Introduces Recuris, a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture. Working Memory tracks task progress and guides skill selection from Experiential Memory, grounding skill use in current needs. A fixed Meta-Agent uses execution evidence to create localized, validation-gated updates to Skill Memory, forming a bounded recursive memory-evolution loop.

Result: Across four long-horizon benchmarks and ten models, Recuris improved task success in 35 of 37 model-benchmark pairs. Achieved significant improvements: +17.8 points for GPT-5.6 Sol, +15.6 for Claude Opus 5 (reaching 87.9%), and +16.6/+13.5 points for Qwen models on SkillFlow. The advantage widens with longer interaction horizons (up to +32.2 points) and reduces common long-horizon failures by up to 80%.

Conclusion: Recursively evolving memory provides a scalable foundation for RSI, enabling agents to continuously transform accumulated experience into increasingly effective long-horizon behavior, addressing the core challenges of context management and skill alignment in extended tasks.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) remains hard in long-horizon tasks, where growing histories obscure the task state and misalign skill invocation. We introduce Recuris, a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture for long-horizon agent harnesses, in which Working Memory tracks task progress and guides skill selection from Experiential Memory, grounding skill use in current needs rather than the full history. This coupling also turns execution into structured evidence that localizes failures to specific memory components. Across tasks, a fixed Meta-Agent turns that evidence into localized, validation-gated updates to Skill Memory that reshape execution and yield new evidence, forming a bounded recursive memory-evolution loop. Across four long-horizon benchmarks and ten models, Recuris improves task success in 35 of the 37 completed model-benchmark pairs, carrying frontier models to SOTA-level task success: on tau-bench it adds +17.8 points to GPT-5.6 Sol and +15.6 to Claude Opus 5, taking Opus 5 to 87.9%, and +16.6/+13.5 points on Qwen3.6-27B/35B on SkillFlow. The advantage widens as the interaction horizon grows, to +32.2 points on the longest tasks, and common long-horizon failures fall by up to 80%. These results position recursively evolving memory as a scalable foundation for RSI, enabling agents to continuously transform accumulated experience into increasingly effective long-horizon behavior. Code: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/Recuris

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [118] [Equivariant Cellular Sheaves for Molecular Electronic Structure: Bridging Sheaf Cohomology and E(3)-Equivariant Hamiltonian Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23571)
*Krishna Harish*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that molecular Hamiltonians can be represented as Laplacians of cellular sheaves, enabling topological invariants (cohomology) to capture non-bonding orbitals and delocalization, while generalizing equivariant message-passing networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a structural understanding of molecular Hamiltonians through sheaf theory, connecting topological deep learning with electronic structure prediction, and capturing non-bonding orbitals and delocalization patterns as topological invariants.

Method: Represent the molecular single-particle Hamiltonian (after positive shift) as Laplacian of cellular sheaf on regular cell complex. Use O(3)-steerable two-center kernels for restriction maps, recovering Slater-Koster form. Derive Equivariant Cellular Sheaf Networks that generalize E(3)-equivariant message passing.

Result: Exact Hamiltonian-to-sheaf embedding (machine precision), cohomology dimension reproduces non-bonding orbital counts across 11 conjugated molecules, sheaf Laplacian is O(3)-equivariant (machine precision), model achieves lower error and better rotation generalization on directional electronic targets.

Conclusion: The sheaf-theoretic formalization provides structural understanding of molecular Hamiltonians, connects topological invariants to electronic properties, and generalizes existing equivariant networks with anti-oversmoothing properties through non-trivial sheaf diffusion.

Abstract: Equivariant message-passing networks are the standard model for molecular property and interatomic-potential prediction, and recent work predicts the electronic Hamiltonian itself in an E(3)-equivariant way. Separately, topological deep learning has extended graph networks to cellular sheaves. Our central observation is structural: in a localized atomic-orbital basis, the molecular single-particle Hamiltonian, after a constant shift that makes it positive semidefinite, is the Laplacian of a cellular sheaf on a regular cell complex built from the molecule. Making the restriction maps O(3)-steerable two-center kernels from bond geometry recovers the Slater-Koster form as a special case and yields an E(3)- and permutation-equivariant operator. Three consequences follow. First, the zeroth sheaf cohomology H^0 = ker L is a topological invariant equal to the non-bonding (zero-mode) orbitals, recovering the classical alternant non-bonding-orbital count as a lower bound. Second, the Hodge 1-Laplacian lets higher cells (rings) carry cycle and delocalization information through H^1. Third, the model strictly generalizes E(3)-equivariant message-passing networks and CW networks, and inherits the anti-oversmoothing of non-trivial sheaf diffusion. We prove equivariance, expressivity, and cohomological-correspondence results for the Equivariant Cellular Sheaf Networks, and validate them numerically: the Hamiltonian-to-sheaf embedding is exact to machine precision, the cohomology dimension reproduces non-bonding-orbital counts across eleven conjugated molecules, the sheaf Laplacian is O(3)-equivariant to machine precision, and the equivariant model attains lower error and rotation generalization on a directional electronic target. Our contribution is this sheaf-theoretic formalization and its invariants, not equivariant Hamiltonian prediction itself.

</details>


### [119] [Data Predictability Shapes Weibull Weight-Scale Growth in Transformer Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23573)
*Tiexin Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformer weight growth follows a predictive law based on pre-training data statistics (bigram conditional entropy), enabling forward prediction of weight magnitudes from data properties before training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what corpus properties determine how much transformer weights grow during training, specifically finding a training-free predictive relationship between data statistics and final weight magnitudes.

Method: Analyze transformer weight distributions (Weibull with k≈1.2), use bigram conditional entropy D as pre-training statistic, test across controlled corruption families, establish learning-rate-conditioned growth law, and validate with held-out data.

Result: Discovered law: λ² - λ₀² = C₀(η) + C₁(η)(Hᵣ - D)⁰·⁵⁹ where D is pre-training bigram entropy, with 23 runs collapsing to unit slope (R²=0.941). Forward prediction achieves 5.7% relative error on held-out data.

Conclusion: Transformer weight growth is predictable from pre-training data statistics via a simple power-law relationship, suggesting redundancy as a second axis in broader data-to-weight mapping framework Φ(D,R,A,H).

Abstract: A trained transformer's weight magnitudes can be summarized by a two-parameter Weibull distribution whose shape $k \approx 1.2$ is stable across layers and models, so the scale $λ$ carries most training-induced movement. What corpus property sets how much $λ$ grows? Using the bigram conditional entropy $D = H(\text{next} \mid \text{prev})$, a training-free statistic computed before training, we find across controlled corruption families a learning-rate-conditioned law, $λ^2 - λ_0^2 = C_0(η) + C_1(η)(H_r - D)^{0.59}$, where $H_r$ is a matched-budget shuffle baseline. The convex exponent is inherited from an independently measured data-side saturation relation rather than fitted directly to the growth curve. After removing the two per-$η$ coefficients, 23 runs spanning an order of magnitude in learning rate collapse onto $(H_r - D)^{0.59}$ with unit slope ($R^2 = 0.941$; direct per-$η$ fits are weaker, $R^2 \approx 0.82$). Because $D$ is computed before training, the law is a forward predictor: an end-to-end self-validation recovers held-out within-family weight growth with 5.7% relative error. The readout holds at model and per-layer resolutions and across two tested architectures, with the functional form preserved and only the coefficients changing. It also marks its boundary: cross-corpus prediction over-predicts code, implicating redundancy as a second axis of a broader $Φ(D,R,A,H)$ data-to-weight framework.

</details>


### [120] [From Causal Plausibility to Causal Reliability: Evaluating LLMs as Calibrated Direct Causal-Edge Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23660)
*Amit Kumar,Elnur Adl Zarabi,Suranjana Trivedy,Zhiqian Chen,Lei Zhang,Kaiqun Fu,Taoran Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs are recall-dominant in causal discovery, producing overly dense graphs with many false positives and substantial overconfidence. Traditional confidence metrics are unreliable, but cross-prompt/cross-model agreement offers better calibration. LLMs capture causal relatedness without reliably identifying directness or orientation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for providing prior causal knowledge in structural causal discovery, but there's uncertainty about whether their direct-edge judgments and confidence levels can be trusted, requiring systematic evaluation of their reliability.

Method: Systematically evaluated 12 instruction-tuned open-weight models across six benchmark causal graphs, using five prompting strategies and four confidence sources: verbalized, logit-based, cross-prompt agreement, and cross-model agreement under a language-only pairwise protocol.

Result: Three key findings: 1) LLM-based judgments are recall-dominant, producing overly dense graphs; 2) LLMs capture causal relatedness but poorly identify directness/orientation, misclassifying indirect/reversed edges as direct; 3) Traditional confidence estimates are unreliable while agreement-based metrics offer better calibration, though advantages are not statistically significant after correction.

Conclusion: LLMs should be viewed as sources of externally validated soft causal priors rather than direct evidence of causal structure, due to their recall-dominant behavior, overconfidence in incorrect predictions, and unreliable conventional confidence estimates.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to provide prior causal knowledge for structural causal discovery, yet whether their direct-edge judgments and confidence can be trusted remains unclear. We systematically evaluate 12 instruction-tuned open-weight models across six benchmark causal graphs, five prompting strategies, and four confidence sources: verbalized, logit-based, cross-prompt agreement, and cross-model agreement. Under our language-only pairwise protocol, our evaluation yields three key findings. (i) LLM-based causal judgments are strongly recall-dominant: models predict overly dense graphs with many false-positive edges, while prompting mainly shifts the precision-recall trade-off rather than resolving overprediction. Gains from model scale diminish on the largest graphs and do not eliminate miscalibration. (ii) LLMs often capture causal relatedness without reliably identifying directness or orientation. Relative to published reference graphs, models misclassify 40.0% of indirect and 36.0% of reversed non-edges as direct edges, versus 28.2% of other non-edges. Moreover, 80.8% and 84.6% of these false positives receive verbalized confidence of at least 80%, revealing substantial overconfidence in structurally incorrect predictions. (iii) Conventional confidence estimates are unreliable, whereas agreement offers a more promising signal. Logit-based confidence frequently collapses near 1.0 regardless of correctness, while cross-prompt and cross-model agreement achieve better mean calibration and discrimination, though their advantages are not statistically significant after Holm correction. A benchmark-familiarity audit further identifies potential familiarity in five model-dataset pairs, all involving AsiaM. Overall, our results suggest LLMs are better viewed as sources of externally validated soft causal priors than as direct evidence of causal structure.

</details>


### [121] [Renormalization Group Flow Matching for Scalable Local Generative Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23696)
*Kanta Masuki,Yuto Ashida*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RGFM uses renormalization group flow as probability paths for scalable generative modeling, enabling local computation while capturing long-range correlations that conventional local methods miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative models face a fundamental tradeoff: global approaches capture structural coherence but are computationally expensive, while local models are efficient but fail to reproduce long-range correlations. The authors aim to bridge this gap using renormalization group principles to achieve both scalability and global coherence.

Method: Renormalization Group Flow Matching (RGFM) uses exact RG flow as probability paths, progressively generating data from long- to short-wavelength structures. It exploits RG's quasi-locality and scale separation properties to approximate the probability flow with local velocity fields acting over spatial ranges scaling as O(Λ⁻¹[ln L + ln(1/ε)]), enabling local computation with patch sizes O(ln L) and nearly linear computational cost scaling.

Result: RGFM reproduces long-range correlations beyond its receptive field in 1D distributions, while conventional local flow matching shows substantial errors at long distances. On FFHQ images at 64x64 and 256x256 resolutions, RGFM yields more coherent and higher-quality samples than local flow matching. Theoretical analysis shows RGFM probability flow can be accurately approximated by local fields with logarithmic patch sizes.

Conclusion: RG-guided probability flows offer a promising path toward scalable generative modeling that captures long-range structure using only local computation, solving the fundamental tradeoff between global coherence and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Despite their remarkable success in modeling complex data, generative models face a fundamental tradeoff. Global approaches can capture full structural coherence but suffer from high computational costs, while local models are efficient but often fail to reproduce long-range correlations and global coherence. The renormalization group (RG) bridges this gap by seamlessly connecting spatial structures across different length scales, retaining quasi-local descriptions at each step while preserving long-range correlations. We introduce renormalization group flow matching (RGFM), a generative framework that systematically structures data generation across different spatial scales. By using an exact RG flow as the probability path, RGFM progressively generates data from long- to short-wavelength structures. To reconcile scalability with global structure, we exploit two key properties of the RG: quasi-locality and scale separation. We rigorously show that the RGFM probability flow can be accurately approximated by local velocity fields acting over a spatial range $O(Λ^{-1}[\ln L+\ln(1/\varepsilon)])$ for RG wavenumber scale $Λ$, linear system size $L$, and prescribed error tolerance $\varepsilon$. This property enables local generative modeling with patches of size $O(\ln L)$ and a computational cost that scales nearly linearly with the system volume. We numerically demonstrate that local RGFM reproduces long-range correlations far beyond its receptive field in representative one-dimensional distributions, while conventional local flow matching exhibits substantial errors at long distances. On FFHQ images, RGFM yields far more coherent and higher-quality samples than local flow matching at 64x64 and 256x256. Our results establish RG-guided probability flows as a promising route toward scalable generative modeling that captures long-range structure using only local computation.

</details>


### [122] [Response Renormalization for Critical Deep Equilibrium Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23725)
*Jose Luis Lima de Jesus Silva*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep Equilibrium Models (DEQs) suffer from unstable gradients when residual Jacobians are near-singular. Response Renormalization stabilizes gradients by lifting near-pole denominators in critical subspaces without damping well-conditioned sensitivities, making optimization more reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DEQs use implicit differentiation for training, but when the residual Jacobian is nearly singular along loss-sensitive directions, small perturbations get strongly amplified, producing large, highly sensitive gradients that make optimization unreliable and unstable.

Method: Introduced Response Renormalization framework with two variants: 1) Collective Mode Response Renormalization (CMR) applies corrections in low-dimensional critical subspaces, and 2) Phi-adaptive CMR computes bounded response mass from positive susceptibility rules. Methods handle dense and matrix-free formulations, distinguish exact gradients from backward-response surrogates, and extend to Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors (SILVA).

Result: Tested across 23 multiphysics families (PDEs, 3D fields, operator maps, complex geometries, particle systems). CMR and Phi-CMR achieve test errors within 5% of models trained with exact implicit differentiation in over 98% of static and 95% of transient family-seed comparisons. Solver-index experiments show convergence toward static adjoint, physical-time rollouts retain predictive fidelity.

Conclusion: Selective response renormalization effectively controls near-critical adjoint amplification without globally damping well-conditioned sensitivity. This makes parameter updates more reliable while preserving useful gradient information needed for learning, providing a practical solution to gradient instability in DEQs.

Abstract: Deep Equilibrium Models (DEQs) compute predictions from a hidden representation unchanged by the model update. Training through this equilibrium uses implicit differentiation and requires solving an adjoint system built from the residual Jacobian. If this Jacobian is nearly singular along loss-sensitive directions, small perturbations can be strongly amplified in the adjoint response, producing large, highly sensitive gradients that can make optimization unreliable. We introduce Response Renormalization, a backward-pass framework that lifts selected near-pole denominators while leaving unlifted response channels unchanged. Collective Mode Response Renormalization (CMR) applies this correction in a low-dimensional critical subspace, while Phi-adaptive CMR computes a bounded response mass from a positive susceptibility rule. We derive dense and matrix-free collective formulations, distinguish exact gradients of a modified frozen-anchor residual from backward-response surrogates, and extend the construction to Structured Implicit Layers and Vector Attractors (SILVA). Across 23 multiphysics families spanning partial differential equations, three-dimensional fields, operator maps, complex geometries, and particle systems, CMR and Phi-CMR yield test errors no more than five percent higher than those from models trained with exact implicit differentiation in more than 98% of static and 95% of transient family-seed comparisons. Solver-index experiments show convergence toward the static adjoint, while physical-time rollouts retain predictive fidelity under the evaluated conditions. These results demonstrate that selective response renormalization can control near-critical adjoint amplification without globally damping well-conditioned sensitivity. Therefore, the method can make parameter updates more reliable while preserving the useful gradient information needed for learning.

</details>


### [123] [Calibration-Preserving Pruning: Compression as a Reliability Contract](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23744)
*Ibne Farabi Shihab,Adria Binte Habib,Anuj Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPP (Calibration-Preserving Pruning) improves efficiency of conformal prediction sets by preserving score geometry during pruning, achieving smaller valid prediction sets with competitive accuracy at high sparsity levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While split conformal prediction provides finite-sample marginal coverage for pruned models, there's a separate efficiency problem: can pruning preserve score geometry well enough to obtain smaller valid prediction sets? The paper aims to address this efficiency gap in reliability-sensitive classification.

Method: CPP augments base pruning scores (like SparseGPT or Wanda) with nonconformity-gradient saliency, using disjoint splits for pruning, validation-selection, conformal-calibration, and testing. It ensures bounded conformal-quantile shifts through controlled score perturbations and maintains coverage while improving set sizes.

Result: At 50% sparsity, CPP-SparseGPT reduces mean set size from 10.1 to 8.6 on DBpedia-14 while increasing accuracy from 0.347 to 0.366. Across 15 dataset-sparsity combinations, CPP-SparseGPT produces smaller sets in 13 cases and higher accuracy in 11. The best threshold-aware candidate-label CPP achieves 7.8 mean set size with explicit accuracy/compute trade-offs.

Conclusion: CPP successfully preserves calibration during pruning, enabling smaller conformal prediction sets without sacrificing coverage guarantees. While much of the gain comes from generic supervised gradients, CPP shows practical benefits for reliability-sensitive classification, with transfer evidence to RoBERTa-base and Llama-3-8B models.

Abstract: Split conformal prediction, not the pruning rule, supplies finite-sample marginal coverage once a pruned model is fixed independently of the conformal calibration split. We study the separate efficiency problem: can pruning preserve score geometry well enough to obtain smaller valid prediction sets? Calibration-Preserving Pruning (CPP) augments a base pruning score with nonconformity-gradient saliency and uses disjoint pruning, validation-selection, conformal-calibration, and test splits. Bounded score perturbations imply bounded conformal-quantile shifts and controlled set inflation, but do not make the generic coverage theorem CPP-specific.
