Final five-seed Qwen2.5-1.5B results at 50\% sparsity show the largest gains on large-label tasks. On DBpedia-14, CPP-SparseGPT reduces mean set size from \(10.1\) to \(8.6\) while changing accuracy from \(0.347\) to \(0.366\); CPP-Wanda reduces \(11.2\) to \(9.0\) with an accuracy trade-off from \(0.310\) to \(0.295\). Across 15 dataset--sparsity cells, CPP-SparseGPT produces smaller sets in 13 and higher accuracy in 11. Matched controls show that generic supervised gradients explain much of the gain: true-label CPP is not statistically resolved from matched Wanda+SNIP, whereas threshold-aware candidate-label CPP reaches \(7.8\) mean set size at explicit accuracy and offline-compute costs. RoBERTa-base and Llama-3-8B diagnostics support transfer, but our claims remain limited to reliability-sensitive classification.

</details>


### [124] [Tight Majorizations and Convergence Rates of Nuclear Norm Minimization IRLS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23765)
*Christian Kümmerle,Tomas Masak,Dominik Stöger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IRLS methods for nuclear norm minimization achieve sharp convergence rates with harmonic-mean weight operators, outperforming traditional one-sided reweighting schemes through optimal global quadratic majorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While IRLS methods are natural for nuclear norm minimization, their convergence rates and the role of weight operators remain poorly understood. Existing one-sided reweighting schemes have limitations, and a theoretical understanding of why certain weight operators perform better is needed.

Method: Developed a majorization analysis for smoothed nuclear norm, proving harmonic-mean weight operator defines a valid global quadratic majorizer. Showed optimality of harmonic-mean within power-mean weight family. Analyzed convergence under Schatten-1 null space property for various weight operators including harmonic-mean weights.

Result: Proved global linear convergence of IRLS algorithms with various weight operators. For harmonic-mean weights, established dimension-independent locally linear convergence rate. Provided counterexample showing one-sided weight operators cannot achieve this dimension-independent rate. Numerical experiments confirm advantages across various problem settings.

Conclusion: Harmonic-mean reweighting provides superior theoretical convergence properties and practical performance over traditional one-sided reweighting schemes for nuclear norm minimization in low-rank recovery, with proven dimension-independent convergence rates.

Abstract: Iteratively reweighted least squares (IRLS) methods constitute a natural approach to nuclear norm minimization, but their convergence rates and the role of the weight operator have remained poorly understood. This paper establishes sharp convergence rates for IRLS methods for constrained nuclear norm minimization in low-rank recovery. A central ingredient is a new majorization analysis for the smoothed nuclear norm: we prove that the harmonic-mean weight operator defines a valid global quadratic majorizer. Furthermore, we show that this weight operator is optimal within the family of power-mean weights, clarifying why it improves over classical one-sided reweighting schemes that use only row- or column-space information. Under a Schatten-1 null space property, we prove global linear convergence of IRLS algorithms using a variety of weight operators, including the harmonic-mean weights. For IRLS with harmonic-mean weights, we prove a dimension-independent, locally linear convergence rate. We provide a counterexample showing that this dimension-independent local rate cannot in general be obtained for IRLS algorithms using one-sided weight operators, which predominate in the literature. Numerical experiments corroborate the theoretical results and illustrate the practical advantage of harmonic-mean reweighting across square, rectangular, and adversarially initialized recovery problems.

</details>


### [125] [Disentangled Skill Representations for Predictive Human Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23776)
*Mariah Schrum,Deepak Gopinath,Srijan Srivatsa,Guy Rosman,Tiffany Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAIL is a method that models human skill as interpretable, multi-dimensional constructs from naturalistic behavior, creating robust skill embeddings that transfer across tasks and improve AI coaching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding human skill is crucial for AI systems that need to collaborate with, coach, or assist people, but skill differs from typical latent variable problems as it's persistent, compositional, and must be inferred from behavioral patterns over time.

Method: SAIL introduces skill embeddings that are robust to transient performance fluctuations by representing individuals with persistent skill embeddings controlling a blend between expert and novice bases, trained using counterfactual subskill swaps for disentanglement to encourage interpretable representations.

Result: Across racing and baseball domains, SAIL achieves strong predictive performance, improves behaviorally grounded disentanglement over baselines, and enhances downstream AI coaching performance.

Conclusion: SAIL successfully models human skill as interpretable, multi-dimensional constructs that are robust to performance variation and transferable across contexts, providing a foundation for more effective AI collaboration, coaching, and assistance systems.

Abstract: Understanding human skill is important for AI systems that collaborate with, coach, or assist people. Unlike typical latent variable estimation problems which rely on single observations, skill is a persistent, compositional, and behaviorally grounded construct that must be inferred from patterns over time. We introduce Skill Abstraction with Interpretable Latents (SAIL), a method for modeling human skill as an interpretable, multi-dimensional construct inferred from naturalistic behavior. Our approach produces a skill embedding that is robust to transient performance fluctuations and learns a transferable representation of human subskills. Furthermore, SAIL supports skill-informed behavior prediction that generalizes across a variety of in-domain contexts. We represent each individual with a persistent skill embedding that controls a blend between expert and novice bases and is trained using counterfactual subskill swaps for disentanglement. This design encourages representations that are both robust to performance variation and structured for interpretability. We demonstrate across racing and baseball that SAIL achieves strong predictive performance and consistently improves behaviorally grounded disentanglement over the evaluated baselines, while also improving downstream AI coaching performance.

</details>


### [126] [GAP-Prompt: Gated Adaptive Prompting for Efficient Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23782)
*Trung-Anh Dang,Duy-Cuong Bui,Ngoc-Son Vu,Christel Vrain,Vincent Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAP-Prompt introduces instance-level adaptive prompting for continual learning, using gated mechanisms and dynamic fusion to achieve state-of-the-art performance while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current prompt-based continual learning methods rely on static, task-level prompting strategies that overlook fine-grained intra-task diversity, limiting their ability to handle instance-level variations effectively.

Method: GAP-Prompt consists of three modules: 1) instance-conditioned gating for dynamic prompt injection layer selection per image, 2) dynamic knowledge fusion for instance-aware aggregation of current/historical prompts, and 3) shared prompt distillation to anchor foundational knowledge in early layers.

Result: Extensive evaluations show GAP-Prompt achieves state-of-the-art performance on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and CUB-200 benchmarks, with 87.29% accuracy on fine-grained CUB-200 (approaching 88% joint training upper bound).

Conclusion: GAP-Prompt effectively addresses catastrophic forgetting in continual learning through instance-level adaptive prompting, demonstrating superior performance across diverse benchmarks while maintaining knowledge integration across tasks.

Abstract: Continual learning faces the persistent challenge of catastrophic forgetting, where sequential task updates degrade previously acquired knowledge. While prompt-based methods integrated with pre-trained models offer a compelling solution by freezing the backbone, they often rely on static, task-level prompting strategies that overlook fine-grained intra-task diversity. In this paper, we propose Gated Adaptive Prompting (GAP-Prompt), a novel method that introduces instance-level adaptability to the prompting process. GAP-Prompt consists of three synergistic modules: (1) instance-conditioned gating, which dynamically determines optimal prompt injection layers for each individual image; (2) dynamic knowledge fusion, which performs instance-aware aggregation of current and historical prompts, enabling knowledge integration across tasks; and (3) shared prompt distillation, which anchors foundational knowledge in early shared layers to mitigate forgetting. Extensive evaluations on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and CUB-200 benchmarks demonstrate that GAP-Prompt consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Notably, on the fine-grained CUB-200 dataset, GAP-Prompt reaches 87.29% accuracy, approaching the joint training upper bound (88.00%) and outperforming existing methods by a significant margin.

</details>


### [127] [Mixture of Channel Experts: Static Sparse Supports with Input-Adaptive Mixing for Pointwise Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23794)
*Elian Iluk,Gil Ben-Artzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoCE is a new channel-mixing layer for CNNs inspired by MoE, using sparse channel selection instead of parallel experts to reduce computational costs while maintaining or improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) designs fail in convolutional networks because parallel convolutional experts reading the same input channels learn similar filters. The authors aim to adapt the MoE concept to CNNs while avoiding this redundancy and reducing computational costs.

Method: MoCE moves the expert axis from operator duplication to channel selection. Each expert is a single output channel with learned sparse support of k << C input channels. A softmax with input-predicted temperature combines selected channels, allowing each expert to shift between mean-like and max-like aggregation. A residual expert summarizes unselected channels, and a load-balancing loss ensures complete channel coverage.

Result: MoCE replaces dense projections with quadratic cost in C with a mechanism scaling as k/C, achieving measured wall-clock time savings. It reduces MACs by 16.7% and end-to-end latency while matching or exceeding dense baselines and prior channel-selection methods across ResNet backbones on ImageNet-1K, CIFAR-100, transfer learning, EfficientViT, and modern training recipes.

Conclusion: MoCE successfully adapts the Mixture-of-Experts concept to convolutional networks through structured sparse channel selection, achieving computational efficiency gains while maintaining or improving model performance across various vision tasks and architectures.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) scales language models by routing each input through a small set of independently parameterized experts. We show that copying this design into convolutional networks fails for a structural reason: parallel convolutional experts that read the same input channels learn nearly identical filters. We therefore move the expert axis from operator duplication to channel selection. We introduce Mixture of Channel Experts (MoCE), a structured sparse channel-mixing layer, inspired by MoE, that replaces pointwise (1x1) channel-reduction projections. In MoCE, an expert is a single output channel with a learned sparse support of k << C input channels. The selected channels are combined by a softmax whose temperature is predicted per input, so each expert can move between mean-like and max-like aggregation. A residual expert summarizes the unselected channels, and a load-balancing loss keeps channel coverage complete. MoCE replaces a dense projection whose cost is quadratic in C with a mechanism whose relative cost scales as k/C, and the predicted savings hold in measured wall-clock time. Across ResNet backbones on ImageNet-1K and CIFAR-100, transfer learning, EfficientViT, and a strong modern training recipe, MoCE matches or exceeds dense baselines and prior channel-selection methods while reducing MACs by 16.7% and end-to-end latency.

</details>


### [128] [A Theory of Speciation in Generative Diffusion Models on Compact Riemannian Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23798)
*Alessio Marta,Paola Causin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops an intrinsic geometric theory of speciation in diffusion models on Riemannian manifolds, showing speciation corresponds to critical point bifurcations rather than pitchfork bifurcations, with topology imposing global constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theoretical descriptions of speciation in diffusion models typically assume pitchfork bifurcations and large-dimensional spaces, which limits understanding of speciation on general manifolds. The paper aims to develop a more fundamental intrinsic theory that accounts for manifold geometry.

Method: Uses spectral heat-kernel representation to relate speciation to critical point bifurcations of evolving probability density. Applies Poincaré-Hopf and Morse theory for global constraints, analyzes mixtures of heat kernels to characterize generic speciation events as A2 fold normal forms, and develops geometry-dependent estimates of speciation times.

Result: Shows generic speciation events have one-dimensional critical kernel and admit A2 fold normal form (pitchforks arise only in symmetric configurations). Proves structural stability of nondegenerate folds under score perturbations, where first-order time shift depends only on score error along critical direction. Demonstrates theory on sphere using von Mises-Fisher distributions and neural network score-learning.

Conclusion: Speciation in diffusion models is fundamentally characterized by critical point bifurcations rather than pitchfork bifurcations, with manifold topology imposing strict global constraints on possible branching patterns, providing a rigorous geometric foundation for understanding speciation phenomena.

Abstract: Speciation in generative diffusion models denotes the emergence of distinct stable branches during denoising, through which initially undifferentiated trajectories progressively commit to different data classes. In this work we develop an intrinsic theory of speciation for diffusion models supported on compact Riemannian manifolds: the aim is to go beyond existing theoretical descriptions, which usually identify speciation with a symmetric pitchfork bifurcation and assume to work in a large-dimensional space. We characterize speciation by bifurcations of the critical points of the evolving probability density. A spectral heat-kernel representation makes explicit the role of the manifold geometry, while Poincaré-Hopf and Morse theory impose global constraints on the number and type of score equilibria and reveal topologically-imposed geometrical modes. For mixtures of heat kernels, we prove that generic speciation events have a one-dimensional critical kernel and admit an A2 fold normal form; pitchforks and simultaneous multidirectional transitions arise from nongeneric symmetric configurations. We derive geometry-dependent estimates of speciation times for bimodal mixtures and Riemannian regular simplices. We further establish structural stability of nondegenerate folds under score perturbations and show that the first-order time shift is determined solely by the component of the score error along the critical direction. The theory is illustrated on the sphere using mixtures of von Mises-Fisher distributions, where pitchfork and saddle-node bifurcations, topological modes, and hierarchical multiple speciations are observed. Finally, a chart-based intrinsic score-learning scheme based on neural networks contrasts the theoretically predicted transitions on prototypal and more complex datasets.

</details>


### [129] [Discovering Cross-Language Reasoning Invariance in LLMs with Geometry-Invariant Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23809)
*Igor Bogdanov,Changcheng Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether multilingual language models solve math problems using shared cross-language features or language-specific computations, finding that geometric similarity of features doesn't guarantee functional interchangeability and that cross-language feature sharing varies by model architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It remains unclear whether multilingual language models solve the same mathematical problems in different languages using shared features or language-specific computations that only produce similar outputs.

Method: Researchers used the Multilingual Grade School Math (MGSM) dataset across 6 languages, identified cross-language aligned layers using Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA), then trained two types of sparse autoencoders: baseline reconstruction-only SAE and a novel Geometry-Invariant SAE (GI-SAE) with InfoNCE loss to produce similar activations for the same problem across languages.

Result: GI-SAE yields higher geometric similarity metrics (CKA and Jaccard) but doesn't consistently improve functional interchangeability. Cross-language feature sharing is model- and architecture-dependent, with GI-SAE primarily amplifying existing patterns rather than creating new shared structure.

Conclusion: Higher geometric similarity of features doesn't guarantee functional interchangeability, and cross-language feature sharing varies significantly by model architecture, appearing at different depths in different models.

Abstract: Multilingual language models can solve the same mathematical problem in different languages, but it remains unclear whether they rely on shared features or on language-specific computations that only produce similar outputs. We study this question in five models from four families using the Multilingual Grade School Math (MGSM) dataset, with problems solved in English, German, French, Spanish, Russian, and Chinese, retaining problems with valid reasoning traces in all six languages and replaying those traces through the model to record representations at multiple layers. For each model, we first use Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) to identify layers with cross-language alignment. At each selected layer, we train two sparse autoencoders (SAE): a baseline reconstruction-only model and a contrastive variant introduced in this work, the Geometry-Invariant SAE (GI-SAE). GI-SAE supplements the reconstruction loss with an Information Noise-Contrastive Estimation (InfoNCE) loss that trains the encoder to produce similar activations for traces of the same problem, regardless of language or token position. We then test whether the resulting shared features are functionally interchangeable by swapping their values between languages during the model's forward pass and measuring the resulting change in output, quantified by Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence per feature. Although GI-SAE yields higher CKA and Jaccard similarity at nearly every layer, higher geometric similarity does not consistently imply greater functional interchangeability. We find that cross-language feature sharing is model- and architecture-dependent in this sample and appears at different depths in different models. GI-SAE primarily amplifies cross-language structure already present: the pattern is model-specific, with strengthening in Qwen, no functional benefit in Gemma, and mixed layer-dependent effects in Llama and Phi.

</details>


### [130] [Learning to Grade Efficiently: A Bandit-Driven Prompt-Selection Framework for Low-Cost LLM Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23814)
*Olga Manakina,Igor Bogdanov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cost-aware multi-armed bandit approach adaptively selects optimal prompting strategies for automated essay scoring, reducing LLM calls by 78.4% while maintaining accuracy comparable to exhaustive search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based automated essay scoring (AES) approaches use fixed prompts, which don't address operational cost concerns or adapt to evolving optimal configurations. There's a need to balance assessment validity with operational costs in educational technology platforms.

Method: Treats each prompt type as an arm in a multi-armed bandit (MAB) controller for adaptive selection of optimal prompting strategies during inference. Implemented four distinct grading recipes: multi-step vs. single-step assessment, with vs. without calibration examples. Tracked token usage and latency alongside agreement metrics.

Result: MAB framework achieves comparable scoring accuracy to exhaustive grid search while reducing LLM calls by 78.4% to find the best grading approach. Multi-step approach with calibration examples achieves highest accuracy. Produced first cost-reliability learning curves for essay scoring.

Conclusion: This represents the first application of online control mechanisms to adaptively select prompting strategies in AES, transforming prompt selection from offline hyperparameter optimization into efficient online learning. Provides actionable insights for balancing operational costs against assessment validity.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate strong capabilities in automated essay scoring (AES), but contemporary approaches typically employ fixed prompt selection, failing to address operational cost concerns and evolving optimal configurations. We propose a novel cost-aware approach that treats each prompt type as an arm in a multi-armed bandit (MAB) controller, enabling adaptive selection of optimal prompting strategies during inference. Our experiments on IELTS Writing Task 2 essays show that the MAB framework achieves comparable scoring accuracy to exhaustive grid search while reducing LLM calls by 78.4\% to find the best grading approach. We implemented four distinct grading recipes (multi-step vs. single-step assessment, with vs. without calibration examples) and found that the multi-step approach with examples achieves the highest accuracy. By tracking token usage and latency alongside agreement metrics, we produce the first cost-reliability learning curves for essay scoring, providing actionable insights for educational technology platforms that must balance operational costs against assessment validity. This work represents the first application of online control mechanisms to adaptively select prompting strategies in AES, transforming prompt selection from an offline hyperparameter optimization problem into an efficient online learning task.

</details>


### [131] [AQLoRA: A Zero-Search Recipe for Fast Quantized LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23816)
*Md Romyull Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AQLoRA is an adaptive-quantization method that speeds up QLoRA fine-tuning by keeping high-error layers in fp16 to skip dequantization, achieving 4.8-11.1% faster training with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QLoRA saves memory but not time because it dequantizes 4-bit weights during training, making it slower than fp16 LoRA. There's a need for a method that recovers some of the training speed while maintaining accuracy.

Method: AQLoRA uses a single CPU pass to rank layers by NF4 reconstruction error, keeps the top-K high-error layers in fp16 within memory budget (skipping dequantization), with quality and speed settings. Quality setting adapts every layer; speed setting adapts only top blocks (early backward pass stopping).

Result: Speed setting: 11.1 ± 2.7% faster than QLoRA with ~1% accuracy drop. Quality setting: 4.8 ± 2.4% faster, accuracy equal to QLoRA and within 1% of fp16 LoRA, using only 0.2 GiB more memory. Method validated across 6 models (1.4B-14B) on Commonsense-170K.

Conclusion: AQLoRA provides significant speed improvements over QLoRA with minimal accuracy/memory tradeoffs. The paper also establishes three important benchmarking rules: fix measurement duration, measure noise floor from duplicated arms, and repeat whole sessions for proper uncertainty estimation.

Abstract: Quantized fine-tuning (QLoRA) saves memory but not time. It dequantizes every 4-bit weight on the fly, so it trains more slowly than fp16 LoRA. We present AQLoRA (Adaptive-Quantization LoRA), a recipe that buys part of that time back. One CPU pass over the weights sets everything, with no search and no calibration data. The pass ranks layers by NF4 reconstruction error and keeps the top-K in fp16 under a memory budget. Those layers skip dequantization, which is where the speed comes from. A quality setting adapts every layer. A speed setting adapts only the top blocks, so the backward pass stops early. The rule reproduces Unsloth's hand-curated dynamic-4bit selection exactly, in seconds, where search-based allocation needs repeated calibration passes.
  We evaluate on Commonsense-170K across six models and four architecture families, from 1.4B to 14B. The speed setting trains 11.1 +/- 2.7% faster than well-tuned QLoRA and gives up about one accuracy point. It was faster in all nine independent timing sessions, at worst by 7%. The quality setting trains 4.8 +/- 2.4% faster. Its accuracy is level with QLoRA on every model and within a point of fp16 LoRA, for 0.2 GiB more memory. These error bars are measured between independent sessions, not within one. Earning them taught us three rules for timing on shared hardware. Fix the measurement duration, not the step count. Measure the noise floor from a duplicated arm, not a nearly identical method. Repeat whole sessions: a floor computed inside one sweep understates the real uncertainty several times over, and the random seed controls almost none of it.
  We validate the recipe with controls and report the two that failed. Choosing adapter layers by weight density is no better than random. Choosing protected layers by quantization error is not either. The count of protected layers, not their identity, carries the speed effect.

</details>


### [132] [Generating Intervention Hypotheses using Explainable Explanations on Graphs: G2I, a Two-Stage Greedy Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23835)
*Mulin Tian,Ajitesh Srivastava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Simple greedy search methods can effectively generate actionable network-level counterfactual explanations for GNNs, outperforming complex mask-based methods in efficiency and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world decision-making in public health/social science needs explainable models, but current GNN explanation methods operate at node level and aren't suitable for network-level intervention design. Mask-based counterfactual explainers assume feasible edge manipulation, may target immutable attributes, are computationally expensive, and are difficult to explain to domain specialists.

Method: 1) Reframe counterfactual explanation as intervention design problem. 2) Local level: Use greedy search to find minimal, actionable changes to node features/neighbor conditions with theoretical guarantees. 3) Convert counterfactuals to interpretable rules. 4) Network level: Formulate intervention selection as DNF coverage problem under budget constraints, leveraging greedy algorithm with theoretical guarantees due to approximately submodular properties.

Result: Experiments on synthetic graphs and real-world suicide risk networks show the approach produces scalable, cost-effective intervention strategies with significantly improved efficiency over mask-based counterfactual methods. Greedy search conditions are empirically shown to be approximately met.

Conclusion: Simple methods can generate good explanations, and the proposed greedy-based approach provides actionable, interpretable, and efficient counterfactual explanations suitable for real-world network-level intervention design in domains like public health.

Abstract: Real-world decision-making in public health and social science can greatly benefit from predictive models, yet translating predictions into effective interventions requires explaining the model behavior. While Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are well-suited for modeling relational data, existing explanation methods largely operate at the node level and fall short of supporting actionable, network-level intervention design. Existing counterfactual GNN explainers, such as CF-GNNExplainer and CF$^2$, rely on continuous mask optimization over features and edges, which implicitly assume feasible edge manipulation, may allocate effort to immutable or non-actionable attributes, and incur substantial computational overhead. Further, the method of arriving at the explanation itself is difficult to explain to a domain specialist who is not an AI expert. Can simple methods generate good explanations? To explore this, we reframe counterfactual explanation as an intervention design problem. At the local level, we generate counterfactuals via a greedy search that directly identifies minimal, actionable changes to node features and neighbor-level conditions. We derive conditions under which the greedy search provides guarantees, and empirically show that these conditions are approximately met. These counterfactuals are converted into interpretable rules suitable for real-world intervention. At the network level, we formulate intervention selection as a Disjunctive Normal Form (DNF) coverage problem under a budget constraint, which is nondecreasing and approximately submodular, enabling a greedy algorithm with theoretical guarantees. Experiments on synthetic graphs and real-world suicide risk networks demonstrate that our approach produces scalable, cost-effective intervention strategies with significantly improved efficiency over mask-based counterfactual methods.

</details>


### [133] [PuzzleKV: Page-Wise Low-Rank Decomposition for KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23843)
*Zizhong Wang,Jieying Wang,Zhao Zhang,Jiajia Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PuzzleKV is a training-free KV cache compression method that partitions cache into pages, applies per-page low-rank compression, and processes attention over mixed dense/factorized representations to significantly reduce memory while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in LLMs is limited by memory requirements for KV cache storage. Existing low-rank compression methods use fixed projection spaces or shared bases that may miss detailed important information, creating a need for more adaptive compression.

Method: Partitions per-head KV cache into fixed-length logical pages, observes low-rank structure within pages, treats each completed page as independent compression unit. Decomposes pages within each layer and KV head, computes attention directly over dense and factorized pages, and incrementally compresses newly eligible pages during autoregressive decoding.

Result: At ~60% of original KV cache storage, achieves >96% of Full KV performance across models and benchmarks. Shows substantial gains over Global SVD on RULER and competitive on LongBench. Combined with quantization achieves >93% performance using only 18.7% of original storage.

Conclusion: PuzzleKV provides effective training-free KV cache compression by leveraging per-page low-rank structure, enabling substantial memory savings with minimal performance loss, and can be combined with quantization for aggressive compression ratios while maintaining high accuracy.

Abstract: Long-context inference in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly limited by the memory required for the key-value (KV) cache. KV cache compression addresses this problem by reducing the storage cost of previous tokens. Among existing approaches, low-rank compression is particularly attractive because it represents every token in reduced dimensions. Previous low-rank methods typically derive fixed projection spaces from model weights, construct fixed spaces from calibration activations, or construct a shared basis over a broad cache region. Such representations may not capture detailed but important information. We partition each per-head KV cache into fixed-length logical pages and observe substantial low-rank structure within individual pages. Based on this observation, we propose PuzzleKV, a training- and calibration-free method that treats each completed page as an independent compression unit. PuzzleKV decomposes pages within each layer and KV head, computes attention directly over dense and factorized pages, and incrementally compresses newly eligible pages during autoregressive decoding. Experiments across models, context lengths, and benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of PuzzleKV under matched storage budgets. At approximately 60% of the original KV cache storage, PuzzleKV achieves more than 96% of Full KV performance across both evaluated models and all benchmark settings, with substantial gains over Global SVD on RULER and competitive performance on LongBench. To achieve a more aggressive compression ratio, PuzzleKV can be further combined with quantization while retaining more than 93% of Full KV performance using only 18.7% of the original storage.

</details>


### [134] [FlowNeg: GFlowNet-Guided Diverse Hard Negative Sampling for Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23849)
*Ibne Farabi Shihab,Naoshin Anzum Hridi,Joyanta Jyoti Mondal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowNeg introduces a hierarchical generative flow network for knowledge graph embedding negative sampling that combines model-based hardness with structural similarity scoring to generate diverse, informative negatives while avoiding collisions with held-out positives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current negative sampling approaches for knowledge graph embeddings face trade-offs: uniform sampling produces diverse but easy negatives, while hard-negative mining focuses on too few entities and often collides with held-out positive triples, limiting model performance.

Method: FlowNeg uses a context-conditioned hierarchical generative flow network that amortizes reward-proportional sampling. It performs two-stage selection: given a positive triple and corruption side, it first selects a type, then an entity within that type. The reward combines bounded model-based hardness with a training-only structural score to avoid collisions with held-out positives over relation-specific type-compatible supports.

Result: FlowNeg achieves higher mean MRR than EMU and IF-NS in 24 out of 25 cells across five architectures and benchmarks (+0.0172 and +0.0160 on average). In controlled experiments on FB15k-237/RotatE with fixed negative count and compute, FlowNeg achieves 0.359±0.001 MRR vs 0.346±0.002 for EMU, with near-uniform diversity, high gradient informativeness, and low collision.

Conclusion: The approach successfully demonstrates mode-covering negative generation that balances diversity and hardness without treating structural similarity as ground truth, providing an effective solution to the negative sampling problem in knowledge graph embeddings.

Abstract: Negative sampling determines whether a knowledge graph embedding (KGE) model learns from informative counterexamples or wastes updates on implausible corruptions. Uniform negatives are diverse but easy, whereas hard-negative miners concentrate on few entities and collide more with held-out positives. We introduce FlowNeg, a context-conditioned hierarchical generative flow network that amortizes reward-proportional sampling without normalizing a composite reward over the entity set: given a positive triple and corruption side, it selects a type, then an entity. Its terminal reward combines bounded model-based hardness with a training-only structural score for held-out-positive collision, over a relation-specific type-compatible support. We derive the reward, specialize standard trajectory balance, and bound multiplicatively how residual imbalance perturbs terminal and mode probability. Across a descriptive five-seed grid of five architectures and five benchmarks, FlowNeg has higher mean MRR than EMU and than IF-NS in 24 of 25 cells ($+0.0172$ and $+0.0160$ on average). A separate 15-seed FB15k-237/RotatE control fixing negative count, diagnostic budget, and compute gives FlowNeg $0.359\pm0.001$ MRR against $0.346\pm0.002$ for EMU, with near-uniform fixed-partition diversity, high gradient informativeness, and low collision. The evidence supports mode-covering negative generation without treating structural similarity as an open-world truth oracle.

</details>


### [135] [UHI-Bench: Benchmarking Dual-Source Urban Heat Island Modeling Across Cities in Diverse Climate Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23857)
*Wanyun Ling,Chenxi Liu,Yi Xie,Aopu Xu,Zhuoqi Zeng,Ziyue Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UHI-Bench is the first benchmark for dual-source urban heat island modeling, evaluating 20+ models across 5 tasks in 20 cities across 9 climate zones, showing no uniformly best model but foundation models perform consistently well.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban heat islands are intensifying but current studies rely on single sources (land surface temperature or air temperature), leading to biases in heat exposure assessment. Cloud gaps in LST data and sparse AirT stations limit dual-source modeling, necessitating cross-city transfer across diverse climates.

Method: Introduces UHI-Bench benchmark with unified signal-mechanism-transfer framework integrating dynamic meteorological drivers and static urban morphology features. Evaluates over 20 baselines from four model families on five tasks across 20 cities and nine Köppen climate classes.

Result: No model is uniformly best across all tasks, although foundation models remain consistently competitive and stable. Environmental covariates generally improve performance but utility varies across sources/tasks. Cross-city transferability is better explained by overlap in UHI regimes than climate-zone similarity.

Conclusion: UHI-Bench provides practical guidance for urban heat modeling, promotes climate data equity through standardized pipeline, and supports future advances in climate research by addressing spatiotemporal incompatibilities in UHI modeling.

Abstract: Urban heat islands (UHIs) are intensifying under climate change, exacerbating thermal exposure risks. Their two primary observations, land surface temperature UHI (LST-UHI) and near-surface air temperature UHI (AirT-UHI), capture physically distinct aspects of urban heat. However, most studies rely on a single source, and substituting one for the other can substantially bias the magnitude and spatial variability of human heat exposure. Accurate UHI modeling also requires dynamic meteorological drivers and static urban morphology features, but spatiotemporal incompatibilities hinder their alignment. Cloud gaps in LST observations and sparse AirT station networks further limit dual-source UHI modeling, motivating cross-city transfer across diverse climates. To bridge these gaps, we introduce UHI-Bench, the first UHI benchmark for dual-source UHI modeling that integrates dynamic and static environmental context. Following a unified signal, mechanism, and transfer framework, it evaluates over 20 baselines from four model families on five tasks across 20 cities and nine Köppen climate classes. Results show that no model is uniformly best, although foundation models remain consistently competitive and stable. Environmental covariates generally improve performance, but their utility varies across sources and tasks. Cross-city transferability is better explained by overlap in UHI regimes than by climate-zone similarity. With the dataset and standardized pipeline, our work provides practical guidance for urban heat modeling, promotes climate data equity, and supports future advances in climate research.

</details>


### [136] [Revelation Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23860)
*Qinyou Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Revelation Control - a framework for optimally pricing information reveals in learning systems based on decision necessity rather than raw information value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning systems need to manage when to reveal hidden states, but current approaches don't distinguish between information that affects decisions vs. information that's merely useful for other purposes. There's a need to optimize intervention costs against decision value.

Method: Develops decision theory framework with concepts of decision-sufficient revelation and revelation depth, separates pure information value from productive reuse, creates cost-adjusted factorization criterion for shallow coordinate revelation, and designs target-independent protocols for model instantiation.

Result: Deeper future-learning probes show positive decision value across Qwen2.5-7B and Mistral-7B-v0.3 models. Productive reuse provides utility advantages. Qwen reveals decision-nonredundant shallow regimes, while Mistral shows scalar sufficiency. Both support structural transfer of the theoretical framework.

Conclusion: Revelation Control provides a principled framework for managing information reveals in learning systems, with structural theory being transferable across systems while empirical specifics remain model-dependent.

Abstract: Revelation Control is the problem of choosing priced interventions that reveal hidden state only insofar as the revealed distinctions can change a consequential decision, while accounting separately for any useful progress created by the intervention itself. We develop this theory for learning systems, where states equivalent under declared current information can respond differently to future training and favor different actions. The framework defines decision-sufficient revelation and revelation depth, separates pure information value from productive reuse, embeds static Bayes refinement into state-dependent continuation value, and gives an exact cost-adjusted factorization criterion: an additional shallow coordinate is decision-nonredundant only when states sharing a scalar summary lie on opposite sides of the priced Stop/Continue boundary. We also give a target-independent protocol for model-specific instantiation and prove that bounded stop-flip risk alone cannot certify positive expected utility under unrestricted severity. Across Qwen2.5-7B and Mistral-7B-v0.3, deeper future-learning probes have positive decision value and productive reuse yields strict equal-compute utility advantages. Qwen additionally provides evidence for a decision-nonredundant shallow revealability regime; in Mistral, a scalar continuation architecture fit only on an independent development panel retains positive familywise-adjusted lower bounds on a disjoint target panel, consistent with scalar decision sufficiency within the tested architecture family and resolution. The evidence supports structural rather than numerical transfer: the decision theory, cost accounting, continuation logic, and evaluation protocol transport, while empirical proxies, coefficients, thresholds, and even the required shallow state dimension may be system-specific.

</details>


### [137] [Every Layer Counts: An Exponential $L_2$ Depth Hierarchy for ReLU Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23877)
*Itay Safran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReLU neural networks show exponential neuron savings with each additional layer; depth hierarchies prove that deeper networks require exponentially fewer neurons than shallower networks for equivalent functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding depth hierarchies in neural networks: whether deeper architectures provide real computational advantages over shallower ones, and quantifying the benefits of depth for ReLU networks. The paper addresses open questions about exponential separations between fixed adjacent depths.

Method: The paper proves theoretical lower bounds using mathematical analysis and construction of specific functions. For depth ℓ≥3, they construct functions realizable by width O(d⁴) depth-ℓ networks, while proving that depth-(ℓ-1) networks with width ≤ 2^d/[2d(ℓ-2)] have squared L₂ error ≥1/24. They also construct explicit benign functions requiring exponential neurons in depth-3 networks but computable by polynomial-width depth-4 networks.

Result: 1) Exponential separation between any fixed adjacent depths ℓ and ℓ-1 for ℓ≥3. 2) First exponential hierarchy for all adjacent fixed depths in ReLU networks. 3) Compact depth-3 vs depth-2 separation answering Safran et al. (2019) question. 4) Exact separation for a benign function computable by polynomial-width depth-4 network but requiring exponential neurons in depth-3 networks.

Conclusion: ReLU networks exhibit strong depth hierarchies where each additional layer provides exponential neuron savings, establishing fundamental advantages of depth in neural network architectures. However, the constructions avoid regular regimes that would imply major threshold-circuit lower bounds, highlighting interesting theoretical boundaries.

Abstract: We prove a depth hierarchy for ReLU neural networks in which every additional ReLU layer can save exponentially many neurons. For every $\ell\geq 3$, a globally $[0,1]$-valued, $1$-Lipschitz function is realized by a depth-$\ell$ network of width $\mathcal{O}(d^4)$, whereas every depth-$(\ell-1)$ network with unrestricted weights and width at most $2^d/[2d(\ell-2)]$ has squared $L_2$ error at least $1/24$ under an absolutely continuous distribution. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first exponential separation for ReLU networks between two fixed depths whose shallower depth is at least $3$, and the first exponential hierarchy across all adjacent fixed depths. The lower bound also immediately yields the corresponding hierarchy for exact computation. Moreover, the case $\ell=3$ gives a compactly supported depth-$3$-versus-depth-$2$ separation with unrestricted shallow-network weights, answering a question raised by Safran, Eldan, and Shamir (2019, Sec. 2.3). The corresponding distribution nevertheless has all its mass at exponential radius, so the construction falls outside the regularity regime in which such a separation would imply major threshold-circuit lower bounds. We also prove an exact separation for a more benign function. It is computed by a polynomial-width depth-$4$ network, whereas every depth-$3$ network agreeing with it on the unit hypercube requires exponentially many neurons in its first hidden layer, again without any restriction on the weights. The function is globally $[0,1]$-valued and $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt d)$-Lipschitz, and maps the unit hypercube onto $[0,1]$.

</details>


### [138] [Partial Optimal Transport on the Circle for All Transported Masses in O(N log N)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23910)
*Soheil Kolouri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAWC: An O(N log N) algorithm for exact partial optimal transport on circles that's N times faster than previous methods, with applications to periodic data and robust matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Partial optimal transport on circles is important for periodic data (angles, phases, time, hue), but existing methods are inefficient with O(N² log N) complexity, limiting practical applications.

Method: PAWC algorithm exploits that circular structure maintains line-like properties without cuts, using a free-gap invariant to provide optimal cuts where all previous updates remain valid as line updates.

Result: Exact O(N log N) time and O(N) memory algorithm returns complete cost profile, nested active sets, and plans in one run with optimal cut for all cardinalities; extends to spheres via slicing.

Conclusion: PAWC provides exact, efficient partial optimal transport on circles with significant speedups (0.56ms vs 1.5s), better robustness to outliers/occlusion (66% vs 16% retrieval score), and improved spherical fitting.

Abstract: Partial optimal transport compares two measures while leaving part of the mass unmatched, which is what makes it robust to outliers, occlusion, and clutter. The quantity of interest is usually the whole profile - the optimal cost at every transported cardinality - because the right amount to transport is rarely known in advance, and on the real line the PAWL algorithm returns that profile in $O(N\log N)$. Much data is periodic rather than linear: angles, phases, orientations, time of day, hue, and every direction obtained by projecting onto a great circle. On the circle the same problem acquires a global circulation, or equivalently an optimized cut, which the naive exact method handles by running the line algorithm once per support gap, at $O(N^{2}\log N)$. We show that this factor $N$ is unnecessary. The line structure survives in cut-free form, and a free-gap invariant supplies, at every step, a cut at which all previous local updates remain valid line updates. This yields PAWC: an exact $O(N\log N)$ time, $O(N)$ memory algorithm returning all $K+1$ costs, nested active sets and plans in one run, together with a single gap that is simultaneously optimal for every cardinality. Slicing over great circles extends it to $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$. Empirically the whole profile costs $0.56$ms at $N=4096$ against $1.5$s for a single transported fraction from a general solver; on occluded, cluttered mpeg-7 shapes, holding the descriptor fixed and varying only the cost, it retains $66\%$ of the clean-data retrieval score against $16\%$ for balanced circular OT, and on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ it halves the fitting error of spherical sliced Wasserstein against contaminated targets, synthetic and real. Code is available at https://github.com/mint-vu/Partial_Wasserstein_on_Circles.

</details>


### [139] [The Loss Floor of Denoising Score Matching: Fisher Geometry from Schrödinger Bridges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23916)
*Avinash Raju,Kai Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diffusion model training with denoising score matching has an irreducible excess loss floor equal to the integrated trace of the Fisher-Rao metric, revealing information geometry as intrinsic to training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Denoising score matching trains models by regressing onto conditional scores, but generation requires marginal scores. While both share the same population minimizer, the conditional target introduces irreducible excess training loss that needs to be understood.

Method: Analyzes the excess loss through a Schrödinger bridge variational principle, showing it equals the integrated trace of the Fisher-Rao metric of the conditional endpoint family. Derives exact conditional-variance decomposition and connects to information geometry in diffusion latent spaces.

Result: The irreducible loss floor exactly equals the trace of Fisher-Rao metric integrated along diffusion trajectory. In Gaussian case, yields closed form for floor, recovers reparametrization invariance, and relates high-SNR divergence to data information dimension. Shows raw losses with different noise ranges aren't comparable due to different additive floors.

Conclusion: Information geometry observed in diffusion latent spaces is an intrinsic component of training loss, with Fisher term proportional to rate of mutual information loss. Training geometry differs from sampling error statistics, explaining inconsistent model rankings from differently-weighted losses.

Abstract: Denoising score matching trains diffusion models by regressing onto a conditional score, although generation ultimately requires the marginal score. The two objectives share the same population minimizer, but the conditional target remains random at fixed noisy state and introduces an irreducible excess in the training loss. We isolate this excess and show that, for a general corruption kernel under mild regularity assumptions, it is exactly the trace of the Fisher--Rao metric of the conditional endpoint family, integrated along the diffusion trajectory. This gives an exact conditional-variance decomposition of the denoising objective and identifies the information geometry observed in diffusion latent spaces as an intrinsic component of the training loss. We derive the result from a Schr"odinger bridge variational principle, in which the ideal objective arises as excess path-space relative entropy. For corruption diffusions, the Fisher term is proportional to the rate at which the noisy state loses mutual information about the clean data, separating the loss floor into an information flow determined by the data and a weight determined by the corruption schedule and objective. In the Gaussian case, this yields a closed form for the floor, recovers reparametrization invariance of the continuous-time objective, and relates its high-SNR divergence to the information dimension of the data. Finally, we show that raw losses obtained with different noise ranges or weightings need not rank models consistently because they contain different additive floors, and contrast the second-order geometry seen by training with the third-order conditional statistics entering numerical sampling error.

</details>


### [140] [GATNextHop: A GAT for Shortest Path Routing with Cross-Topology Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23917)
*Chia-Hong Chou,Katerina Potika*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph Attention Network (GAT) model called GATNextHop learns to approximate shortest paths and generalize across network topologies, offering potential scalability advantages over traditional Dijkstra's algorithm in dynamic/large networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional shortest-path algorithms like Dijkstra's (used in OSPF) must be recomputed for each network topology change, limiting scalability in dynamic or large-scale networks. There's a need for approaches that can approximate routing solutions and generalize across different network structures.

Method: Proposes GATNextHop model using Graph Attention Networks (GNN) to approximate shortest paths. Trained on synthetic graphs and evaluated on real-world Internet Service Provider networks from Internet Topology Zoo. Aims to learn routing heuristics that transfer across network structures.

Result: Performance evaluated in terms of accuracy, inference speed, and generalization. Compares GNN against Dijkstra's algorithm to quantify trade-offs between learned and classical routing approaches. Benchmarking aims to show model's ability to transfer learning across different network topologies.

Conclusion: The paper investigates whether Graph Attention Networks can effectively approximate shortest paths and generalize across network topologies, potentially offering a scalable alternative to traditional recompute-intensive routing algorithms in dynamic or large-scale networks.

Abstract: Common shortest-path algorithms, such as Dijkstra's (SPF), that OSPF uses, provide exact routing solutions but must be recomputed for each network topology, limiting scalability in dynamic or large-scale networks. This paper proposes the GATNextHop model to determine whether a Graph Neural Network, namely the Graph Attention Network, can approximate shortest paths and generalize across topologies. By training on synthetic graphs and evaluating on real-world Internet Service Provider networks from the Internet Topology Zoo, we aim to benchmark our model's ability to learn routing heuristics that transfer across network structures. Performance will be evaluated in terms of accuracy, inference speed, and generalization, comparing the GNN against Dijkstra's algorithm to quantify trade-offs between learned and classical routing approaches.

</details>


### [141] [MnemoDyn: Learning Resting State Dynamics from 40K FMRI sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23936)
*Sourav Pal,Viet Luong,Hoseok Lee,Tingting Dan,Guorong Wu,Richard Davidson,Won Hwa Kim,Vikas Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MnemoDyn is a dynamical-systems based model for rs-fMRI that outperforms transformer approaches through multi-resolution temporal modeling and efficient computation, showing strong generalization across diverse populations and protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing rs-fMRI models use transformer backbones, but the authors aim to develop a more compute-efficient approach that better captures the temporal dynamics of brain activity across parcellated regions. They seek to leverage large-scale non-proprietary rs-fMRI datasets to create a model that generalizes well and works even with small sample sizes common in neuroimaging studies.

Method: The model uses a dynamical-systems based approach with multi-resolution temporal modeling of dynamics across parcellated brain regions. It was trained on approximately 40K rs-fMRI sequences from diverse public and permission-based datasets, covering various populations and scanning protocols. The method focuses on temporal dynamics rather than transformer-based architectures.

Result: MnemoDyn demonstrates compute efficiency and superior generalization across diverse populations and scanning protocols. When benchmarked against current state-of-the-art transformer-based approaches, it consistently delivers superior reconstruction quality. The model shows strong performance on various downstream tasks and provides evidence of efficacy even with small sample sizes.

Conclusion: Large-scale pre-training on non-proprietary rs-fMRI datasets yields a highly performant model for downstream tasks. The dynamical-systems based approach with multi-resolution temporal modeling outperforms transformer-based methods and offers practical value for neuroimaging studies where small sample sizes are common and resting-state fMRI is frequently acquired.

Abstract: We present a dynamical-systems based model for resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI), trained on a dataset of roughly 40K rs-fMRI sequences covering a wide variety of public and available-by-permission datasets. While most existing proposals use transformer backbones, we utilize multi-resolution temporal modeling of the dynamics across parcellated brain regions. We show that MnemoDyn is compute efficient and generalizes very well across diverse populations and scanning protocols. When benchmarked against current state-of-the-art transformer-based approaches, MnemoDyn consistently delivers superior reconstruction quality. Overall, we find that with such large-scale pre-training on (non-proprietary) rs-fMRI datasets, we get a highly performant model for various downstream tasks. Our results also provide evidence of the efficacy of the model on small sample size studies which has implications for neuroimaging studies at large where resting state fMRI is a commonly acquired imaging modality.

</details>


### [142] [CoDrift: Compositional Drifting for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23939)
*Xiewei Ni,Ruofeng Mei,Xiangyu Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoDrift is a compositional framework for offline RL that combines multiple objectives (behavioral conditioning, action pooling, value guidance) as motion fields to train one-step generative policies, achieving state-of-the-art results on benchmark tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline RL is intrinsically multi-objective: policies must stay close to the behavioral distribution of the fixed dataset while selecting high-value actions. Existing methods struggle to effectively combine these heterogeneous objectives into a unified learning framework.

Method: The paper proposes CoDrift, which views each objective as an action-space motion field and combines them through field composition. It uses three key fields: 1) conditional field (preserves state-dependent behavioral structure), 2) marginal field (pools actions across states for stability), and 3) value field (moves actions toward higher-value regions). The composed field is absorbed into a stochastic generator that produces actions with single forward passes.

Result: CoDrift was evaluated on 73 tasks from OGBench and D4RL in both offline and offline-to-online settings. It compares favorably with state-of-the-art methods and achieves the best average rank in both settings.

Conclusion: The compositional motion field approach provides an effective framework for unifying heterogeneous learning objectives in offline RL, enabling one-step generative policies that balance behavioral support preservation with value optimization while maintaining deployment efficiency.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning is intrinsically multi-objective: a policy must remain compatible with the behavioral support of a fixed dataset while preferentially selecting high-value actions. We recast these objectives in a common form by viewing each as an action-space motion field that specifies how generated actions should move. This perspective enables heterogeneous learning objectives to be combined directly through field composition. Inspired by drifting models, we propose CoDrift, a compositional framework for one-step generative policy learning. CoDrift combines three objective-level fields into a unified policy field. The conditional field preserves state-dependent behavioral structure, while the marginal field pools actions across states to provide a more stable generative signal in the single-positive-sample regime of continuous-control offline RL. The value field moves generated actions toward higher-value regions. The composed field is absorbed into a stochastic generator that produces an action with a single forward pass at deployment. We evaluate CoDrift on 73 tasks from OGBench and D4RL in both offline and offline-to-online settings. CoDrift compares favorably with state-of-the-art methods and achieves the best average rank in both settings.

</details>


### [143] [Low-Latency Activation-Regularized Sparse Neural Operators with Distillation Assistance Towards Real-Time Edge-Deployable Virtual Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23987)
*William Howes,Farid Ahmed,Syed Bahauddin Alam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel Sparse-Activation-ReLU (SAR) layer for efficient neural operators enables 5x improvement in Latency-Error-Energy metric, with synthetic knowledge distillation and enhanced spiking neurons achieving further gains for virtual sensing applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Virtual sensing for digital twins needs real-time spatial-temporal physics reconstruction, but current methods struggle with generalization, latency, and energy efficiency for edge deployment. Neural operators are promising but power-intensive, while spiking neurons face training convergence and latency issues.

Method: Propose SAR layer - single-step alternative promoting activation sparsity without surrogate-gradient training, compatible with event-based computing. Implement within trunk-based NOMAD architecture, enhance with synthetic knowledge distillation, and improve Variable Spiking Neurons via ReLU-based spiking loss and graph-neighbor thresholding.

Result: SAR achieves over 5x improvement in Latency-Error-Energy metric vs VSN and LIF implementations. Synthetic knowledge distillation reduces LEE score by over 2x. On Heat Exchanger dataset, VSN improvements reduce L2 error by over 2x and nearly 7x respectively while reducing spiking and spatial aggregation.

Conclusion: This work advances energy-efficient virtual sensing with an alternative framework suitable for neuromorphic/edge integration, establishing a gold standard for comparing latency, energy, and error performance in sparsity/spiking-based efficient designs.

Abstract: Virtual sensing enables digital twins and safety-critical systems to reconstruct and forecast spatial-temporal physics in real time. However, conventional computational and data-driven methods often face challenges in generalization, latency, and energy efficiency for edge deployment. Neural operators offer a promising alternative but remain reliant on power-intensive hardware. Spiking neurons and neuromorphic computing can improve efficiency, yet surrogate-gradient training and multi-step spiking introduce convergence and latency challenges. We propose the Sparse-Activation-ReLU (SAR) layer, a single-step alternative that promotes activation sparsity without surrogate-gradient training while remaining compatible with event-based computing. Within a trunk-based NOMAD architecture, SAR achieves over a fivefold improvement in the combined Latency-Error-Energy (LEE) metric compared with Variable Spiking Neuron (VSN) and Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) implementations. We further analyze spiking entropy and feature usage and introduce synthetic knowledge distillation, reducing the LEE score by more than twofold. Finally, we improve VSN through a ReLU-based spiking loss and graph-neighbor thresholding. On the Heat Exchanger dataset, these approaches reduce L2 error by more than twofold and nearly sevenfold, respectively, while reducing spiking and spatial aggregation. Overall, the work presented is a step towards energy-efficient virtual sensing by providing an alternative framework that can be positioned towards neuromorphic or other edge device integration that can be a gold standard to compare latency, energy, and error performance for future efficient designs that are sparsity or brain-inspired spiking based.

</details>


### [144] [Revenge of Monosemanticity: Specialized Neurons Improve Data Efficiency in MLPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24007)
*Amirhesam Abedsoltan,Enric Boix-Adsera,Fivos Kalogiannis,Mikhail Belkin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MLPs develop monosemantic specialized neurons aligned with specific features relevant to particular input regions, rather than a single global low-dimensional representation, giving them data-efficiency advantages over global representation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how neural networks learn and organize features is central to understanding their behavior, but existing theory focusing on global low-dimensional predictive geometry is incomplete.

Method: Analysis of multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) in regression problems with clustered data, demonstrating how they naturally develop monosemantic specialized neurons aligned with specific predictive features for particular input regions.

Result: MLPs learn a collection of local low-dimensional representations that collectively span a high-dimensional space, with individual neurons becoming strongly aligned with specific features relevant to particular input regions.

Conclusion: This specialization gives MLPs a provable data-efficiency advantage over feature-learning methods based on a single global low-dimensional representation, challenging the conventional view of global low-dimensional predictive geometry.

Abstract: Understanding how neural networks learn and organize features is central to understanding their behavior. Much existing theory of feature learning has focused on the emergence of a global low-dimensional predictive geometry. We show that this picture is incomplete. In regression problems with clustered data, we demonstrate that multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) naturally develop monosemantic specialized neurons: individual neurons become strongly aligned with a specific predictive feature relevant to a particular region of the input space. Rather than learning a single global low-dimensional representation, MLPs learn a collection of local low-dimensional representations that can collectively span a high-dimensional space. This specialization provably gives MLPs a data-efficiency advantage over feature-learning methods based on a global low-dimensional representation.

</details>


### [145] [ChorusTIC: Training-Free Multivariate Time Series Classification via Chorus In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24033)
*Juntao Fang,Shifeng Xie,Ruichu Cai,Shengji Zheng,Zijian Li,Keli Zhang,Lujia Pan,Themis Palpanas,Zhifeng Hao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ChorusTIC is a foundation model for time series classification that performs in-context learning across variable channel configurations without needing task-specific fine-tuning, achieving strong results on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series foundation models require fitting task-specific classifiers for each target dataset and handle multivariate channels independently. There's a need for a classification-native approach that works across heterogeneous channel configurations without parameter updates.

Method: Combines Random Subchannel Slot Concatenation with a dual-axis encoder to model temporal and cross-channel interactions, maps variable channels to fixed-width representations, uses context-derived distribution calibration, and employs leakage-protected in-context learning. Pretrained only on synthetic labeled episodes with context/query sets sharing task backgrounds.

Result: Strong performance on complete UEA-30 and UCR-128 archives for both full-context and low-label scenarios, without needing target-specific classifier fitting.

Conclusion: ChorusTIC demonstrates effective in-context time series classification across diverse channel configurations, eliminating the need for per-dataset classifier training while maintaining competitive performance.

Abstract: Time series classification underpins applications in healthcare, sensing, and industrial monitoring. Although time series foundation models support forecasting and transferable representation learning, classification still typically requires fitting a task-specific classifier on each target dataset, while individual channels of multivariate inputs are often encoded independently. We introduce ChorusTIC, a classification-native foundation model for in-context classification across heterogeneous channel configurations without target-task parameter updates. ChorusTIC combines episode-consistent Random Subchannel Slot Concatenation with a shared dual-axis encoder to model temporal and cross-channel interactions and map variable channel configurations into a fixed-width representation independent of the original channel count. It then calibrates feature axes using context-derived distributions and predicts query labels through leakage-protected in-context learning. We pretrain ChorusTIC solely on synthetic labeled episodes comprising context and query sets that share a task background, with classes distinguished by sparse temporal or cross-channel rules. Evaluations on the complete UEA-30 and UCR-128 archives show strong full-context and low-label performance without target-specific classifier fitting.

</details>


### [146] [PinSieve: Production Selective VLM Serving and a Governed Memory Flywheel for Enterprise Content-Quality Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24040)
*Chuqing Gao,Yuanfang Song,Jonathan Zhang,Yifan Wu,Vishwakarma Singh,Qinglong Zeng,Andrey Gusev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PinSieve: A production AI agent system for content-quality filtering that uses selective VLM serving on grey-zone cases, with governed memory flywheel for continuous improvement and 25.7% productivity gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI agents in production need to be bounded, stateful, observable, and governable rather than fully autonomous, requiring systems that can operate safely at scale while maintaining human oversight.

Method: 1) Selective VLM Serving Agent that only processes unresolved grey-zone cases left by lightweight upstream models; 2) Governed memory flywheel with Data Curation Agent using proposal-verifier loops with guardrails; 3) Reasoning Review Agent that audits teacher-generated rationales; 4) Scalar routing scores, human escalation, and audit sampling for feedback.

Result: Deployed system filters 2.05x more non-actionable items with slightly reduced miss rate; improves review productivity by 25.7%, reduces operating cost by 16.2%, moves signal delivery from next-day to same-day; reduces average false negative rate from 17.73% to 13.29% in memory replay.

Conclusion: PinSieve demonstrates a successful production AI agent framework that balances automation with governance, achieving significant performance improvements while maintaining safety controls, with the recipe showing transferability to multiple internal signals beyond the initial task.

Abstract: Enterprise AI agents in production often need to be bounded, stateful, observable, and governable rather than fully autonomous. We present PinSieve, a production case study in a large-scale content-quality pipeline. Its deployed component is a selective vision-language-model (VLM) Serving Agent that operates only on the grey-zone slice left unresolved by lightweight upstream models, exposes a scalar routing score online, and preserves controlled human escalation. On this slice, the deployed system filters 2.05x more non-actionable items than the previous production module while slightly reducing estimated miss rate; after promotion, it improves review productivity by 25.7%, reduces normalized operating cost by 16.2%, and moves signal delivery from next-day to same-day. We then study maintenance through a governed memory flywheel under selective feedback, where escalated items are reviewed by default and auto-passed items are labeled mainly through audit sampling. Feedback Memory records routing traces, observation paths, audit propensities, and replay metadata for evaluation and debugging. The Data Curation Agent uses a bounded proposal-verifier loop over representative, uncertainty, recency, and fresh-review replay, with positive-rate and score-bin guardrails before batch acceptance. In chained monthly refresh over six months of production data, this design reduces average FNR@50% from 17.73% under representative random replay to 13.29%. A Reasoning Review Agent audits teacher-generated rationales and supports keep/repair/drop decisions. Production claims are attributed only to the deployed Serving Agent; replay and rationale-review results are offline or sampled-governance evidence. The same serving-agent recipe has been adopted to several additional internal signals, suggesting transferability beyond one task.

</details>


### [147] [XP-JEPA: Cross-Predictive Physics Grounding for Forecastable Latent Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24044)
*Kehan Wen,Ziming Li,Siyuan Luo,Fan Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XP-JEPA uses privileged physical trajectories during training to ground visual latent dynamics, improving forecastability and control performance without needing physical sensors at test time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-predictive world models can suffer from co-adaptation between encoder and predictor, resulting in latent dynamics that are easy to predict but not properly constrained by physical reality. This leads to poor rollout performance and control.

Method: XP-JEPA encodes visual observations and physical states separately, passes both through a shared action-conditioned predictor, and matches predictions across both modalities. This cross-predictive objective encourages unified latent dynamics grounded in physical transitions.

Result: XP-JEPA reduces rollout drift from 0.361 to 0.104 and increases mean control success from 53.6% to 78.2% across six evaluation subfamilies. Physical-state regression alone improves position decoding but doesn't help forecastability or control.

Conclusion: Cross-predictive physical grounding produces more forecastable latent dynamics for rollout-based control without requiring privileged inputs at test time, solving the co-adaptation problem in self-predictive models.

Abstract: Latent world models plan by predicting how candidate actions transform learned representations. In self-predictive models, however, the encoder and predictor are optimized jointly and can co-adapt to latent transitions that are easy to predict but only weakly constrained by the physical evolution of the scene. We introduce the cross-predictive JEPA (XP-JEPA), which grounds visual latent dynamics in privileged physical trajectories. XP-JEPA separately encodes visual observations and physical states, advances both through a shared action-conditioned predictor, and matches each prediction to both future representations. This objective encourages unified latent dynamics across the two modalities, grounded in the underlying physical transitions. The physical branch is discarded after training, leaving a visual-only model at deployment. On a multi-task suite spanning six evaluation subfamilies, XP-JEPA reduces rollout drift of a newly fitted predictor from $0.361$ to $0.104$ and increases mean control success from $53.6\%$ to $78.2\%$. Direct physical-state regression raises position decodability but leaves forecastability and control near the visual-only baseline. Cross-predictive physical grounding can therefore produce more forecastable latent dynamics for rollout-based control without privileged inputs at test time.

</details>


### [148] [Physics-Integrated Operator Learning via Gaussian Splatting Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24049)
*Jihao Zhang,Junyi Guo,Jian-Xun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel approach integrates physics into neural operators using feed-forward Gaussian splatting (FFGS) representation, enabling direct incorporation of governing PDE operators without physics-residual losses, achieving significant error reduction in long-horizon predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural operators for PDE systems have limitations: purely data-driven approaches accumulate substantial errors during long-horizon autoregressive prediction and fail to exploit available governing-equation structure. Existing physics-informed methods use residual-based training or PDE-specific architectural constraints, which can cause optimization difficulties or limit architectural generality.

Method: The authors introduce a representation-level approach using feed-forward Gaussian splatting (FFGS) as a continuous interface between discretized solution fields and governing operators. FFGS reconstructs the state as a continuous Gaussian field with closed-form spatial derivatives, allowing physical PDE operators to be integrated directly within the learned evolution map without introducing a physics-residual loss.

Result: The framework reduces relative ℓ₂ error by 1.5×-2.2× compared with strongest purely data-driven baselines across 2D and 3D PDE systems (advection, diffusion, nonlinear self-advection, reaction dynamics). It consistently improves spectral fidelity and remains effective when governing equations are partially known, demonstrating robustness to incomplete physics.
