Conclusion: Continuous field representations like FFGS provide a practical interface for incorporating known physical structure into generic neural-operator surrogates, offering a more effective way to integrate physics while maintaining architectural generality and avoiding optimization difficulties of residual-based approaches.

Abstract: Neural operators provide efficient surrogates for spatiotemporal PDE systems, but purely data-driven formulations often accumulate substantial errors during long-horizon autoregressive prediction and may fail to exploit available governing-equation structure. Existing approaches incorporate physics primarily through residual-based training objectives or PDE-specific architectural constraints, which can introduce optimization difficulties or limit architectural generality. In this work, we introduce a representation-level approach to physics integration in which a feed-forward Gaussian splatting (FFGS) representation serves as a continuous interface between discretized solution fields and governing operators. The FFGS representation reconstructs the state as a continuous Gaussian field with closed-form spatial derivatives, allowing available physical PDE operators to be integrated directly within the learned evolution map without introducing a physics-residual loss. We evaluate the framework across two- and three-dimensional PDE systems, including advection, diffusion, nonlinear self-advection, and reaction dynamics. Over long-horizon autoregressive rollouts, the proposed framework reduces relative $\ell_2$ error by $1.5\times$--$2.2\times$ compared with the strongest purely data-driven baseline across the benchmark suite, while consistently improving spectral fidelity. The framework also remains effective when the governing equations are partially known, demonstrating robustness to incomplete physics. These results demonstrate that continuous field representations can provide a practical interface for incorporating known physical structure into generic neural-operator surrogates.

</details>


### [149] [ALPHABET: A Laplace-Pole History Aggregator with Banked Exponential Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24051)
*Daehwa Ko,JaeHyeon Kim,Oh Seong Kwon,Jay Hoon Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALPHABET is an extremely compact linear-time sequence model with only 6,437 parameters that compresses temporal history into stable complex pole modes, achieving competitive performance with significantly faster inference and training than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a highly compact and efficient sequence model that remains competitive while offering an explicitly auditable prediction interface, challenging the notion that powerful sequence models require large numbers of parameters.

Method: ALPHABET uses a modal decomposition approach: it compresses temporal history into stable complex pole modes, with a direct bank synthesizing modal states into feature trajectories, an independent cascaded bank analyzing the transformed trajectory without resynthesis, and an affine head reading modal energies and lag moments from both banks.

Result: On a Gaussian control task with matched low-lag statistics, ALPHABET approaches the Bayes oracle performance while raw autocovariances remain at chance level. Across 82-task registry, it attains mean rank 3.97 in a ten-family comparison. With D=64 runtime anchor, its 6,437 parameters deliver 5.02× faster inference and 3.93× faster complete training steps than nine baselines on average.

Conclusion: ALPHABET demonstrates that compact sequence models with explicitly auditable interfaces can remain competitive, offering significant efficiency gains while maintaining strong performance through its modal decomposition approach to temporal information processing.

Abstract: Can a sequence model remain competitive with only a few thousand parameters and an explicitly auditable prediction interface? We introduce ALPHABET, a compact linear-time model that compresses temporal history into stable complex pole modes: a direct bank synthesizes its modal states back into the feature trajectory, an independent cascaded bank analyzes the transformed trajectory without resynthesis, and an affine head reads only modal energies and lag moments from both banks. We characterize the temporal information this descriptor retains: for a stationary, fully observed feature process, each mode energy is a frequency-localized measurement of the second-order spectrum, the continuum of such measurements identifies the spectrum, and almost every mode separates any fixed finite set of spectrally distinct classes. On a Gaussian control with matched low-lag statistics, the learned descriptor approaches the Bayes oracle where raw autocovariances remain at chance. Across the fixed 82-task registry, ALPHABET attains mean rank 3.97 in the complete ten-family comparison. At the common-width D=64 runtime anchor, its 6,437 parameters deliver 5.02 times faster inference and 3.93 times faster complete training steps than the nine baselines on average.

</details>


### [150] [PhysicsBench: A Unified Leaderboard for Generative and Predictive Models in Engineering Design and Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24056)
*Sang Won Lee,Hyogu Jeong,Namwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PhysicsBench is a unified benchmark that evaluates generative and predictive AI models for engineering design and simulation across 7 tasks, 9 datasets, and 28 configurations using standardized procedures and metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative and predictive AI models for engineering are evaluated in isolation with inconsistent metrics on academic datasets at unconstrained scales, lacking standardized comparison for realistic engineering applications.

Method: Created PhysicsBench with 7 generation/prediction tasks across 1D/2D/3D domains, evaluating 66 models on 9 datasets including industrial CAD/CFD/FEA simulations. Uses realistic limited data scales (S to XL), common metric suite for geometric fidelity, physical accuracy, and engineering validity. BenchRank system debiases correlated metrics and ranks via PageRank over head-to-head dominance graphs.

Result: Benchmark ranks 66 models across tasks; reveals that academic large-scale performance weakly predicts small-data ranking. Top model changes with data scale in 6/7 tasks, and no single model leads more than one task. Established first standardized procedure for comparing generative and predictive models in engineering.

Conclusion: PhysicsBench transforms "state-of-the-art" claims into an openly published foundation for model selection in engineering AI, showing that model performance is task-specific and data-scale dependent, providing practical guidance for industrial applications.

Abstract: Generative and predictive artificial intelligence models are increasingly used to generate geometry and to predict physical fields and scalar quantities in engineering design and simulation. Yet these models are typically evaluated in isolation, on academic datasets at unconstrained scales, with inconsistent metrics and procedures. We present PhysicsBench, a unified benchmark and leaderboard that evaluates generative and predictive models under one standardized procedure. PhysicsBench spans seven generation and prediction tasks across 1D, 2D, and 3D domains and ranks 66 models on nine datasets, comprising industrial-scale CAD/CFD/FEA simulations and public references, expanded into 28 configurations. One procedure and ranking apply to both families, each ranked within its own tasks. Evaluation spans realistic, limited data scales from S to XL rather than the unlimited training sets common in academic benchmarks. A common metric suite captures geometric fidelity with distributional distances, physical-field and scalar accuracy, and engineering-specific field- and shape-validity. BenchRank debiases correlated metrics and ranks by PageRank over a head-to-head dominance graph, so every reported quality metric is also ranked, with computational cost in a separate efficiency view. Across tasks, an architecture's large-scale academic standing weakly predicts its small-data ranking. The top model changes with data scale in six of the seven tasks, and no model leads more than one task. PhysicsBench turns "state-of-the-art" from a self-reported claim into an openly published foundation for model selection.

</details>


### [151] [Mechanistic Circuit Identification for Controllable Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24065)
*Nakyung Lee,Sangwoo Hong,Jungwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Circuit-grounded framework using mechanistic interpretability to actively steer data generation along three utility axes, improving diversity and downstream performance over heuristic prompt-based approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most data synthesis pipelines rely on heuristic prompt-based control, which is a black-box paradigm that provides limited insight into how individual samples interact with a model's learning dynamics. There's a need to bridge the gap between data generation and mechanistic understanding.

Method: Proposes a circuit-grounded framework connecting training-dynamics-based data valuation with mechanistic interpretability. Conceptualizes data quality along three utility axes (learnability, challenge, alignment), uncovers specialized model-internal circuits that causally govern these utility signals, and uses these circuits as controllable interfaces to actively steer data generation. Introduces SAMS (Stage-Aware Mechanistic Scheduling) to schedule circuit-steered data according to model's evolving optimization needs.

Result: Experiments on multiple-choice QA tasks demonstrate that the approach yields precisely controlled data with greater diversity than prompt-based baselines, consistently improving downstream performance and calibration.

Conclusion: Establishes a principled white-box paradigm for interpretable data generation, pioneering the use of mechanistic interpretability not just as an analytical tool, but as a practical, controllable interface for data synthesis.

Abstract: While recent advances in data synthesis aim to curate high-quality datasets, most generation pipelines still rely on heuristic prompt-based control. This black-box paradigm provides limited insight into how individual samples interact with a model's underlying learning dynamics. To bridge this gap, we propose a circuit-grounded framework that connects training-dynamics-based data valuation with mechanistic interpretability (MI). Specifically, we conceptualize data quality along three complementary utility axes, learnability, challenge, and alignment. First, we uncover specialized model-internal circuits that causally govern these utility signals. Then, moving beyond heuristic prompting toward mechanistic control, we leverage these circuits as controllable interfaces, actively steering generation to produce utility-targeted data. Building on this capability, we introduce SAMS (Stage-Aware Mechanistic Scheduling), which schedules circuit-steered data according to the model's evolving optimization needs. Experiments on multiple-choice QA tasks demonstrate that our approach yields precisely controlled data with greater diversity than prompt-based baselines, consistently improving downstream performance and calibration. Ultimately, this work establishes a principled white-box paradigm for interpretable data generation, pioneering the use of MI not just as an analytical tool, but as a practical, controllable interface.

</details>


### [152] [A Feature-Major Codebook for Memory-Efficient Sparse-Binary Self-Organizing Maps: Scaling a MEDLINE Atlas to 1.05 Million Neurons on a Single Consumer GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24067)
*Andrew James Amos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel memory layout optimization for self-organizing maps enables training at MEDLINE scale by accelerating BMU search 4.5-8.5x through feature-major storage and tiled sparse-dense product computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building self-organizing maps at MEDLINE scale (29.9 million articles) has been impractical due to computational bottlenecks in best-matching-unit (BMU) search, which dominates training time and is limited by memory bandwidth when reading the codebook.

Method: Proposed storing the codebook in feature-major layout (W[v.M+i]) with each feature's weights contiguous, which recasts BMU search as a tiled sparse-dense product where loaded weight columns are reused across tile samples. Combined with radius-independent box-blur update and convergence-based stopping rule.

Result: Achieved 4.5-8.5x acceleration in BMU search with no accuracy loss (0.5% quantisation error agreement). Trained converged map over 29.9 million MEDLINE articles in ~72s on 24GB GPU (64x64). Scaled to 1,048,576 neurons (1024x1024) on H200 GPU - largest reported SOM. 82x faster than MedSOM and 621x faster than best CPU library.

Conclusion: Memory layout optimization fundamentally changes scalability of self-organizing maps, enabling previously impossible scales. The limit on resolution is compute power rather than data structure, with smooth power law error scaling across three decades of map size.

Abstract: A self-organising map turns a large corpus into a browsable two-dimensional atlas, but building one at MEDLINE scale has been impractical: the best-matching-unit (BMU) search that dominates training is bound by the bandwidth needed to read the codebook every epoch. I show that this bottleneck is largely an artefact of codebook layout. Storing it feature-major with each feature's weights contiguous, W[v.M+i], recasts the search as a tiled sparse-dense product in which every loaded weight column is reused across a tile of samples. Varying only the layout, with implementation, precision and update rule held fixed, accelerates the BMU search by 4.5-8.5x. Because an exact-argmin BMU is invariant to how the codebook is stored, this gain costs nothing: held-out quantisation error agrees with a cuSPARSE baseline to within 0.5% at every map size. Against that baseline the advantage is a crossover rather than a constant: cuSPARSE.SOM is faster at small maps, SparseBin.SOM is 1.5x faster at 128x128 and 2.6x at 256x256, and at 512x512 it is the only one that runs at all on 24 GB. Paired with a radius-independent box-blur update and a convergence-based stopping rule, it trains a converged map over 29.9 million MEDLINE articles in about 72 s at 64x64 on one 24 GB GPU, and accommodates 262,144 neurons (512x512 edges) where every alternative algorithm I tested exceeds memory constraints. On a 141 GB H200 it reaches 1,048,576 neurons (1024x1024 edges) - to my knowledge the largest self-organising map yet reported. Held-out error follows a smooth power law with no elbow across three decades of map size, so the limit on resolution is compute rather than any breakpoint in the data. At matched work the design is ~82x faster than MedSOM, the CUDA implementation behind our earlier MEDLINE atlases and, at 128x128, 621x faster than the best available multicore-CPU library.

</details>


### [153] [Knowing When to Ask for Help: Bayesian Self-Escalation in Hierarchical LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24087)
*Nadeem Shaikh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces intra-generation delegation, where AI agents recognize during reasoning when they're unlikely to succeed and dynamically transfer control to stronger models, formulated as a Bayesian optimal-stopping problem with learned competence posteriors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent systems handle delegation either before reasoning begins (router picks model) or after response completion (verifier scores and may retry). The authors identify a gap: recognizing during reasoning when an agent is unlikely to succeed and dynamically transferring control to stronger models.

Method: Formulates intra-generation delegation as a Bayesian optimal-stopping problem using a learned competence posterior (online estimate of eventual task success). Derives myopic escalation threshold in closed form, characterizes optimal policy via dynamic programming, and proves properties of the optimal policy. Provides theoretical guarantees including regret bounds and finite-sample convergence rates.

Result: Theoretical results show optimal policy is time-varying threshold, exponential separation at Chernoff-information rate, regret bound governed by posterior calibration, and 1/sqrt(n) regret decay. Experimental validation confirms theoretical predictions in controlled simulation and real-model tests on Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B->7B code cascade (MBPP, 257 tasks), showing escalation frontier dominates post-hoc routing at equal cost and cumulative competence belief discrimination rises over generation.

Conclusion: Intra-generation delegation is a promising third regime for AI agent systems that dynamically transfers control based on real-time competence estimates, with strong theoretical guarantees and empirical validation showing superiority over existing delegation approaches.

Abstract: Current LLM agent systems decide delegation before reasoning begins (a router picks a model) or after a response is complete (a verifier scores it and may retry). We study a third regime: an agent that recognises, during its own reasoning, that it is unlikely to succeed and transfers control to a stronger model. We formulate intra-generation delegation as a Bayesian optimal-stopping problem over a learned competence posterior -- an online estimate of the agent's eventual task success whose sufficient statistics are learned from labelled trajectories, not read off raw entropy. We derive the myopic escalation threshold in closed form, characterise the optimal policy via dynamic programming, and prove that the optimal policy is a time-varying threshold with no shape assumption on the raw signal. We further prove exponential separation of the oracle belief at the Chernoff-information rate of the signal, a regret bound governed by the calibration of the posterior, and a finite-sample guarantee: with n labelled calibration trajectories the deployed plug-in policy's regret decays as 1/sqrt(n). A controlled simulation study confirms each prediction of the theory, including the predicted 1/sqrt(n) rate. We additionally report a real-model validation on a Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B->7B code cascade (MBPP, 257 tasks), confirming two of three pre-registered predictions: the escalation frontier dominates post-hoc routing at equal cost, and the cumulative competence belief's discrimination rises over generation.

</details>


### [154] [The Sharp Tail of Uniform Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24098)
*Pahan Dewasurendra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper closes the gap in understanding optimal high-probability generalization bounds for uniformly stable algorithms with bounded loss, showing that the linear dependence on log(1/δ) in upper bounds is indeed achievable by constructing a deterministic learning problem that matches these bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's an open question about whether the linear dependence on log(1/δ) in uniform stability generalization bounds (O(γlog(1/δ) + L√(log(1/δ)/n))) is actually achievable by real learning algorithms. Previous constructions only achieved it for auxiliary weakly dependent variables with growing range, and lower bounds only held for constant probability.

Method: The authors construct a deterministic γ-uniformly stable learning problem using bounded absolute-loss regression with constant labels. The key innovation is a multiscale collection of rare Rademacher features, where a coordinatewise ramp is stable in sup norm, and an odd symmetrized maximum converts a unique extreme feature into a gap of order γp without violating the loss bound. Geometrically spaced ramps allow all confidence levels to be captured in the same problem.

Result: For any n, stability level γ, and loss bound L, they construct a learning problem where the generalization gap satisfies ℙ(R(A_S)-R_S(A_S) ≥ c'min{L, γp + L√(p/n)}) ≥ e^{-p} for 1 ≤ p ≤ cn. This shows the linear dependence on p (which corresponds to log(1/δ)) is achievable, matching the upper bound up to constants.

Conclusion: The construction, together with the logarithmic-free upper bound, determines the optimal high-probability and moment dependence of uniform stability up to universal constants, fully closing the gap between upper and lower bounds for uniformly stable algorithms with bounded loss.

Abstract: Uniform stability controls how much one training example can change the loss at any test point. A new logarithmic-free upper bound shows that a $γ$-uniformly stable algorithm with loss in $[0,L]$ has generalization gap at most $O \left(γ\log(1/δ) +L\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/δ)}{n}}\right)$ with probability $1-δ$. Whether an actual bounded-loss learning algorithm can realize the linear dependence on $\log(1/δ)$ has remained open. The known construction realizes it only for auxiliary weakly dependent random variables whose pointwise range grows with $n$. The known learning lower bound holds only at constant probability. We close this gap. For every $n$, stability level $γ$, and loss bound $L$, we construct one deterministic $γ$-uniformly stable learning problem whose tail satisfies, simultaneously for $1\le p\le c n$, $\mathbb P \left( R(A_S)-R_S(A_S) \ge c'\min \left\{L,γp+L\sqrt{p/n}\right\} \right)\ge e^{-p}.$ The construction is ordinary bounded absolute-loss regression with constant labels. Its key is a multiscale collection of rare Rademacher features. A coordinatewise ramp is stable in sup norm, while an odd symmetrized maximum converts a unique extreme feature into a gap of order $γp$ without violating the loss bound. Geometrically spaced ramps put all confidence levels into the same problem. Together with the logarithmic-free upper bound, this determines the optimal high-probability and moment dependence of uniform stability up to universal constants.

</details>


### [155] [Structured Frequency-Domain Evidence for LLM-Based Time-Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24113)
*Jungwook Seo,Sangwon Son,Minjeong Kim,Seungmin Han,Seojin Yoo,Sungyong Baik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Explicit frequency-domain evidence improves zero-shot LLM-based time-series anomaly detection by capturing spectral patterns that time-domain representations miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based TSAD methods focus on time-domain evidence (indexed values, plots, de-seasonalized data) but miss spectral structure anomalies like shifted periodicity and oscillatory fluctuations.

Method: Evidence-augmented zero-shot framework with FFT-computed frequency-domain evidence at two resolutions: global for sequence-level periodic context and local for time-localized spectral departures, preserving indexed de-seasonalized observations.

Result: Experiments with multiple LLMs (InternVL2-LLaMA3-76B, Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, Gemini-2.5-Flash, GPT-4o) on AnomLLM and TSB-AD-U show explicit frequency-domain evidence improves baseline LLM-based TSAD performance.

Conclusion: Frequency-domain evidence effectively complements indexed and de-seasonalized time-domain inputs for zero-shot LLM-based time-series anomaly detection.

Abstract: Time-series anomalies can appear not only as pointwise deviations but also as changes in recurring temporal structure, such as shifted periodicity or localized oscillatory fluctuations. However, existing LLM-based time-series anomaly detection methods mainly expose time-domain evidence through indexed values, plots, or de-seasonalized representations, leaving spectral structure implicit. We propose an evidence-augmented zero-shot TSAD framework that preserves indexed de-seasonalized observations while adding compact frequency-domain evidence computed with the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT). The evidence is constructed at two resolutions: global frequency-domain evidence summarizes sequence-level periodic context, while local frequency-domain evidence captures time-localized spectral departures. Experiments on AnomLLM with InternVL2-LLaMA3-76B, Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct, Gemini-2.5-Flash, and GPT-4o, together with evaluation on the TSB-AD-U subset, show that explicit frequency-domain evidence improves LLM-based TSAD baselines. These results suggest that frequency-domain evidence can complement indexed and de-seasonalized time-domain inputs for zero-shot LLM-based TSAD.

</details>


### [156] [A mesh-free multiresolution deep energy method with phase-field modeling of brittle fracture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24126)
*Han Zhang,Mehrisadat Makki Alamdari,Babak Shahbodagh,Mohammad Vahab,Cosmin Anitescu,Timon Rabczuk,Elena Atroshchenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks with multiresolution encoding trained by minimizing incremental energy provide a mesh-free approach for phase-field brittle fracture modeling, outperforming traditional methods on various benchmark problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional phase-field fracture modeling requires dense discretization to resolve localization bands of unknown paths, creating computational challenges for mesh-based methods.

Method: Uses a single neural network to represent displacement and phase fields, trained by minimizing incremental energy directly. Features multiresolution encoding with C¹ quadratic B-spline grids and stratified Monte Carlo integration with points redrawn each iteration.

Result: Achieves load-displacement curves matching staggered FEM references (within 1% on single-edge-notched tests, 8% where crack topology changes). Classifies 90% of seeded cracks correctly on benchmark data vs. deep Ritz baseline failure.

Conclusion: The pairing of multiresolution encoding with adaptive Monte Carlo integration enables effective mesh-free phase-field fracture modeling, handling complex crack patterns and outperforming existing neural network baselines.

Abstract: Phase-field modeling of brittle fracture removes the need to track cracks explicitly by recasting their evolution as the minimization of an energy functional. In return it requires a discretization dense enough to resolve a localization band whose width is set by a regularization length and whose path is not known in advance. We propose a mesh-free discretization in which a single neural network represents the displacement and phase fields and is trained by minimizing the incremental energy directly. The coordinates enter the network through a multiresolution feature encoding built from $C^1$ quadratic B-spline grids, so the finest scale the representation can express is set by choice rather than reached through slow training, and the energy is estimated by stratified Monte Carlo integration on points redrawn at every optimizer iteration. This pairing proves critical, since the crack fails to advance both when the integration points are held fixed and when the encoding is too coarse to represent the band, while each ingredient tolerates a wide range of settings once the other is in place. Because the representation is globally $C^1$, the second- and the fourth-order fracture energy densities run on the identical discretization. Across six problems, from single-edge-notched tension and shear to a thick-walled ring on a single spline patch, the computed load-displacement curves follow staggered finite element references at matched regularization length, with peak loads within about 1% on the single-edge-notched tests and within 8% where the crack pattern changes topology. On a public benchmark dataset of random multi-crack configurations the method classifies the active or dormant state of 90% of the seeded cracks in twenty zero-shot runs, where the deep Ritz baseline of the dataset authors fails.

</details>


### [157] [From Gradient-Boosted Trees to Deep Recommenders: Practical Lessons from Migrating a Production Customer Support Recommender](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24132)
*Sonia Sharma,Jeyendran Balakrishnan,Shreya Rajpal,Swapnil Parekh,Nagaraj Janardhana,Andrew Mattarella-Micke*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper describes migrating a production conversational recommendation system from tree-based classifiers to a pairwise-binary deep recommender that handles dynamic bundled offerings and incorporates multimodal signals including conversation transcripts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional tree-based classifiers (like gradient-boosted multiclass models) struggle with modern product catalogs that feature dynamically bundled, discount-coupled offerings. These classifiers assume fixed label spaces and cannot effectively incorporate multimodal signals like tabular data and conversation transcripts, creating challenges for conversational recommendation systems.

Method: Reformulated recommendation as pairwise binary prediction to learn jointly from user and item features. Enhanced representations via negative sampling and noise injection. Used attention pooling over transcript chunks to incorporate long conversation context. Explored multiple architectures including two-tower models and DeepFM variants, with loss functions like contrastive loss.

Result: The deep recommender approach achieved parity with the CatBoost baseline at the beginning of conversations and outperformed it at later conversational stages across all evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: Pairwise-binary deep recommenders can successfully replace traditional tree-based classifiers in conversational recommendation systems, particularly for handling dynamic product catalogs and incorporating multimodal conversational context while maintaining or improving recommendation quality.

Abstract: Product catalogs in fast-moving service businesses are shifting from static, independently priced SKUs toward dynamically bundled, discount-coupled offerings--a shift that strains the tree-based classifiers traditionally preferred for sparse and highly imbalanced data. These classifiers assume a fixed, slowly changing label space and struggle to incorporate multimodal signals such as tabular data and transcripts. We present the migration of a live, production conversational recommendation system from a gradient-boosted multiclass model to a pairwise-binary deep recommender. Because this system is critical to ecosystem growth initiatives and downstream features like dynamic pitching--surfacing the most relevant pitch text to a support agent in real time during a live customer conversation--maintaining live recommendation quality was a non-negotiable constraint. We detail the techniques that made this migration successful--reformulating recommendation as pairwise binary prediction to learn jointly from user and item features, and enhancing learned representations via negative sampling and noise injection. To efficiently incorporate long, live conversation context, we apply attention pooling over transcript chunks and benchmark it against TF-IDF and sentence-embedding baselines. Finally, we explore multiple architectures (including two-tower models, DeepFM, and their variants) and loss functions such as contrastive loss. Evaluating against a CatBoost baseline across all conversational stages, we demonstrate that our approach achieves parity at conversation beginning and outperforms at later conversational stages.

</details>


### [158] [Steering Recurrent Reasoners at Inference Time with Readout Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24136)
*Shunsuke Kamiya,Masanori Koyama,Seongcheol Jeong,Fumiya Uchiyama,Kenji Kubo,Kohei Hayashi,Masahiro Suzuki,Yutaka Matsuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Readout Feedback (RoFB) improves recurrent model performance at inference time by using intermediate predictions to steer latent dynamics without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing inference-time methods for recurrent models only scale computation by running more steps or sampling more trajectories, but ignore valuable information revealed within each trajectory during inference.

Method: RoFB is a test-time intervention that converts intermediate readout probabilities into token-wise pairwise coupling forces, which are injected into the latent dynamics of recurrent models to provide closed-loop steering.

Result: RoFB yields clear performance gains in 4 out of 6 model-task pairs across Sudoku and Maze tasks, achieving performance unattainable by simply running more steps or selecting from multiple trajectories, at comparable or lower computational cost.

Conclusion: Closed-loop steering of latent dynamics through readout feedback can serve as a complementary inference-time control mechanism for recurrent reasoning models, providing a new way to improve performance without retraining.

Abstract: Recurrent models, which repeatedly update latent states with shared computation blocks, have emerged as powerful architectures for solving complex reasoning tasks. Existing inference-time methods scale computation by running more steps or sampling more trajectories, but ignore information revealed within each trajectory. Here we show that recurrent models can be improved at inference time by using their own readout probabilities to steer latent dynamics without retraining. We introduce Readout Feedback (RoFB), a test-time intervention that converts intermediate predictions into token-wise pairwise coupling forces injected into the latent dynamics. Across three recurrent models (AKOrN, ItrSA++, TRM) on Sudoku and Maze, RoFB yields clear gains in four of six model-task pairs, achieving performance unattainable by merely running more steps or selecting from multiple trajectories, at comparable or lower computational cost. These results suggest that closed-loop steering of latent dynamics can serve as a complementary inference-time control mechanism for recurrent reasoning models.

</details>


### [159] [Robust Data-Collection Policy Learning for Low-Variance Online Policy Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24146)
*Claire Chen,Shuze Daniel Liu,Licheng Luo,Rohan Chandra,Nan Jiang,Shangtong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A double-loop gradient-based algorithm for learning robust behavior policies that reduce evaluation variance in RL, accounting for transition function uncertainties between simulation and real-world environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classic on-policy RL evaluation methods suffer from high variance, and existing behavior policy search approaches don't account for transition function uncertainties. Simulator transitions often differ from real world due to modeling errors, causing behavior policies trained in simulation to still yield high variance when deployed, leading to costly real-world evaluation requirements.

Method: Proposes a double-loop gradient-based algorithm for learning behavior policies that are robust to transition uncertainty. The method involves novel transition-variance gradient expressions to optimize policies that perform well despite discrepancies between simulated and real-world transitions.

Result: Theoretically establishes global convergence guarantees for the algorithm. Numerically demonstrates that the method is less sensitive to transition perturbations than existing approaches, showing practical utility through reduced sensitivity to transition uncertainties.

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm successfully addresses the problem of transition uncertainty in RL policy evaluation, providing both theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical robustness, making behavior policies more reliable when deployed from simulation to real-world environments.

Abstract: In reinforcement learning policy evaluation, classic on-policy methods often suffer from high variance when estimating policy performance. To mitigate this issue, behavior policy search has been proposed to learn data-collecting policies tailored to reduce online evaluation variance. However, these approaches do not account for uncertainties in the transition functions. In practice, simulator transitions often differ from the real world due to modeling errors or approximation limitations. As a result, behavior policies trained in simulation may still yield high variance when deployed in real environments, leading to costly reliance on real-world evaluation samples. In this work, we propose a double-loop gradient-based algorithm for learning behavior policies that are both efficient and robust to transition uncertainty. Theoretically, we derive novel transition-variance gradient expressions and establish global convergence guarantees for the algorithm. Numerically, we demonstrate that our method is less sensitive to transition perturbations than existing approaches, providing supportive evidence for its practical utility.

</details>


### [160] [From Relaxed Indexability to Exact Indexability: A $t$-Step Approach for Partially Observable Restless Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24167)
*Qizhen Jia,Keqin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a t-step lookahead threshold policy for restless multi-armed bandits under partial observability, extending Liu's linearization approach by considering longer-horizon continuation values, with proven geometric convergence to the exact Whittle index.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whittle index policies scale well for restless multi-armed bandits, but under partial observability, determining the indifference subsidy requires solving infinite-horizon belief-state problems with no closed-form value function. Liu's approach linearizes the decision boundary but only uses one-step active-passive comparisons, ignoring longer-horizon continuation values.

Method: Extends Liu's framework to a t-step lookahead threshold policy where thresholds are defined by the active-minus-passive advantage under t-step finite-horizon value iteration. The method includes indexability verification and doesn't require indexability as input. At t=1, it recovers Liu's linear threshold; for t>1, thresholds become subsidy-dependent through induced first-crossing structure.

Result: Under original Whittle indexability, proves t-step approximate Whittle index converges geometrically to exact Whittle index with O(β^t) error. Numerically verifies 2,715 tested three-state instances as indexable. P95 index error decreases from 2.18×10⁻² at t=1 to 8.93×10⁻⁴ at t=8. In an exact-comparable instance with β=0.9999, t=2 recovers exact Whittle-index ordering. Moderate-depth policies outperform one-step baseline and stay close to optimal DP benchmark with mild runtime growth with t.

Conclusion: The t-step lookahead threshold policy effectively addresses limitations of Liu's one-step approach by incorporating longer-horizon continuation values, achieving geometric convergence to exact Whittle indices while maintaining computational efficiency and practical performance improvements.

Abstract: Whittle index policies offer a scalable method for restless multi-armed bandits, but under partial observability even determining the indifference subsidy at a single belief requires solving an infinite-horizon belief-state problem with no closed-form value function. Liu [10] addresses this difficulty by linearizing the unknown decision boundary, leading to a linear system and a closed-form approximate Whittle index. However, the resulting threshold uses only a one-step active--passive comparison and does not account for longer-horizon continuation values.
  We extend this framework to a \emph{$t$-step lookahead threshold policy}. For each subsidy $m$, the threshold is defined by the active-minus-passive advantage under $t$-step finite-horizon value iteration. At $t=1$, the threshold is $m$-independent and recovers the linear threshold of Liu [10]; for $t>1$, it becomes subsidy-dependent through the induced first-crossing structure and tracks the exact decision boundary more closely. The proposed algorithm does not require indexability as an input and includes an indexability verification. Under the original Whittle indexability, we prove that the $t$-step approximate Whittle index converges geometrically to the exact Whittle index, \[ |\widehat W_t(ω)-W(ω)|=O(β^t). \] Numerically, all 2,715 tested three-state instances are verified as indexable according to the proposed criterion. The P95 index error decreases from $2.18\times10^{-2}$ at $t=1$ to $8.93\times10^{-4}$ at $t=8$. In an exact-comparable instance with $β=0.9999$, $t=2$ already recovers the exact Whittle-index ordering. Moderate-depth threshold policies also outperform the one-step baseline and remain close to the optimal dynamic-programming benchmark, while runtime grows mildly with $t$.

</details>


### [161] [PRQ-KMeans: Projection Residual Quantization for Semantic ID Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24207)
*Yunxiao Luo,Siyuan Wang,Ben Chen,Chenyi Lei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRQ-KMeans improves semantic identifier tokenization by removing global mean, using Top-k similarity-weighted centroid refinement, and projection residuals instead of full subtraction, achieving significant performance gains in retrieval and recommendation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current residual-quantization tokenizers for semantic identifiers have three key limitations: 1) corpus-wide shared components consume first-level capacity, 2) hard assignment ignores graded similarities to nearby codewords, and 3) full-codeword subtraction leaves variation along selected directions in subsequent residuals.

Method: Proposed PRQ-KMeans with three key improvements: 1) removes the global-mean component first, 2) refines centroids with Top-k similarity-weighted updates instead of hard assignments, and 3) replaces full-codeword subtraction with projection residuals that remove each representation's selected-centroid component.

Result: Experiments on large-scale industrial search dataset and four public recommendation benchmarks show PRQ-KMeans achieves strongest overall performance among evaluated tokenizers, with gains up to 7.4% in HitRate and 11.8% in MRR on industrial dataset.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that addressing three identified limitations in residual-quantization tokenizers through PRQ-KMeans leads to significant performance improvements in generative retrieval and recommendation systems by better modeling progressive commonality removal.

Abstract: Semantic identifiers (SIDs) represent entities as hierarchical token sequences for generative retrieval and recommendation. Residual-quantization tokenizers construct these sequences by selecting a codeword at each level and passing a residual to the next. We view this process as progressive commonality removal: each token captures a component shared within its group, while later tokens should model the remaining differences. This view reveals three limitations: a corpus-wide shared component can consume first-level capacity, hard assignment ignores graded similarities to nearby codewords, and full-codeword subtraction can leave variation along the selected-codeword direction in the next residual. We therefore develop our solution in the post-hoc setting, where residual construction is not constrained by input reconstruction. Specifically, we propose PRQ-KMeans, which removes the global-mean component, refines centroids with Top-k similarity-weighted updates, and replaces full-codeword subtraction with a projection residual that removes each representation's selected-centroid component. Experiments on a large-scale industrial search dataset and four public recommendation benchmarks show that PRQ-KMeans achieves the strongest overall performance among the evaluated tokenizers, including gains of up to 7.4% in HitRate and 11.8% in MRR on the industrial dataset.

</details>


### [162] [A Data-dependent Early Stopping Rule using Rademacher Complexity with L1-norm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24210)
*Duy Hoang,Bastien Berret,Olivier Bruneau,Laurent Fribourg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces an analytic framework using Rademacher complexity with L1-norm to estimate optimal early stopping time for neural networks without requiring actual training, focusing on linear models but extendable to nonlinear networks via linear probing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for estimating generalization error require numerical training with early stopping strategies. There's a need for analytical methods that can predict optimal early stopping time without actual training. Current analytical methods rely on random matrix theory with restrictive assumptions about data distribution or covariance eigenvalues.

Method: The authors develop an analytic framework based on Rademacher complexity (RC) using L1-norm instead of L2-norm. They focus on linear models and linear regression problems. The "linear probing" method allows application of results to nonlinear neural networks. The approach avoids probabilistic assumptions required by random matrix theory methods.

Result: The framework successfully estimates optimal early stopping time without requiring gradient descent training. It provides analytic estimations that don't depend on training data. The method is demonstrated to work for nonlinear neural networks in a classification task using MNIST dataset via linear probing.

Conclusion: The proposed Rademacher complexity-based analytic framework offers a practical alternative to numerical early stopping methods, providing theoretical advantages and practical applicability to both linear and nonlinear models while avoiding restrictive probabilistic assumptions.

Abstract: Training neural networks requires balancing the trade-off between fitting the training data and achieving robust performance on unseen inputs. This ability, commonly referred to as generalizability, is determined by the gap between the empirical risk on the training set (``empirical loss'') and the expected risk over the data distribution (``generalization error''). Existing approaches typically estimate the generalization error numerically, requiring gradient descent training and an ``early stopping'' strategy. In this work, we introduce an analytic framework that estimates the optimal time of early stopping without the need for training. Several works in the literature also give such analytical estimations, but they are generally based on random matrix theory and often make assumptions on the distribution of the data or the eigenvalue distribution of the covariance matrix. In contrast, our work is based on Rademacher complexity (RC) without needing such probabilistic assumptions. For both theoretical and numerical reasons, it is more relevant to express RC with the L1- norm rather than with the L2-norm. We focus on the case of linear models and the problem of linear regression. Thanks to the ``linear probing'' method, our results can, however, be successfully applied to nonlinear neural networks, as illustrated in the classification MNIST example.

</details>


### [163] [Contrastive Branch Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24300)
*Ying Wang,Changlin Qiu,Bang Lin,Linbo Jin,Wen Jiang,Zhe Sun,Jingli Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CBPO introduces a novel reinforcement learning method for language models that disentangles branch sampling problems into separate mechanisms for rollout allocation and credit assignment, enabling fine-grained attribution of outcomes to specific decisions in tool-integrated agent training without process-level supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR enables language models to learn multi-turn tool interaction, but sparse outcome rewards provide no signal for identifying which intermediate decisions are responsible for success. Existing branch sampling methods conflate two distinct problems: allocating fixed rollout budgets and translating branch outcomes into token-level credit.

Method: Contrastive Branch Policy Optimization (CBPO) introduces separate mechanisms for each problem: generation entropy screens candidate branch positions, path-level and node-level decay distribute budget to prevent exploration collapse. Uses exact-prefix groups to define Contrastive Branch Value (CBV) - an outcome-based estimate of local decision sensitivity that rescales continuation advantages. Partitions trajectories into non-overlapping credit segments to avoid duplicated gradients on shared tokens.

Result: Extensive experiments on ten benchmarks (five for mathematical reasoning and five for knowledge-intensive search) show CBPO consistently outperforms state-of-the-art policy-optimization and branch-based methods, attaining the highest macro-average accuracy in both domains and across two model scales.

Conclusion: CBPO provides a practical solution for fine-grained credit assignment in tool-integrated agent training that requires only outcome rewards without process-level annotation. The method successfully disentangles exploration budget allocation from credit assignment, enabling more effective attribution of outcomes to specific decisions in language model agents using external tools.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enables language models to learn multi-turn interaction with external tools, yet its sparse outcome rewards provide no signal for identifying which intermediate decisions are responsible for success. Branch sampling induces local comparisons among alternative continuations, but existing methods tend to conflate two distinct problems: allocating a fixed rollout budget and translating branch outcomes into token-level credit. We introduce Contrastive Branch Policy Optimization (CBPO), which disentangles these two problems and assigns a dedicated mechanism to each. Generation entropy screens candidate branch positions across the entire response, while path-level and node-level decay distribute a fixed budget across trajectories and positions to prevent exploration from collapsing onto a few paths or adjacent tokens. A parent trajectory together with the branches that share an identical token prefix forms an exact-prefix group, and the reward variation within this controlled group defines the Contrastive Branch Value (CBV), an outcome-based estimate of local decision sensitivity that rescales continuation advantages without altering their sign. When multiple nodes are selected along the same trajectory, CBPO partitions it into non-overlapping credit segments, thereby avoiding duplicated gradients on shared tokens. Requiring only outcome rewards and no process-level annotation, CBPO provides a practical solution for fine-grained credit assignment in tool-integrated agent training. Extensive experiments on ten benchmarks, including five for mathematical reasoning and five for knowledge-intensive search, show that CBPO consistently outperforms state-of-the-art policy-optimization and branch-based methods, attaining the highest macro-average accuracy in both domains and across two model scales.

</details>


### [164] [Causal Analysis for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24303)
*Mathis Jander,Wouter van Heeswijk,Martijn Mes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A causal analysis framework to identify biases and failure modes in time series foundation models before deployment, tested on Chronos-2 and TimesFM-2.5 across six patterns, revealing specific vulnerabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The shift from bespoke time series models to foundation models introduces concentration risk where many applications are exposed to the same biases and failure modes of a single model, requiring methods to identify these issues before deployment.

Method: Proposed a causal analysis framework that intervenes on parameterized synthetic time series generators and measures corresponding model output changes under ceteris paribus conditions to test preservation of time series patterns.

Result: Found safe configurations for trend and harmonic oscillation patterns, bias toward overestimating persistence in both models, sudden failures against regime switch pattern for both models, and failure for TimesFM-2.5 against energy-release pattern, potentially explained by pretraining data.

Conclusion: Provides suggestions for further model development, application-specific model selection recommendations, and discusses limitations and research directions for ensuring reliable deployment of time series foundation models.

Abstract: Transitioning from bespoke time series models towards time series foundation models changes the relationship of model and application from one-to-one to one-to-many. This shift introduces concentration risk as many, potentially high-risk, forecasting applications are exposed to the same biases and failure modes of a single time series foundation model. At the same time, this centralization allows for economies of scale in model development and validation. In this study we investigate how biases and failure modes of time series foundation models can be identified before deployment. We propose a causal analysis framework to investigate the ability of a time series foundation model to preserve time series patterns. To achieve this, we intervene on parameterized synthetic time series generators and measure the corresponding change in model output under ceteris paribus conditions. We apply our causal analysis framework to Chronos-2 and TimesFM-2.5 and test them across six distinct time series patterns. We find safe configurations for trend and harmonic oscillation patterns. The results also indicate a bias in both models towards overestimating persistence, sudden failures for both models against the regime switch pattern and failure for TimesFM-2.5 against the energy-release pattern. Our review of the original works for both models indicates that the findings might be explained by the data used for pretraining. We conclude our study with suggestions for further model development, recommendations for application-specific model selection, and a discussion of limitations and further research directions.

</details>


### [165] [A Structural FHMM for Interpretable Disease Trajectories in T2DM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24328)
*Alessandro Mari,Ekaterina Krymova,Guillaume Obozinski,Maria Luisa Marques de Sa Faquetti,Adrian Martinez de la Torre,Andrea Burden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A structural Factorial Hidden Markov Model (FHMM) is proposed to analyze Type 2 diabetes disease trajectories using EHR data, identifying multiple clinically coherent latent components and heterogeneous progression pathways.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To better understand disease trajectories in Type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) by capturing the complex longitudinal structure of patient health states and their evolution over time, particularly focusing on comorbidities and disease progression patterns.

Method: The authors propose a structural variant of the Factorial Hidden Markov Model (FHMM) that represents a patient's latent health state as a combination of multiple independent, simultaneously evolving components associated with comorbidities and lab results. This allows for structured latent representation and identification of clinically meaningful patient states.

Result: The model identifies multiple clinically coherent latent components corresponding to known patterns of diabetes-related complications. It reveals heterogeneous progression pathways, including distinct microvascular-dominant and multi-organ trajectories associated with elevated comorbidity burden and mortality, using data from IQVIA Medical Research Data (THIN database).

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully captures meaningful longitudinal structure in EHR data and provides interpretable insights into the evolution of T2DM and its comorbidities, demonstrating its utility for understanding complex disease trajectories in clinical practice.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a structural variant of the Factorial Hidden Markov Model (FHMM) for the analysis of disease trajectories in patients with Type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). The model represents a patient's latent health state as a combination of multiple independent, simultaneously evolving components, associated with comorbidities and lab results. This structured latent representation facilitates the identification of clinically meaningful patient states and clustering of common disease trajectories. We evaluate the proposed approach using The IQVIA Medical Research Data incorporating data from THIN, a Cegedim database of anonymized electronic health records (EHR), identifying patients with a first-ever prescription for a non-insulin antidiabetic drug (NIAD) between January 2006 and December 2019. The model identifies multiple clinically coherent latent components corresponding to known patterns of diabetes-related complications and reveals heterogeneous progression pathways, including distinct microvascular-dominant and multi-organ trajectories associated with elevated comorbidity burden and mortality. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework captures meaningful longitudinal structure in EHR data and provides interpretable insights into the evolution of T2DM and its comorbidities.

</details>


### [166] [When Does Self-Supervised Pretraining Help Tabular Models? A Study of Label Scarcity and Missing Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24381)
*Sahand Mazrouei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mask-and-recover SSL for tabular data shows modest benefits under label scarcity but lacks statistical significance, with best improvements on clean datasets rather than those with inherent missingness, though it provides some robustness to test-time missingness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-supervised learning (SSL) for tabular data needs evaluation under extreme label scarcity and test-time missingness, as these realistic scenarios are under-explored despite SSL's promise.

Method: Evaluated mask-and-recover SSL pretraining objective across 14 diverse classification tasks, comparing against: 1) training from scratch, 2) classical baselines (Random Forest), 3) test-time missingness scenarios (MCAR and MNAR), and 4) three established tabular SSL baselines (VIME, SCARF, SubTab) with identical encoder architecture.

Result: 1) SSL modestly outperforms training from scratch (0.8954 vs 0.9015 AUC at 10% labels) but lacks statistical significance (p=0.626); 2) SSL works best on clean datasets, not those with high inherent missingness; 3) SSL provides better robustness to test-time missingness (+0.0245 AUC for MCAR, +0.0418 for MNAR) but not statistically significant after correction; 4) No significant difference between mask-and-recover and established SSL baselines.

Conclusion: Tabular SSL's benefits are modest and lack statistical significance under label scarcity, with findings reflecting general properties of tabular SSL rather than specific pretext tasks, highlighting the need for more robust SSL approaches for real-world tabular data challenges.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) has emerged as a promising approach for tabular data, yet its efficacy under extreme label scarcity and test-time missingness remains under-explored. In this paper, we evaluate a mask-and-recover SSL pretraining objective against training from scratch and classical baselines across 14 diverse classification tasks. First, while SSL outperforms training from scratch on average and remains competitive with state-of-the-art tree ensembles (achieving ~0.8954 AUC vs. Random Forest's 0.9015 at 10% labels), the SSL-vs-scratch gains exhibit high inter-task variance and lack significance (p = 0.626 at both 5% and 10% labels). Second, contrary to the hypothesis that missing-value imputation objectives universally benefit datasets with native missingness, SSL yields the most reliable improvements on clean datasets, while frequently degrading performance on datasets with high inherent missingness. Third, despite this training variance, SSL-pretrained models achieve a higher average AUC than scratch-trained models under both test-time missingness completely at random (MCAR) injection (+0.0245 AUC, positive on 11 of 14 tasks) and structured missingness shifts (MNAR, +0.0418 AUC, positive on 8 of 14 tasks), though neither difference remains statistically significant after Holm-Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons (adjusted p = 0.118 and p = 0.518, respectively). Fourth, comparing our mask-and-recover objective against three established tabular SSL baselines (VIME, SCARF, SubTab) under an identical encoder architecture, we find no significant difference from any of them (adjusted p = 0.459, p = 1.000, p = 1.000), indicating our findings reflect general properties of tabular SSL rather than idiosyncrasies of one particular pretext task.

</details>


### [167] [Equivariant Covariance Tensors: Guaranteed SPD Uncertainty for Tensor-Valued Geometric Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24386)
*Ruihan Liu,Yu Ji,Jianbo Yu,Shifu Yan,Qingchao Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An E(3)-equivariant uncertainty quantification framework for symmetric rank-2 tensor prediction that models full predictive distributions with rotationally symmetric means and covariances, using Lie algebra to SPD manifold mapping and a Log-Euclidean Equivariant Scoring Objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tensor-valued prediction is fundamental to geometric deep learning, but uncertainty quantification for such outputs remains an open challenge. While E(3)-equivariant neural networks excel at point estimates, they lack rigorous confidence measures, particularly for symmetric rank-2 tensors where uncertainty should be represented by a 6×6 covariance matrix.

Method: The framework models full predictive distributions where both mean and covariance preserve rotational symmetry. It decomposes covariance into irreducible representations (2×l=0 ⊕ 2×l=2 ⊕ 1×l=4), maps from flat Lie algebra 𝔰𝔶𝔪(6) to curved SPD manifold via matrix exponentiation for positive-definite covariances, and uses a Log-Euclidean Equivariant Scoring Objective (LE-ESO) based on Multivariate Laplace distribution for robustness to heavy-tailed errors.

Result: Validation on ModelNet40 inertia tensors and Materials Project dielectric tensors shows competitive performance with physically consistent, symmetry-preserving uncertainty estimates that provide useful risk assessment and out-of-distribution sensitivity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully addresses the challenge of E(3)-equivariant uncertainty quantification for symmetric rank-2 tensor prediction, providing rigorous confidence measures while maintaining exact rotational symmetry and physical consistency in uncertainty estimates.

Abstract: Tensor-valued prediction is fundamental to geometric deep learning, yet uncertainty quantification (UQ) for such outputs remains an open challenge. While E(3)-equivariant neural networks excel at point estimates, they lack rigorous confidence measures. We focus on symmetric rank-2 tensor prediction, where the target has six Kelvin--Mandel coordinates and full uncertainty is represented by a $6\times6$ covariance matrix. We introduce a framework for E(3)-equivariant UQ, modeling the full predictive distribution where both mean and covariance preserve rotational symmetry. Our approach decomposes the covariance into irreducible representations $\mathrm{Sym}^2(ρ_c) \cong 2\times(l=0) \oplus 2\times(l=2) \oplus 1\times(l=4)$. By mapping from the flat Lie algebra $\mathfrak{sym}(6)$ to the curved SPD manifold via matrix exponentiation, we strictly ensure positive-definite covariances while maintaining exact equivariance. Furthermore, we formulate a Log-Euclidean Equivariant Scoring Objective (LE-ESO)---a robust surrogate loss based on the Multivariate Laplace distribution---providing robustness to heavy-tailed errors and stable optimization. Validation on ModelNet40 inertia tensors and Materials Project dielectric tensors demonstrates that our method achieves competitive performance and provides physically consistent, symmetry-preserving uncertainty estimates with useful risk and OOD sensitivity.

</details>


### [168] [Joint Distribution Alignment for Universal Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24429)
*Shizhe Li,Hongshan Pu,Mengying Xie,Yi Xiang,Xiaowei Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes JAUA, a novel Universal Domain Adaptation algorithm with theoretical generalization error bound, using joint distribution alignment and progressive pseudo-labeling to handle different label spaces between source and target domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional UDA assumes identical label spaces between source and target domains, but real-world applications often have different label spaces, creating Universal Domain Adaptation (UniDA) scenarios with both sample distribution drift and class spatial differences. Existing works lack theoretical analysis for UniDA.

Method: 1) Derives theoretical upper bound for generalization error in UniDA. 2) Proposes JAUA algorithm that aligns joint distributions by minimizing Chi-Square divergence discrepancy. 3) Implements progressive pseudo-labeling method to assign labels to unlabeled target samples.

Result: JAUA demonstrates superiority in handling UniDA problems through experiments on six public image datasets, showing effectiveness in scenarios with different label spaces between domains.

Conclusion: The proposed theoretical bound provides theoretical foundation for UniDA, while JAUA algorithm offers an effective solution for real-world domain adaptation problems with different label spaces through joint distribution alignment and progressive labeling.

Abstract: Unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) has been widely concerned in the fields of machine learning, pattern recognition, and computer vision. Traditional UDA learning usually assumes that the label spaces of the source and target domains are exactly the same and only needs to solve the problem of sample distribution drift existing between two domains. However, in real world applications, the label spaces between two domains may be different. In this case, there are both sample distribution drift and class spatial difference between domains, namely Universal Domain Adaptation (UniDA) learning scenario. At present, existing works rarely offer theoretical analysis for universal domain adaptation. In this paper, we provide an upper bound of the generalization error for universal domain adaptation. According to the proposed generalization error bound, we propose a novel UniDA algorithm called Joint Distribution Alignment for Universal Domain Adaptation (JAUA), which aligns the joint distributions by minimizing the distribution discrepancy calculated by Chi-Square divergence. Furthermore, we propose a progressive pseudo-labeling method to assign the pseudo labels to unlabeled target samples. The experiment results on six public image datasets demonstrate the superiority of JAUA in handling the UniDA problem.

</details>


### [169] [Evaluating Deep Multivariate Imputation Models on Wearable Device Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24436)
*Skye Goodman,Roussel Desmond Nzoyem,Leandro Junges,Peter Kissack,Yasser Qureshi,Amberly Brigden,Jeff Clark,Nawid Keshtmand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed new evaluation protocol for imputation methods on wearable sensor data that preserves realistic structured missingness patterns, showing model rankings depend heavily on evaluation design and no single method dominates all scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evaluation methods for imputation use random-point holdout protocols that incorrectly assume independent missingness across features and time, failing to capture the realistic structured missingness patterns in wearable sensor data where features sharing physical sensors drop out together.

Method: Developed an evaluation protocol that mines contiguous missing-run templates from training data, stratifies them by per-feature gap-length quantiles, and injects them as block masks preserving co-missingness structure. Also proposed a matched training protocol exposing models to the same missingness distribution, and extended BRITS with time-of-day encoding and circadian harmonic channels.

Result: Matched training protocol reduced BRITS's severe-gap MAE by 43%. No single model dominated: linear interpolation was best for slow-moving features over short gaps; extended BRITS performed better on dynamic cardiac features in moderate/severe gaps; SAITS better preserved ground-truth distribution by Jensen-Shannon distance despite higher MAE. Model rankings strongly depend on evaluation designs.

Conclusion: Traditional evaluation methods obscure true model capabilities. The proposed transferable protocol establishes critical steps toward developing better imputation strategies for multi-sensor wearable datasets by exposing how model performance depends on realistic missingness patterns.

Abstract: Wearable device data enables continuous health monitoring, but suffers from structured missingness: features sharing a physical sensor drop out together. Deep imputation methods such as BRITS and SAITS have seen limited evaluation on multimodal physiological data under realistic missingness, and existing benchmarks use random-point holdout protocols that incorrectly assume missingness is independent across features and time. Using data from a person with epilepsy recorded on a Garmin smartwatch, we develop an evaluation protocol that mines contiguous missing-run templates from training data, stratifies them by per-feature gap-length quantiles, and injects them as block masks with preserved co-missingness structure. A matched training protocol exposing models to the same missingness distribution reduces BRITS's severe-gap MAE by 43%, demonstrating the potential benefit of the proposed evaluation and training protocol within this single-participant dataset. We further extend BRITS with time-of-day encoding and a circadian harmonic channel. No single model dominates: linear interpolation is optimal for slow-moving features over short gaps; extended BRITS achieves lower MAE on dynamic cardiac features in moderate and severe gaps; and SAITS better preserves the ground-truth distribution by Jensen-Shannon distance despite higher MAE. Ultimately, model rankings strongly depend on evaluation designs. By exposing how traditional evaluation methods obscure true model capabilities, our transferable protocol establishes critical steps towards developing better imputation strategies for future multi-sensor wearable datasets.

</details>


### [170] [WarpSAC: Towards the Pinnacle of Scalable Off-policy RL by Rethinking Exploration and Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24479)
*Zihao Wu,Hongyao Tang,Yi Ma,Huizhong Song,Pengyi Li,Yifu Yuan,Fei Ni,Jinyi Liu,Wei Wei,Jianrong Wang,Yan Zheng,Jianye Hao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WarpSAC adapts off-policy RL stabilizers to data regimes: uses Sample Weight Decay and offers variants for CPU-limited (Norm ON, clipped double-Q) vs GPU-parallel abundant data (Norm OFF, single-Q), improving performance significantly over FlashSAC across various environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Massively parallel simulation creates abundant data regimes that challenge existing off-policy RL stabilizers designed for data-limited scenarios, requiring regime-aware adaptation for optimal performance.

Method: Through controlled experiments across 8 benchmark families, identified data-regime-dependent stabilizer effects. Proposed WarpSAC with Sample Weight Decay and two variants: WarpSAC-L for data-limited CPU training (parameter normalization ON, clipped double-Q) and WarpSAC-A for data-abundant GPU-parallel training (parameter normalization OFF, single-Q).

Result: WarpSAC improves normalized score-step AUC over FlashSAC by 4.5% across 9 CPU environments and 23.1% across 14 GPU-parallel environments. Achieves 96.4% success rate on UnitreeG1TransportBox-v1 (vs 19.8%), 19.1% better mean normalized wall-time AUC on MuJoCo Playground, and 36.4% faster sim-to-real deployment on Unitree G1.

Conclusion: Scalable off-policy RL should adapt its stabilizers to the available data regime, with different configurations optimal for data-limited vs data-abundant training scenarios.

Abstract: Massively parallel simulation changes the data regime in which off-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is trained, challenging stabilizers designed for data-limited replay. Through controlled experiments across eight benchmark families, we show that these stabilizers are data-regime-dependent: parameter normalization helps with narrow replay coverage but restricts value fitting when data are abundant, while clipped double-Q can be relaxed in high-throughput manipulation. Age-biased replay weighting improves learning efficiency across regimes, especially with limited network capacity.
  Based on these findings, we propose WarpSAC, a regime-aware family of off-policy RL algorithms. WarpSAC uses Sample Weight Decay for efficient exploitation and provides two variants: WarpSAC-L (Norm ON, clipped double-Q) for data-limited CPU-scale training, and WarpSAC-A (Norm OFF, single-Q) for data-abundant GPU-parallel training. WarpSAC improves normalized score--step AUC over FlashSAC by 4.5% across nine CPU-scale environments and 23.1% across fourteen GPU-parallel environments. It increases UnitreeG1TransportBox-v1 success rate from 19.8% to 96.4%, improves mean normalized wall-time AUC on MuJoCo Playground by 19.1%, and achieves 36.4% faster sim-to-real deployment on Unitree G1 than FlashSAC. These results show that scalable off-policy RL should adapt its stabilizers to the available data regime.

</details>


### [171] [Beyond Static Interpretability: Anticipating Post-SFT Mechanisms from Pre-SFT Parameters for Better Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24482)
*Hang Chen,Jiaying Zhu,Wenya Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mechanistic Localization bridges interpretability and optimization, but existing retrospective approaches fail for novel tasks. The paper proposes a forward-looking framework that predicts post-tuning interpretability states using only pre-tuning parameters and target data, enabling accurate localization before training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional mechanistic interpretability is retrospective and fails for novel tasks because directly interpreting pre-SFT models leads to misleading conclusions. Initial neurons identified differ drastically from those governing the final model, introducing biases that disrupt supervised fine-tuning.

Method: Proposes a forward-looking localization framework that models SFT as continuous parameter evolution using Taylor expansion to bridge post-tuning mechanistic objectives with pre-SFT dynamic gradients. Designs dual-granularity (neuron- and component-level) localization pipelines to accurately estimate post-SFT interpretability state using only pre-SFT parameters and target dataset.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate superior SFT guidance, robust performance, and temporal scalability across increasing model sizes. The approach provides accurate localization that effectively guides parameter-efficient supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: This work transcends the fundamental limitation of traditional interpretability - its inability to identify task-critical mechanisms before they are trained - pioneering a predictive frontier that unites mechanistic interpretability with targeted optimization.

Abstract: Mechanistic Localization bridges mechanistic interpretability and post-training optimization by isolating critical parameters via interpretative approaches and then guiding parameter-efficient Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) in a ``locating-then-tuning'' paradigm. However, due to the retrospective nature of mechanistic interpretability, directly interpreting pre-SFT models introduces misleading conclusions. Specifically for novel tasks, initially identified neurons differ drastically from those governing the final model, introducing biases that actively disrupt SFT. To address this, we propose a forward-looking localization framework that accurately estimates the post-SFT interpretability state using only pre-SFT parameters and the target dataset. Theoretically, we model SFT as a continuous parameter evolution, leveraging Taylor expansion to rigorously bridge the post-tuning mechanistic objective with the pre-SFT model's dynamic gradients. Practically, we design dual-granularity (neuron- and component-level) localization pipelines. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach not only provides superior SFT guidance but also exhibits robust performance and temporal scalability across increasing model sizes. This work transcends the fundamental limitation of traditional interpretability-its inability to identify task-critical mechanisms before they are trained-pioneering a predictive frontier that unites mechanistic interpretability with targeted optimization.

</details>


### [172] [Where Entropy Is Measured Matters: Policy Geometry in Bounded Continuous-Control PPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24488)
*Yiyang He,Zhichun Zhou,Ziwei Wang,Tao Xue,Haolin Fei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that where entropy is measured (in latent action space vs. executed action space) significantly affects policy geometry in bounded-action reinforcement learning, with executed-action entropy creating more centered policies while latent entropy leads to actions near boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many continuous-control policies use unbounded Gaussians that are then mapped to bounded actions via clipping or tanh transforms. The authors investigate how the choice of measuring entropy (in latent action space vs. executed action space) affects learned policy geometry in bounded-action settings.

Method: The study uses proximal policy optimization (PPO) on two tasks: an 80-muscle MyoLeg task and a 38-dimensional Dog-Stand task. They analyze policy geometry by comparing three conditions: latent Gaussian entropy H(u), executed-action entropy H(a), and no entropy. They conduct same-state decomposition to separate mean and variance effects, and validate findings with an independent CleanRL-based PPO implementation.

Result: Latent entropy policies execute 71.42% of actions near boundaries, while no entropy policies show 29.76%, and executed-action entropy policies only 18.83%. Clipping produces extreme near-boundary behavior (89.07% within 5% of bounds). The geometry difference persists across tasks, implementations, and boundary margins from 1% to 10%. Direct mean penalties can match executed entropy's centering effect, showing interior means aren't unique to executed entropy.

Conclusion: Entropy measurement space is a coupled mean-variance design choice that significantly affects bounded-policy geometry independently of task return. Executed-action entropy creates more centered policies, while latent entropy leads to boundary-hugging behavior. Task return alone doesn't characterize bounded-policy geometry.
