Abstract: Many continuous-control policies are optimized as unbounded Gaussians and then mapped into bounded actions. We show that where entropy is measured changes the policy geometry learned by proximal policy optimization (PPO). In an 80-muscle MyoLeg task, a clipped Gaussian executes 89.07% of actions within 5% of a bound. A same-state decomposition shows that this is not due to variance alone: setting variance to zero still leaves 83.83% of actions near a bound, while 82.12% of state-conditioned means lie outside the executable interval. Replacing clipping with a tanh map does not remove the high-variance regime. For latent Gaussian entropy H(u), the entropy loss has zero gradient with respect to the mean and a constant variance-increasing gradient. For executed-action entropy H(a), the transform Jacobian adds an inward gradient on the mean. Across three matched MyoLeg seeds, near-boundary occupancy is 71.42%, 29.76%, and 18.83% under latent entropy, no entropy, and executed-action entropy. A 38-dimensional Dog-Stand replication with an independent CleanRL-based PPO implementation reproduces the ordering in mean geometry, which also survives shared-state evaluation and boundary margins from 1% to 10%. Direct mean penalties can match or exceed the centering produced by H(a), showing that interior means are not unique to executed entropy. However, matched mean geometry can coexist with substantially different variance and return. Entropy measurement space is therefore a coupled mean-variance design choice, and task return alone does not characterize bounded-policy geometry.

</details>


### [173] [When Do Supervised UQ Ensembles Improve LLM Hallucination Detection? A Robustness Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24492)
*Mohit Singh Chauhan,Vipin Gyanchandani,Dylan Bouchard*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Supervised ensembles of uncertainty quantification methods effectively detect LLM hallucinations across models, datasets, and generation tasks using minimal labeled data, maintaining robustness under distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has proposed combining uncertainty quantification (UQ) signals via learned ensembles for hallucination detection in LLMs, but there's limited empirical investigation into the robustness of these ensembles across different settings.

Method: A supervised ensembling framework that trains a classifier over heterogeneous UQ-based scorer outputs on small domain-specific datasets of labeled LLM responses, applied to out-of-sample hallucination classification without retrieval, tools, or reference documents.

Result: Supervised ensembles outperform the best individual scorer in 30 of 32 settings, with gains from as few as 100 labeled instances. They maintain advantage in 23 of 28 transfer settings under distribution shift. Sampling-based black-box ensembles are nearly as effective as full ensembles.

Conclusion: Supervised ensembles of UQ methods provide robust hallucination detection across diverse LLM settings with minimal labeled data, though single-generation white-box ensembles offer limited benefits compared to sampling-based approaches.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods are widely used for hallucination detection in large language models (LLMs) in closed-book settings where ground-truth evidence is unavailable at inference time. Prior work has proposed combining UQ signals via learned ensembles, but empirical investigations into the robustness of these ensembles are limited. We study a supervised ensembling framework that trains a classifier over heterogeneous UQ-based scorer outputs on a small, domain-specific dataset of labeled LLM responses, then applies it to out-of-sample hallucination classification without retrieval, tools, or reference documents. Across four LLMs, nine datasets, and three generation regimes (short-form QA, long-form generation, and code generation), we provide a systematic robustness analysis along three axes: sample efficiency, in-domain dataset transfer, and generation regime dependence. We find that supervised ensembles outperform the best individual scorer in 30 of 32 settings, with gains realized from as few as 100 labeled instances. Ensembles retain most of their advantage in cases of in-domain transfer under distribution shift, outperforming the best non-ensemble scorer in 23 of 28 transfer settings. Sampling-based black-box ensembles are nearly as effective as full ensembles, while single-generation white-box ensembles offer limited benefit.

</details>


### [174] [It depends: Incorporating correlations for joint aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties of high-dimensional output spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24518)
*Leonhard F. Feiner,Manuel Nickel,Martin Menten,Laurin Lux,Rickmer Braren,Daniel Rueckert,Georgios Kaissis,Raphael Rehms,Johannes Paetzold*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a novel joint aleatoric-epistemic uncertainty quantification method for high-dimensional regression using low-rank+diagonal covariance structure with stabilization strategies, achieving superior performance on image tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification is crucial for reliable deep learning predictions, especially in high-dimensional outputs like medical imaging. Both aleatoric (data noise) and epistemic (model confidence) uncertainties must be modeled jointly for complete transparency and robustness, as modeling only one type limits reliability in complex applications.

Method: The approach approximates joint uncertainty using a low-rank plus diagonal covariance structure that captures essential output correlations while avoiding computational burdens of full covariance matrices. It explicitly combines aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties into a unified second-order distribution. The method includes stabilization strategies for efficient training and inference.

Result: Achieves superior uncertainty quantification performance in high-dimensional regression tasks including image inpainting, colorization, optical flow, and depth estimation. Supports robust downstream analyses like sampling and log-likelihood evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed joint uncertainty modeling approach with low-rank covariance approximation effectively captures both aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties in high-dimensional regression, providing more reliable predictions while maintaining computational efficiency across various image processing tasks.

Abstract: Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) plays a vital role in enhancing the reliability of deep learning model predictions, especially in scenarios with high-dimensional output spaces. This paper addresses the dual nature of uncertainty -- aleatoric and epistemic -- focusing on their joint integration in high-dimensional regression tasks. For example, in applications like medical image segmentation or restoration, aleatoric uncertainty captures inherent data noise, while epistemic uncertainty quantifies the model's confidence in unfamiliar conditions. Modeling both jointly enables more reliable predictions by reflecting both unavoidable variability and knowledge gaps, whereas modeling only one limits transparency and robustness. We propose a novel approach that approximates the resulting joint uncertainty using a low-rank plus diagonal covariance structure, capturing essential output correlations while avoiding the computational burdens of full covariance matrices. Unlike prior work, our method explicitly combines aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties into a unified second-order distribution that supports robust downstream analyses like sampling and log-likelihood evaluation. We further introduce stabilization strategies for efficient training and inference, achieving superior UQ in the tasks of image inpainting, colorization, optical flow, and depth estimation.

</details>


### [175] [From Numerical Simulators of PDEs to Neural Emulators and Back](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24547)
*Felix Koehler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This thesis argues neural PDE emulators and classical numerical solvers are fundamentally related, provides a common analysis framework using Fourier analysis, and contributes three elements: a benchmark suite, investigation of unconverged solver effects, and analysis of numerical error influences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional numerical solvers for PDEs remain computationally expensive for fast or many-query applications, creating bottlenecks in engineering and science simulations. While neural emulators promise speedups, they're typically treated as black-box alternatives rather than being understood in relation to the classical solvers they emulate.

Method: The author approaches the relationship by disentangling solver roles in the emulator learning pipeline and applying mode-wise Fourier analysis as a common language. This framework allows simultaneous analysis of solver errors, architectural biases, and training objectives. The research implements differentiable pseudo-spectral solvers in JAX and conducts systematic benchmarking and analysis.

Result: Three main contributions: (1) APEBench - a comprehensive benchmarking suite for autoregressive neural PDE emulators using fast differentiable pseudo-spectral solvers; (2) Progressively Refined Differentiable Physics - analysis of how unconverged solvers affect surrogate training; (3) Neural Emulator Superiority - investigation of how numerical errors and architectural inductive biases influence performance.

Conclusion: Neural emulators and classical numerical solvers share fundamental similarities rather than being distinct paradigms. By analyzing them through a unified Fourier analysis framework, researchers can better understand their relationships, improve emulator design, and make more informed trade-offs between accuracy and computational cost in PDE simulation.

Abstract: Simulation is central to modern engineering and science, but the cost of numerical solvers for partial differential equations (PDEs) remains a bottleneck whenever fast or many-query evaluations are required. Neural emulators trained on solver-generated data promise significant speedups, yet they are usually framed as opaque alternatives to the very methods that produce their training signal. This thesis argues the two paradigms are more alike than different: neural architectures mirror classical discretizations, their errors are amenable to the same spectral analysis, and insight flows profitably in both directions. We approach the relationship by disentangling the multiple roles a solver plays in the emulator learning pipeline. Mode-wise Fourier analysis then provides a common language in which solver errors, architectural inductive biases, and training objectives can all be read off simultaneously. Taken together, this allows synthesizing three contributions. (1) APEBench, a comprehensive benchmarking suite for autoregressive neural emulators of PDEs that uses fast differentiable pseudo-spectral solvers in JAX. (2) Progressively Refined Differentiable Physics, an investigation of the effect of unconverged solvers on surrogate training. (3) Neural Emulator Superiority, an analysis of the influence of numerical errors and architectural inductive biases.

</details>


### [176] [Persistent Cross Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24549)
*Sijin Yeom,Jae-Hun Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Persistent Cross Entropy (PCE), a novel extension of cross-entropy to persistence diagrams that bridges different event spaces using induced probabilities from similarity functions and persistence weighting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-entropy cannot be naturally defined for persistence diagrams because they typically have different event spaces, limiting the ability to measure information divergence between topological structures.

Method: Define induced probabilities by combining similarity functions with persistence weighting to bridge event spaces between persistence diagrams. Assign unexplained probability mass to unexplained events, then extend cross-entropy to create Persistent Cross Entropy (PCE).

Result: Prove main properties and stability theorems for both induced probabilities and PCE. Show through three numerical studies that PCE: 1) distinguishes diagrams with same persistent entropy, 2) separates causal directions in dynamical systems without joint persistence diagrams, 3) serves as directional topology loss for knowledge distillation.

Conclusion: Persistent Cross Entropy provides a principled way to measure information divergence between persistence diagrams with different event spaces, enabling new applications in topological data analysis and machine learning.

Abstract: Persistent entropy is the Shannon entropy of a persistence-based probability measure defined on a persistence diagram. However, its cross-entropy version is not naturally defined because two persistence diagrams generally have different event spaces. To bridge these event spaces, we combine a similarity function with persistence weighting to define an induced probability. The induced probability reflects information from one diagram on the event space of the other diagram and assigns unexplained probability mass to the unexplained event. Using the induced probability, we extend cross entropy to persistence diagrams, called persistent cross entropy (PCE). We establish the main properties of both the induced probability and PCE and prove stability theorems for both. Through three numerical studies, we show that PCE distinguishes diagrams with the same persistent entropy, separates causal directions in dynamical systems without constructing a joint persistent diagram, and can be used as a directional topology loss for knowledge distillation.

</details>


### [177] [FraudBench: Protocol-Sensitive Benchmarking of Adversarial Robustness for Financial Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24551)
*Xitong Zeng,Zhaoge Bi,Yitian Yang,Huaming Chen,Quan Z. Sheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FraudBench is a protocol-sensitive benchmark for evaluating adversarial robustness in financial fraud detection that shows robustness conclusions depend heavily on how domain constraints are incorporated during evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current adversarial robustness evaluations for financial fraud detection are inadequate because they don't properly account for domain-specific constraints, severe class imbalance, and asymmetric attacker capabilities, leading to potentially misleading robustness conclusions.

Method: Developed FraudBench, which evaluates adversarial robustness under three matched protocols: (1) unconstrained attacks, (2) post-hoc feasibility filtering, and (3) deployment-aware constraint-integrated attacks. The benchmark covers four public financial datasets and evaluates neural, tree-based, and ensemble models using three attack settings (white-box, black-box).

Result: Robustness conclusions are highly protocol-sensitive. On Lending Club data, post-hoc filtering left only 3.7 feasible-flipped examples, while constraint-integrated attacks produced 2,832.3 feasible-flipped examples under the same perturbation budget. Protocol choice can even alter model-family rankings in black-box evaluations.

Conclusion: Financial fraud robustness evaluation should report both predictive degradation and attack feasibility jointly, and must incorporate domain constraints directly into attack generation rather than treating them as post-processing checks, as robustness depends on evaluation protocol.

Abstract: Machine learning models are widely used in financial fraud and credit-risk detection, yet their adversarial robustness remains difficult to evaluate because financial tabular data involve domain-specific constraints, severe class imbalance, and asymmetric attacker capability. We argue that, in this setting, robustness is not only an attribute of the model, but also an attribute of the evaluation protocol. Different ways of enforcing constraints and capability can lead to substantially different robustness conclusions. This paper presents FraudBench, a protocol-sensitive benchmark for adversarial robustness evaluation in financial fraud and credit-risk detection. Rather than treating domain constraints as post-hoc validity checks, FraudBench evaluates the same dataset--model--attack--defence setting under three matched protocols: unconstrained attacks, post-hoc feasibility filtering, and deployment-aware constraint-integrated attacks. FraudBench covers four public financial datasets, and evaluates neural, tree-based, and ensemble models using three attack settings. Our results show that robustness conclusions are highly protocol-sensitive. On Lending Club Loan Data under the white-box setting, post-hoc filtering leaves only 3.7 feasible-flipped examples on average, whereas in-attack projection with attacker mutability masking produces 2,832.3 feasible-flipped examples under the same perturbation budget. The results on IEEE-CIS further show that feasibility and attacker capability are separate axes, while black-box evaluation shows that protocol choice can alter model-family rankings. These findings suggest that fraud robustness evaluation should report predictive degradation and attack feasibility jointly, and should incorporate domain constraints into attack generation rather than treating them as post-processing checks.

</details>


### [178] [SeisMamba: Low-Latency Single-Station Seismic Magnitude Estimation for Spatially Distributed Earthquake Early Warning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24561)
*Quenton Yeo,Zhaoge Bi,Linghan Huang,Luke Stephen Higgins,Flora Salim,Huaming Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SeisMamba: A lightweight Mamba-based architecture for fast, single-station earthquake magnitude estimation that outperforms transformer baselines in speed and accuracy while maintaining useful cross-region generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current earthquake early warning systems rely on dense regional seismic networks and region-specific calibration, creating coverage barriers for high-risk areas with sparse sensing infrastructure. Single-station alternatives face accuracy-latency trade-offs and degrade under regional distribution shift.

Method: SeisMamba uses hierarchical convolutional encoding, sparse selective state-space modeling (Mamba architecture), multi-scale feature fusion, and auxiliary temporal prediction head for efficient long-sequence waveform analysis from minimally processed three-component seismic waveforms at single stations.

Result: On STEAD benchmark: Best MSE, RMSE, and R² among baselines; only 0.55ms for batch of 32 waveforms on NVIDIA T4 GPU (3x faster than transformers). In Chile-Taiwan regional hold-out: retains useful performance on geographically unseen seismic regions.

Conclusion: Selective state-space waveform modeling provides promising accuracy-latency backbone for spatially distributed, low-cost earthquake early warning systems that can overcome coverage barriers in sparse sensing regions.

Abstract: Rapid earthquake magnitude estimation is central to earthquake early warning, yet many operational systems depend on dense regional seismic networks and region-specific calibration. This creates a spatial coverage barrier for high-risk areas with sparse sensing infrastructure. Single-station learning offers a lower-cost alternative, but existing models often face an accuracy--latency trade-off and may degrade under regional distribution shift. We present SeisMamba, a lightweight Mamba-based architecture for low-latency magnitude estimation from minimally processed three-component seismic waveforms recorded at a single station. SeisMamba combines hierarchical convolutional encoding, sparse selective state-space modelling, multi-scale feature fusion, and an auxiliary temporal prediction head to support efficient long-sequence waveform analysis. On the STEAD benchmark, SeisMamba achieves the best MSE, RMSE, and $R^2$ among tested baselines while requiring only 0.55 ms for a batch of 32 waveforms on an NVIDIA T4 GPU, making it about three times faster than transformer-based baselines. We further conduct a Chile--Taiwan regional hold-out experiment as a diagnostic test of cross-region deployment, where SeisMamba retains useful performance on geographically unseen seismic regions. These results suggest that selective state-space waveform modelling provides a promising accuracy--latency backbone for spatially distributed, low-cost earthquake early warning.

</details>


### [179] [Across the Loss Landscape with Progressive Growth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24568)
*Paul Caillon,Christophe Cerisara,Alexandre Allauzen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes "incremental grow-and-optimize" training where neural networks are expanded from small submodels to full size via progressive constraint relaxation, showing this biases solutions toward flatter minima through volume effects in parameter space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks generalize well despite nonconvex, overparameterized loss landscapes. Understanding why stochastic optimization finds good minima and the relationship between flatness and generalization remains unclear. The paper aims to explain how training strategies bias solutions toward certain types of minima.

Method: Progressive subspace growth: Start with low-dimensional submodel, iteratively expand trainable parameters by unlocking nested random subspaces while freezing orthogonal complement at initialization, re-optimizing after each expansion until reaching full architecture. Use geometric analysis under regularity conditions to characterize basin accessibility via effective curvature in frozen directions.

Result: Progressive growth increases relative weight of wide basins and suppresses sharp ones through volume effect induced by frozen constraints. Empirically validated in toy landscapes and ResNet/CIFAR-100 settings - reliably produces flatter solutions but curvature reductions don't universally translate to improved test performance.

Conclusion: Incremental grow-and-optimize strategies bias training toward flatter minima through progressive constraint relaxation and volume effects, revealing subtleties in the flatness-generalization connection and suggesting flatness alone doesn't guarantee better generalization.

Abstract: Deep neural networks generalize well despite their highly nonconvex, overparameterized loss landscapes, a phenomenon often associated with the geometry of the minima found by stochastic optimization. We study how incremental grow-and-optimize strategies bias training toward flatter regions by viewing growth as progressive constraint relaxation. Starting from a low-dimensional submodel, we iteratively expand the trainable parameters by unlocking nested random subspaces while freezing the orthogonal complement at the network initialization, re-optimizing after each expansion until the full architecture is reached. Under standard local regularity conditions around non-degenerate minima, we prove that local sublevel sets are well approximated by ellipsoids and that basin accessibility under frozen constraints can be characterized by an explicit effective curvature in the frozen directions. This leads to an explanation of the bias: progressive growth increases the relative weight of wide basins and suppresses sharp ones through a volume effect induced by the frozen constraints. We empirically validate these predictions in controlled toy landscapes and in a realistic ResNet/CIFAR-100 setting and confirm that although progressive subspace growth reliably produces flatter solutions, curvature reductions do not universally translate into improved test performance, highlighting subtleties in the flatness-generalization connection. The code is available at https://github.com/p0lcAi/Across-the-Loss-Landscape.

</details>


### [180] [IAPO: Influence-Aware Policy Optimization for Credit Assignment in Multi-Turn Service Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24588)
*Bo Ren,Yirong Mao,Yi Yang,Wenhui Que*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IAPO is a novel policy optimization method that addresses credit assignment in multi-turn LLM agent interactions by modeling influence-dependency graphs from rollouts and redistributing trajectory-level advantages based on support-use and failed-use patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn LLM agent tasks, especially service agents, task information unfolds over time rather than being fully specified initially. Users clarify goals, tools provide needed information, and the final reward alone cannot indicate which specific actions contributed to task resolution. Current methods rely on comparative evidence from other trajectories or separate step-level signals, but a completed rollout already contains rich information about how information and errors flow between actions.

Method: IAPO (Influence-Aware Policy Optimization) represents each rollout as a typed influence-dependency graph over trainable agent actions, with user and tool observations as evidence. It converts support-use and failed-use structure into routing weights that redistribute the same trajectory-level advantage, enabling better credit assignment from sparse outcome feedback.

Result: Experiments with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B demonstrate superior performance over multi-turn RL baselines across three service-agent benchmarks: τ²-Bench, UserBench, and AgentChangeBench. BFCL-v4 Multi-Turn further shows these gains don't compromise multi-turn function-calling performance.

Conclusion: This work advances understanding of credit assignment in multi-turn user interactions and provides a principled approach to training service agents from sparse outcome feedback, improving LLM agent performance in complex, multi-turn service scenarios.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly solve long-horizon tasks through multi-turn interactions with users and external tools. In these settings, relevant task information often unfolds over time rather than being fully specified at the initial prompt. Service agents make this challenge especially concrete: users may clarify or revise their goals, while tool responses provide information needed for subsequent decisions. Thus, a final reward alone cannot indicate which actions contributed to resolving the task. Recent methods rely on comparative evidence from other trajectories or resampled continuations, or on separately constructed step-level learning signals, to refine credit. However, a completed rollout already records how information and errors flow between agent actions. We introduce Influence-Aware Policy Optimization (IAPO), which represents each rollout as a typed influence-dependency graph over trainable agent actions, with user and tool observations serving as evidence. IAPO converts support-use and failed-use structure into routing weights that redistribute the same trajectory-level advantage. Experiments with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B demonstrate superior performance over multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) baselines across three service-agent benchmarks: {τ^2}-Bench, UserBench, and AgentChangeBench. BFCL-v4 Multi-Turn further shows that these gains do not compromise multi-turn function-calling performance. This work advances the understanding of credit assignment in multi-turn user interactions and provides a principled approach to training service agents from sparse outcome feedback.

</details>


### [181] [Delayed Optimizer-State Transport Shapes Short-Horizon Training Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24593)
*Jinhui Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive optimizer memory effects cause short-horizon loss changes, enabling targeted interventions through future data scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive optimizers like AdamW retain gradient history in moment variables, which creates delayed transport effects that could influence short-horizon training decisions. The paper investigates whether these optimizer memory effects are significant enough to warrant consideration in training interventions.

Method: The researchers differentiate eight-step AdamW trajectories through complete model-optimizer states, selecting exposure-matched Math-Code loss schedules. They test on 12 unused 0.3M Transformer histories, compare full transport against optimizer-aware immediate derivatives, and conduct crossed checkpoint-future-path tests to attribute effects to optimizer state-data interactions. An independent Ising-CNN experiment validates the importance of moment-state transport.

Result: Full transport lowered token-disjoint loss in 10/12 histories with mean benefit of 4.71×10⁻⁴ (p=0.0193). Controllers selected different schedules in 60/96 windows despite equal action frequency. Crossed tests confirmed optimizer state-near-future data interactions drive schedule reordering. Deleting moment-state transport destroyed accurate response prediction in Ising-CNN experiment. Full-transport scores effectively identified winners in larger candidate libraries.

Conclusion: Optimizer memory and near-future data ordering are actionable components of training state. This provides a mechanism-based criterion for determining when finite-horizon interventions (rather than one-step approaches) are required for effective training optimization on committed short paths.

Abstract: Adaptive optimizers retain gradient history in moment variables, allowing a local change in loss weighting to alter later updates. We examine whether this delayed transport is large enough to change prospective short-horizon decisions. On committed future-minibatch sequences, we differentiate eight-step AdamW trajectories through the complete model--optimizer state and select exposure-matched Math--Code loss schedules before independent evaluation. Across 12 unused 0.3M Transformer histories, full transport lowers token-disjoint loss relative to an optimizer-aware immediate derivative in 10/12 histories (mean benefit $4.71\times10^{-4}$; exact one-sided sign test, $p=0.0193$). The two controllers act equally often but select different schedules in 60/96 windows. Crossed checkpoint--future-path tests attribute this reordering to the interaction between optimizer state and near-future data, while an independent Ising--CNN experiment shows that deleting moment-state transport destroys accurate response prediction. Full-transport scores also concentrate exact-rollout winners in larger candidate libraries, focusing finite-amplitude evaluation on a shortlist. On these committed short paths, optimizer memory and near-future data order are therefore actionable components of the training state, providing a mechanism-based criterion for when finite-horizon rather than one-step intervention is required.

</details>


### [182] [Taming foundation model with invariance-oriented pre-training for broad-spectrum EEG analysis across signal-level, brain-state, and brain-health tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24597)
*Yulong Dou,Han Wu,Guo Chen,Fangmao Ju,Zhiming Cui,Dinggang Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: INCEPT is an invariance-oriented EEG foundation model trained on 11,000+ hours of clinical EEG that learns stable neural representations across correlated observations, outperforming recent EEG foundation models on comprehensive benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most EEG models follow a one-dataset-one-model supervised paradigm, while existing EEG foundation models focus on reconstruction, assuming predictable EEG content from local context equals transferable neural information. This paper aims to develop a more transferable foundation model by focusing on invariance learning.

Method: INCEPT uses invariance-oriented pre-training on over 11,000 hours of unlabelled clinical EEG, learning representation-level stability across correlated EEG observations to separate stable neural structure and subject-sensitive information from nuisance variability while preserving discriminative information.

Result: INCEPT ranks first among recent EEG foundation models on 26/30 linear-probing metrics and 24/30 fine-tuning metrics, and surpasses strong task-specific specialist encoders across diverse downstream settings. It shows improved transfer and organizes subject-sensitive neural representations beyond reconstruction alone.

Conclusion: Invariance learning is a promising principle for building reusable EEG foundation models, offering better transfer and organization of neural representations compared to reconstruction-centered approaches.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is a widely used window into human brain function, but most EEG models remain tied to a one-dataset-one-model supervised paradigm. Recent EEG foundation models offer a route toward reusable representations, but most remain reconstruction-centered, assuming that EEG content predictable from local context is necessarily transferable neural information. Here we present INCEPT, an invariance-oriented EEG foundation model trained on over 11,000 hours of unlabelled clinical EEG. Rather than prioritizing signal recovery alone, INCEPT learns representation-level stability across correlated EEG observations, separating stable neural structure and essential subject-sensitive information from the nuisance variability that dominates scalp recordings while preserving subject-, state- and condition-discriminative information. We evaluate INCEPT on a broad-spectrum benchmark of ten datasets spanning three levels of post-acquisition EEG analysis: signal-level assessment, brain-state decoding, and brain-health evaluation. INCEPT ranks first among recent EEG foundation models on 26 of 30 linear-probing metrics and 24 of 30 fine-tuning metrics, and also surpasses strong task-specific specialist encoders across diverse downstream settings. Objective ablations and representation analyses further show that invariance-oriented pre-training improves transfer and organizes subject-sensitive neural representations beyond reconstruction alone. These results establish invariance learning as a promising principle for building reusable EEG foundation models.

</details>


### [183] [Conditional GraphGANFed: Optimizing Graph-Structured Molecule Generation in Federated Generative Adversarial Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24610)
*Daniel Manu,Abee Alazzwi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: cGraphGANFed extends GraphGANFed by adding a critic network to optimize user-defined metrics in molecule generation, outperforming the baseline on multiple metrics while improving resilience to mode collapse and non-IID data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GraphGANFed combines GANs with federated learning for privacy-preserving molecular discovery but lacks the ability to optimize specific user-defined metrics, which is crucial for targeted drug discovery applications.

Method: The authors propose conditional GraphGANFed (cGraphGANFed) by incorporating a critic network to evaluate generated molecules based on user-defined metrics, integrating both critic and discriminator evaluations into the generator's loss function to guide optimization of desired properties while maintaining chemical similarity to real molecules.

Result: In two scenarios: 1) optimizing all seven common metrics, cGraphGANFed significantly outperforms GraphGANFed in Validity and LogP with slight advantages in QED; 2) focusing solely on QED optimization, cGraphGANFed achieves >10% improvement in QED over GraphGANFed. The method also shows enhanced resilience against mode collapse and performance reduction from non-IID data.

Conclusion: cGraphGANFed successfully addresses the limitation of GraphGANFed by enabling targeted optimization of user-defined metrics while maintaining privacy through federated learning, making it a valuable extension for drug discovery applications requiring specific molecular property optimization.

Abstract: Generative adversarial networks (GANs) have garnered considerable attention in molecular discovery for their ability to generate novel and high-quality molecules. To efficiently train a GAN model while preserving data privacy, GraphGANFed has been proposed to incorporate federated learning and graph convolutional networks into GAN. Yet, GraphGANFed cannot produce synthetic molecules that only optimize a user-defined metric(s) to facilitate the new drug discovery process. To address this issue, we introduce a novel extension to GraphGANFed, namely conditional GraphGANFed (cGraphGANFed), by incorporating the critic network to assess generated molecules using user-defined metric(s). The evaluation results from both the critic network and discriminator are integrated into the loss function of the generator, guiding it to generate novel molecules that maintain similar chemical properties to real ones while optimizing user-defined metrics. Extensive simulations are conducted in two scenarios. First, cGraphGANFed endeavors to optimize all seven commonly used metrics, and the results show that cGraphGANFed significantly outperforms GraphGANFed in Validity and LogP, with a slight advantage in QED, across different settings. Second, cGraphGANFed focuses solely on optimizing QED, and the results show that the synthetic molecules produced by cGraphGANFed can achieve more than 10% improvement in QED than GraphGANFed. Also, the results demonstrate cGraphGANFed has enhanced resilience against mode collapses and performance reduction caused by non-IID data.

</details>


### [184] [Bandit Submodular Maximization under Matroid Constraints: Learning Compressed Exchange Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24627)
*Zongqi Wan,Zhijie Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First sublinear-regret algorithm for adversarial bandit submodular maximization under matroid constraints, achieving (1-1/e)-regret of Õ(n^{1/3}k^{2/3}T^{2/3}) with polynomial time complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for efficient algorithms for adversarial bandit maximization of monotone submodular functions under general matroid constraints, where previous solutions had exponential time/space requirements.

Method: Views problem as learning exchange policy for Poisson base walk, connects to contextual bandits, then introduces balanced fractional exchanges to compress policy mixture into single fractional base while retaining exchange information, enabling polynomial time algorithm.

Result: Develops randomized oracle-polynomial algorithm making one feasible value query per round with expected (1-1/e)-regret Õ(n^{1/3}k^{2/3}T^{2/3}) for rank-k matroid on n elements.

Conclusion: Achieves first sublinear-regret algorithm for adversarial bandit submodular maximization under general matroid constraints with polynomial time complexity, solving computational barriers of previous approaches.

Abstract: We study adversarial bandit maximization of monotone submodular functions under a matroid constraint. For a rank-$k$ matroid on $n$ elements, we give a randomized oracle-polynomial algorithm that makes one feasible value query per round and has expected $(1-1/e)$-regret $\widetilde O(n^{1/3}k^{2/3}T^{2/3})$. This is the first sublinear-regret algorithm for adversarial bandit submodular maximization under general matroid constraints.
  Technically, we view the problem as learning an exchange policy for the Poisson base walk. This connects the problem to contextual bandits and gives an information-theoretic sublinear-regret guarantee, but directly learning the exponentially many policies requires exponential time and space. We therefore introduce \emph{balanced fractional exchanges}, which compress the policy mixture into a single fractional base while retaining the exchange information needed by the Poisson analysis. This leads to an polynomial time algorithm with the same regret guarantee.

</details>


### [185] [Data Leakage Inflates Generalizability of Power Outage Prediction Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24665)
*Yamil Essus,Ranga Raju Vatsavai,Benjamin Rachunok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Power outage prediction models show inflated performance under random splits due to autocorrelation, fail to generalize spatially/temporally, and offer limited operational value despite GeoAI foundation model attempts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation practices for power outage prediction models obscure whether they can generalize to novel conditions needed for climate-driven infrastructure risk assessments, creating uncertainty about their operational value.

Method: Compared predictive performance impacts of different methodological decisions using US East Coast data (2018-2023), weather reanalysis/land-cover features, and GeoAI foundation model embeddings (Prithvi WxC) under multiple test strategies: random splits, leave-one-state-out, and leave-one-event-out designs.

Result: Random train-test splits yield strong performance inflated by spatial/temporal autocorrelation. Under spatial/temporal holdouts, predictive accuracy degrades substantially, often failing to outperform null baselines. GeoAI embeddings provide limited/inconsistent improvements (primarily spatial generalization) and don't solve poor event-level transferability.

Conclusion: Current publicly trained outage prediction models offer limited operational value. Progress requires improved data coverage, more realistic evaluation protocols, and addressing structural data constraints rather than marginal modeling advances.

Abstract: Power outage prediction models are increasingly used in assessments of climate-driven infrastructure risk, yet current evaluation practices obscure whether these models generalize to the novel conditions such applications require. We identify three common methodological choices in power outage prediction models that influence their ability to generalize across spatial, temporal, and event-based settings. We compare the predictive performance impacts of different methodological decisions using publicly available data for the U.S. East Coast from 2018 to 2023 and feature sets derived from weather reanalysis and land-cover data, and embeddings from a GeoAI foundation model (Prithvi WxC). Specifically, we assess model performance under multiple test selection strategies, including unfiltered random splits, leave-one-state-out, and leave-one-event-out designs, which increasingly approximate real-world deployment conditions. While random train-test splits yield strong performance, we show that these results are inflated by spatial and temporal autocorrelation. Under spatial and temporal holdout experiments, predictive accuracy degrades substantially, with models often failing to outperform a simple null baseline. Incorporating GeoAI foundation model embeddings yields limited and inconsistent improvements, primarily for spatial generalization, and does not resolve poor event-level transferability. These findings suggest that, given current data availability and evaluation practices, publicly trained outage prediction models offer limited and uncertain operational value. Progress will likely require improved data coverage, more realistic evaluation protocols, and a shift in focus from marginal modeling advances toward addressing structural data constraints.

</details>


### [186] [A Multimodal Foundation Model for Longitudinal Patient Representation and Scalable Insight Generation in Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24688)
*Eugene Vorontsov,Yi Kan Wang,Alican Bozkurt,Adam Casson,Ludmila Tydlitatova,Michal Zelechowski,Ezra E. W. Cohen,Jyoti D. Patel,Max Banaszak,Caitlin McWilliams,Shane Colley,Kate Sasser,Ryan Fukushima,Eric Lefkofsky,Razik Yousfi,Siqi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The oFM is a foundation model trained on 1.67M cancer patients that integrates multimodal clinical, molecular, and pathology data over time to produce patient state embeddings, significantly outperforming baseline features in prognostic tasks and treatment benefit prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precision oncology requires longitudinal models that capture cancer evolution and treatment responses over time, integrating multimodal observations to understand patient state and predict outcomes.

Method: Developed the oFM foundation model on 1.67M cancer patients using patient-level partitions for training/validation/testing. The model encodes daily clinical and molecular episodes, integrates pathology images over time to produce patient state embeddings, and evaluates these against baseline clinical/molecular features.

Result: oFM significantly outperformed baseline features: improved AUC for treatment response, progression-free and overall survival (0.774 vs 0.563 for OS). Achieved 3x higher pooled treatment-benefit AUTOC across 11 cohorts, with improved benefit ranking in 9/11 cohorts. Provided stronger prognostic discrimination within treatment arms and enabled mechanism discovery via evidence-grounded temporal graphs.

Conclusion: The oFM demonstrates superior performance over traditional baseline features in multiple oncology prognostic tasks and treatment benefit prediction, enabling mechanism discovery and showing promise for clinical and drug development applications through its comprehensive longitudinal patient state modeling.

Abstract: Precision oncology necessitates a longitudinal model of patient state that captures cancer evolution and treatment over time, integrating multimodal observations. We introduce the oFM, a foundation model developed on a real-world oncology cohort of 1.67 million cancer patients that integrates clinical trajectories with DNA, RNA, and H&E pathology. Patient-level partitions were reserved for training, validation, and testing, with over one million patients used for training. The oFM encodes daily clinical and molecular episodes and, along with pathology images, integrates them over time to produce a patient state embedding. We evaluate frozen oFM embeddings against expert-curated clinical and molecular baseline features. In prognostic benchmarks, the oFM improved AUC for treatment response, progression-free survival, and overall survival (0.774 vs. 0.563 for overall survival). Across 11 comparative-treatment cohorts, the oFM embeddings achieved a three-fold higher pooled and scale-normalized treatment-benefit AUTOC than baseline features with improved benefit ranking in 9 of 11 cohorts, and provided stronger prognostic discrimination within both treatment arms. We also evaluated a mechanism discovery framework that interprets downstream models built on oFM embeddings by linking their predicted outcomes to clinically and biologically grounded mechanisms through an evidence-grounded temporal graph, enabling evaluation in clinical and drug-development applications.

</details>


### [187] [On-policy Distillation with Verifiable Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24696)
*Wenze Lin,Jiale Zhao,Xitai Jiang,Songde Rao,Yining Li,Shenzhi Wang,Bingxiang He,Gao Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OPDVR combines on-policy distillation with verifiable rewards to overcome limitations of both methods: OPD provides dense token-level guidance while RLVR adds task-level correctness, without needing extra hyperparameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR suffers from sparse task-level feedback, while OPD provides dense token-level guidance but ignores trajectory correctness and is limited to teacher performance. Combining them is promising but existing integrations require weighted combinations or heuristic switching with extra hyperparameters.

Method: OPDVR reformulates sampled-token OPD's implicit reward based on trajectory correctness, then applies a ReLU gating mechanism to ensure correct trajectories get non-negative rewards and incorrect ones get non-positive rewards, aligning distillation with task success while preserving teacher's distributional guidance. This modification transforms sampled-token OPD into a proper RLVR method compatible with policy gradient algorithms.

Result: Experiments on six reasoning benchmarks show OPDVR consistently outperforms standard OPD. The method works with policy gradient algorithms like GRPO and doesn't require extra hyperparameters.

Conclusion: OPDVR effectively combines the strengths of OPD and RLVR without adding hyperparameters, providing a seamless integration that improves performance over standard OPD across multiple reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) and on-policy distillation (OPD) have become two widely adopted paradigms for post-training large language models. However, RLVR suffers from sparse task-level feedback, while OPD provides dense token-level guidance but ignores trajectory correctness, limiting its performance to that of the teacher. Combining them is a promising direction: OPD supplies dense supervisory signals, while RLVR provides task-level correctness. Nevertheless, existing integrations often rely on weighted combination or heuristic switching, introducing extra hyperparameters and trade-offs. We propose On-policy Distillation with Verifiable Reward (OPDVR), a simple yet effective method that seamlessly combines OPD and RLVR without adding any hyperparameters. We first reformulate the implicit reward of sampled-token OPD based on trajectory correctness, then apply a ReLU gating mechanism to ensure that correct trajectories receive non-negative rewards and incorrect ones receive non-positive rewards---thereby aligning the distillation signal with task success while preserving the teacher's distributional guidance. Furthermore, our modification transforms sampled-token OPD into a proper RLVR method, making it readily combinable with any policy gradient algorithm, such as GRPO. Experiments on six reasoning benchmarks show that OPDVR consistently outperforms standard OPD. Our code is available at https://github.com/LeapLabTHU/OPDVR.

</details>


### [188] [Single State Update Predictive Coding training for Time Series Forecasting and Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24697)
*Matteo Cardoni,Sam Leroux*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Parallelizable Predictive Coding Networks with dual PCN architecture for time series anomaly detection, enabling stable online learning without sequential error propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive Coding Networks suffer from sequential backwards error propagation bottleneck, which limits their parallelization potential and online learning capabilities.

Method: Proposes training technique with paired Generative PCN and Encoding PCN that are trained in parallel to match neural activations, eliminating need for sequential error propagation.

Result: Applied to time series anomaly detection, resulting in more stable, continuous, online learning compared to traditional PCN approaches.

Conclusion: The dual PCN architecture successfully addresses the parallelization bottleneck of PC networks, enabling practical applications like time series anomaly detection with improved online learning stability.

Abstract: Predictive Coding (PC) is a neural learning paradigm that enables parallelizable neural network layer updates. However, the main bottleneck of PC Networks (PCN) is the sequential backwards error propagation. To tackle this, we introduce a training technique that pairs a Generative PCN with a support Encoding PCN. The two PCNs are trained in parallel to match their neural activations, without sequential propagation. We apply this to time series anomaly detection and show that our approach results in more stable, continuous, online learning.

</details>


### [189] [Parameter-Level Attribution of Symmetry in Trained Networks Though Parameter-Wise Functional Sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24700)
*Alan Muriithi,Vedanta Thapar,Torben Berndt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies whether learned symmetries in neural networks can be "moved" through parameter space via group actions that realize the symmetry in function space, characterizing when this is possible and testing predictions on rotation-invariant classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand if symmetries learned by neural networks can be traced through the parameterization - specifically, whether there exists a parameter-space action that realizes the symmetry group action observed in function space. This addresses fundamental questions about how networks encode and preserve symmetries they learn from data.

Method: Formulates the problem as a lifting problem for the realization map Φ: θ ↦ f_θ. Shows smooth parameter-space actions exist only when the tangent space to the function's symmetry orbit lies within the image of dΦ_θ (functional sensitivities). Proposes two local parameter directions: one following the symmetry orbit and one descending toward the equivariant subspace, with residuals indicating what the parameterization cannot reach.

Result: On rotationally invariant classifiers, predicted directions induce correct function-space motion but only locally: recomputed directions track the orbit and reduce equivariance defect, while fixed directions depart after training. Similar results hold for Hamiltonian neural networks trained on rotationally symmetric potentials despite no architectural symmetry enforcement.

Conclusion: The paper establishes conditions for lifting symmetries through neural network parameterizations and demonstrates empirically that while local symmetry-following directions exist, they don't generally form a global parameter-space action, revealing limitations in how networks encode learned symmetries.

Abstract: When a network has learned a function with a known symmetry, can that symmetry be moved through the parametrisation---is there a motion in parameter space realising the group action in function space? We formulate this as a lifting problem for the realisation map $Φ:θ\mapsto f_θ$, and show that a smooth parameter-space action exists only if the tangent space to the function's symmetry orbit lies within the image of $\mathrm dΦ_θ$, whose columns are the \emph{functional sensitivities} of individual parameters. This condition is also sufficient for pointwise first-order lifting. Relaxing it in least squares yields two local parameter directions: one following the symmetry orbit, one descending towards the equivariant subspace, with residuals measuring what the parametrisation cannot reach. On a rotationally invariant classifier we find these directions induce their predicted function-space motion, but only locally: recomputed directions track the orbit and reduce the equivariance defect, while directions held fixed depart from both after training. The same holds for Hamiltonian neural networks trained on a rotationally symmetric potential, even though the architecture does not explicitly enforce the symmetry.

</details>


### [190] [Constrained Hyperparameter Optimization for Streaming Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24712)
*Bruno Veloso,João Gama*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces four boundary constraint-handling strategies for online hyperparameter optimization in data streams, showing they outperform existing boundary strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current hyperparameter optimization research focuses mainly on batch-learning scenarios, leaving a gap for data stream applications. Online learning requires self-adjusting hyperparameters, but optimization operators often produce invalid solutions when hyperparameters have bounded search spaces, necessitating better boundary constraint-handling techniques.

Method: The paper presents four strategies for managing boundary constraints in online optimization algorithms for hyperparameter tuning. These are compared against the existing "boundary" strategy (which simply clamps values to limits when they exceed boundaries).

Result: Empirical investigations on established datasets demonstrate that the proposed boundary constraint-handling strategies outperform the conventional "boundary" strategy for online hyperparameter optimization.

Conclusion: Effective boundary constraint-handling strategies are crucial for successful online hyperparameter optimization in data stream scenarios, and the proposed approaches represent significant improvements over existing boundary strategies.

Abstract: Optimization of hyperparameters is a critical factor to obtain optimal model performance. While existing research has predominantly concentrated on batch-learning scenarios, addressing the complexities inherent in data streams presents a challenge. The deployment of sophisticated methodologies to manage data streams becomes highly important. Consequently, the capacity for self-adjusting hyperparameters during on-line learning phases emerges as a goal. Many hyperparameters exhibit constraints and are confined within bounded search spaces, rendering specific solutions unacceptable upon applying optimization operators. To solve this issue, employing boundary constraint- handling techniques becomes imperative to rectify invalid solutions. This paper presents strategies for effectively managing boundary constraints within constrained numerical optimization problems. Recent methodologies, including heuristic and evolutionary-based optimization, employ a "boundary" strategy, wherein values that surpass boundary thresholds for a given hyperparameter are realigned to the respective limits. Our study introduces four strategies to navigate boundary constraints in online optimization algorithms. Through empirical investigations conducted on established datasets, we demonstrate that adopting boundary strategies outperforms the "boundary" strategy.

</details>


### [191] [Enhancing Bayesian Optimization and Active Learning Through Kernel Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24721)
*Heng Zhang,Haotian Xiang,Qin Lu,Konstantinos D. Polyzos,Tara Javidi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KENDO is a unified framework combining Ensemble Gaussian Processes with disagreement-aware acquisition strategies for Bayesian optimization and active learning, offering computational efficiency and improved performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperparameter selection in Bayesian optimization and active learning suffers from model misspecification issues and computational inefficiency of fully Bayesian approaches (MCMC). There's a need for a more efficient yet accurate method.

Method: KENDO integrates Ensemble Gaussian Processes with adaptive Bayesian weighting instead of hyperparameter sampling, and combines this with disagreement-aware acquisition strategies. It instantiates as KENDO-BO for optimization and KENDO-AL for active learning, both sharing a common self-correcting mechanism with task-specific acquisition objectives. Extended to multi-objective optimization via random scalarization.

Result: KENDO-BO achieves competitive or superior optimization performance with up to 5× computational speedup. KENDO-AL achieves superior predictive calibration with up to 27× speedup over MCMC-based active learning baselines.

Conclusion: KENDO provides a unified, computationally efficient framework for Bayesian optimization and active learning that addresses model misspecification while maintaining or improving performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Hyperparameter selection remains a key challenge in Bayesian optimization (BO) and Bayesian active learning (AL), as model misspecification can lead to suboptimal performance, while more accurate fully Bayesian treatments typically rely on computationally expensive MCMC sampling. This paper proposes a unified framework, KENDO (Kernel ENsemble Disagreement-aware Operator), that integrates Ensemble Gaussian Processes (EGP) with disagreement-aware acquisition strategies. The central idea is to replace hyperparameter sampling with a kernel ensemble and adaptive Bayesian weighting, combined with disagreement-aware acquisition strategies. Within this unified framework, we instantiate KENDO-BO for BO and KENDO-AL for Bayesian AL, demonstrating that both arise from a common self-correcting mechanism with task-specific acquisition objectives. We further extend the approach to multi-objective optimization via random scalarization that preserves the single-optimizer conditioning structure. Thorough numerical tests on synthetic and real-world benchmarks across single-objective optimization, multi-objective optimization, and active learning demonstrate that (i) KENDO-BO achieves competitive or superior optimization performance compared to state-of-the-art methods while reducing computational overhead by up to $5\times$ and (ii) KENDO-AL achieves superior predictive calibration over MCMC-based active learning baselines with up to $27\times$ speedup.

</details>


### [192] [Parameter-Efficient Self-Supervised Adaptation for EEG-FM under Fixed Computational Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24727)
*Meghal Dani,Stefanie Liebe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Parameter-efficient self-supervised adaptation (updating only 9% of parameters) enables effective deployment of EEG foundation models in clinical settings with minimal computational overhead, achieving up to 20x AUCPR improvement over linear probing while maintaining performance with limited data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG foundation models pretrained via self-supervised learning have limited generalization across diverse clinical datasets, and full fine-tuning is impractical for resource-constrained clinical settings due to high computational requirements.

Method: Investigate parameter-efficient self-supervised adaptation that updates only 9% of parameters to align representations to target tasks. Evaluate on two state-of-the-art models (BIOT with contrastive pretraining and CBraMod with masked reconstruction pretraining) across three clinical EEG datasets for abnormality detection (TUAB), event classification (TUEV), and seizure detection (CHB-MIT), testing both in-distribution and out-of-distribution conditions.

Result: SSL adaptation yields consistent gains over linear probing (up to 20x AUCPR). Under fixed compute budget, peak performance requires only 20-50% of available unlabeled data. Performance remains invariant to patient count when total window count is fixed, suggesting dependence only on overall temporal window diversity.

Conclusion: Parameter-efficient adaptation enables effective deployment of EEG Foundation models (EEG-FM) with minimal computational overhead and data collection burden, making them practical for resource-constrained clinical settings.

Abstract: EEG foundation models pretrained via self-supervised learning promise transferable representations, but their generalization remains limited, especially across diverse clinical datasets. Full fine-tuning is impractical for resource-constrained clinical settings due to high computational requirements. In this work, we investigate whether parameter-efficient self-supervised adaptation, updating only 9% of parameters suffices to align representations to target tasks. We evaluate our method on two state-of-the-art models with different pretraining objectives: BIOT (contrastive) and CBraMod (masked reconstruction), and evaluate on three clinical EEG datasets for abnormality detection (TUAB), event classification (TUEV), and seizure detection (CHB-MIT) under both in-distribution and out-of-distribution conditions. SSL adaptation yields consistent gains over linear probing, up to 20x AUCPR. Under a fixed compute budget, peak performance requires only 20--50% of available unlabeled data. Critically, when total window count is fixed, performance remains invariant to patient count, suggesting that performance is dependent on overall temporal window diversity only. Our findings demonstrate that parameter-efficient adaptation enables effective deployment of EEG Foundation models (EEG-FM) with minimal computational overhead and data collection burden. Code available at: https://github.com/c3n-group/efficient-eeg-adapt

</details>


### [193] [Optimal Alternating Regret for Online Learning and Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24731)
*Yixin Tao,Weiqiang Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper settles the minimax-optimal alternating regret for online linear optimization and online convex optimization, achieving constant alternating regret for simplex domains and improved bounds for general convex domains, with applications to faster convergence to equilibria in game theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by alternating learning dynamics in games and aims to improve previous regret bounds for online linear optimization (OLO) over the probability simplex and online convex optimization (OCO) over general convex sets, which would lead to faster convergence to Nash equilibria and coarse correlated equilibria in game theory.

Method: The authors develop new algorithms for OLO over the probability simplex that achieve O(log d) alternating regret (constant for any time horizon T) and for general OCO that achieve O(d log(1+T/d)) alternating regret. They also provide matching lower bounds to establish minimax optimality.

Result: For OLO over Δ_d: O(log d) alternating regret (constant independent of T) with matching lower bound, improving previous O(log^{2/3}d·T^{1/3}) results. For general OCO: O(d log(1+T/d)) alternating regret improving previous O~(d^{2/3}T^{1/3}) results, with matching Ω(d log(1+T/d)) lower bound showing the log T factor is unavoidable.

Conclusion: The paper establishes minimax-optimal alternating regret bounds for both OLO and OCO, leading to the first uncoupled learning dynamics with O(1/T) convergence to coarse correlated equilibria in two-player general-sum games without additional log T factors, significantly advancing the theoretical understanding of alternating learning dynamics in games.

Abstract: We settle the minimax-optimal alternating regret, a regret notion motivated by alternating learning dynamics in games, for both online linear optimization (OLO) and online convex optimization (OCO).
  For OLO over the probability simplex $Δ_d$, we give an algorithm with $O(\log d)$ alternating regret that remains a constant for any time horizon $T$, and a matching lower bound. Our constant regret bound significantly improves previous results with $O(\log ^{2/3}d \cdot T^{1/3})$ regret [Cevher, Cutkosky, Kavis, Piliouras, Skoulakis, Viano, NeurIPS 2023, Hait, Li, Luo, Zhang, COLT 2025]. As a result, we obtain alternating learning dynamics with $O(\log d /T)$ convergence to Nash equilibria in two-player zero-sum games and $O(\log d /T)$ convergence to coarse correlated equilibria in two-player general-sum games. This is the first uncoupled learning dynamics with $O(1/T)$ convergence to CCE in two-player general-sum games, while all prior works suffer additional $\log T$ factors.
  For general OCO over a $d$-dimensional compact convex set, we give an algorithm with $O(d\log (1+T/d))$ alternating regret, improving the previous best of $\widetilde{O}(d^{2/3}T^{1/3})$. We also prove a matching lower bound of $Ω(d\log (1+T/d))$, showing that the $Ω(\log T)$ factor is unavoidable.

</details>


### [194] [$(\text{DNN})^2$: Doubly Non-Negative Relaxations for Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24743)
*Hanna Jiamei Zhang,Alan Papalia,Michael Everett,David M. Rosen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Novel eigenvalue maximization method for certifying global optimality in DNN-based neural network verification, producing tighter bounds than SDP methods and enabling scalable safety verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LP/SDP relaxations for ReLU neural network verification yield overly-conservative safety guarantees with significant relaxation gaps. While the CPP formulation closes this gap, it's NP-hard, and its cheapest tractable relaxation (DNN) has SDP constraints too large for practical scale. Standard Burer-Monteiro factorization can't handle DNN's non-unique dual multipliers for optimality certification.

Method: Proposed (DNN)² approach with novel eigenvalue maximization procedure that searches the non-unique multiplier space for valid optimality certificates. This enables global optimality guarantees for DNN formulations despite non-unique dual multipliers, overcoming certification obstacles of standard methods.

Result: Experiments show (DNN)² produces bounds consistently tighter than standard SDP method, often matching exact solutions. The certification procedure successfully confirms global optimality when valid certificates exist, demonstrating practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: The approach represents a key step toward providing tight, certifiable, and computationally scalable verification guarantees needed for deploying neural network controllers and perception modules in safety-critical autonomous systems.

Abstract: Existing linear program (LP) and semidefinite program (SDP) relaxations for rectified linear unit (ReLU) neural network (NN) verification yield overly-conservative safety guarantees due to significant relaxation gaps. While the completely positive program (CPP) formulation closes this gap, it is NP-hard to solve. Its cheapest tractable relaxation, the doubly non-negative program (DNN), retains critical constraints as an SDP, but one whose size exceeds the reach of interior-point methods at practical scale. While Burer-Monteiro (BM) factorization has been applied to make SDP-based verification scalable, no such result exists for the strictly tighter DNN formulation. A key obstacle is that additional non-negativity constraints in the DNN cause dual multipliers for optimality certification to be non-unique, making standard certification methods inapplicable. We propose a novel eigenvalue maximization procedure that searches the non-unique multiplier space for a valid certificate, i.e. a global optimality guarantee. Experiments demonstrate that our approach $(\text{DNN})^2$ produces bounds consistently tighter than the standard SDP method, often matching the exact solution, and that our certification procedure confirms global optimality when a valid certificate exists. These results are a key step toward providing tight, certifiable, and computationally scalable verification guarantees needed to deploy neural network controllers and perception modules in safety-critical autonomous systems.

</details>


### [195] [Beyond Uniform Local Isometry and Topology: FactoMap for Disentangled Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24762)
*Sohini Gupta,Bahareh Tolooshams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces factor-space structure to properly model non-Euclidean factor geometries, proposes FactoMap for learning factor-space prototypes on a lattice, and shows this approach enables disentanglement by respecting the underlying factor geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current disentanglement methods often assume Euclidean factor spaces, but real generative factors can have complex geometries involving wrapping, collapsing, or position-dependent variations that standard approaches cannot capture.

Method: Introduces factor-space structure concept (factor domains, generator-induced identifications, position-dependent scales) and proposes Factor-Space Topographic Map (FactoMap) that learns interpretable prototypes on a factor-space lattice, transferring lattice properties to representations.

Result: Shows that statistically independent factors need not be geometrically separable due to anisotropic growth rates, and that FactoMap preserves factor continuity and enables disentanglement by matching the underlying factor-space structure.

Conclusion: Proper modeling of factor-space structure is essential for disentanglement, and topographic learning approaches like FactoMap that respect non-Euclidean factor geometries are effective for learning interpretable, continuous representations.

Abstract: Many disentanglement methods represent generative factors using Euclidean product coordinates, although the underlying factor spaces may wrap, collapse, or have position-dependent geometry. We introduce factor-space structure, combining factor domains, generator-induced identifications, and position-dependent scales to distinguish topologically equivalent spaces with different factor geometries. We show that statistically independent factors need not be geometrically separable: hue and scale produce effects that grow at different rates, yielding anisotropy that no fixed rescaling removes. We propose the Factor-Space Topographic Map (FactoMap), which learns interpretable prototypes indexed by a factor-space lattice. Topographic learning transfers the lattice's periodicity, collapses, and non-uniform extent to the representation. Experiments show that matching this structure preserves factor continuity and enables disentanglement of the underlying factors.

</details>


### [196] [LION: A Clifford Neural Paradigm for Multimodal-Attributed Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24795)
*Xunkai Li,Zekai Chen,Zhengyu Wu,Henan Sun,Daohan Su,Guang Zeng,Hongchao Qin,Rong-Hua Li,Guoren Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LION proposes a Clifford algebra-based neural paradigm for multimodal-attributed graphs that addresses modality alignment and fusion limitations through geometric manifolds and adaptive holographic aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal graph ML methods have two key limitations: 1) They neglect graph context in modality alignment using topology-constrained or modality-specific operators, inhibiting modality interaction. 2) They lack adaptation in modality fusion, being simple adaptations for 2-modality graphs that fail to exploit aligned tokens with topology priors, leading to poor generalizability.

Method: LION uses Clifford algebra and decoupled graph neural paradigm (propagation-then-aggregation) for alignment-then-fusion. First constructs modality-aware geometric manifold based on Clifford algebra for high-order graph propagation enabling modality interaction. Then proposes adaptive holographic aggregation using topology-aware Clifford components of aligned tokens, integrating component-wise energy and propagation-scale information with learnable parameters.

Result: Extensive experiments on 9 text-image MAG datasets show LION significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across 3 graph and 3 modality downstream tasks.

Conclusion: LION successfully addresses modality alignment and fusion challenges in multimodal-attributed graphs through Clifford algebra-based geometric manifolds and adaptive aggregation, demonstrating superior performance on various downstream tasks.

Abstract: Recently, the rapid advancement of multimodal domains has driven a data-centric paradigm shift in graph ML, transitioning from text-attributed to multimodal-attributed graphs. This advancement significantly enhances data representation and expands the scope of graph downstream tasks, such as modality-oriented tasks, thereby improving the practical utility of graph ML. Despite its promise, limitations exist in the current neural paradigms:(1) Neglect Context in Modality Alignment: Most existing methods adopt topology-constrained or modality-specific operators as tokenizers.These aligners inevitably neglect graph context and inhibit modality interaction, resulting in suboptimal alignment.(2) Lack of Adaptation in Modality Fusion: Most existing methods are simple adaptations for 2-modality graphs and fail to adequately exploit aligned tokens equipped with topology priors during fusion, leading to poor generalizability and performance degradation.To address the above issues, we propose LION (c\underline{LI}ff\underline{O}rd \underline{N}eural paradigm) based on the Clifford algebra and decoupled graph neural paradigm (i.e., propagation-then-aggregation) to implement alignment-then-fusion in multimodal-attributed graphs. Specifically, we first construct a modality-aware geometric manifold grounded in Clifford algebra.This geometric-induced high-order graph propagation efficiently achieves modality interaction, facilitating modality alignment.Then, based on the topology-aware Clifford components of aligned tokens, we propose adaptive holographic aggregation. This module integrates component-wise energy and propagation-scale information with learnable parameters to improve modality fusion. Extensive experiments on 9 text-image MAG datasets demonstrate that LION significantly outperforms SOTA baselines across 3 graph and 3 modality downstream tasks.

</details>


### [197] [MDTE: Minority-Aware Diffusion over Temporal Edge Events for Imbalanced Node Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24812)
*Zhou Zelong,Zhang Tianming,Yang Zhengyi,Tang Yifu,Hou Chenyu,Cao Bin,Fan Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MDTE: A minority-aware diffusion framework for class-imbalanced temporal graph node classification that uses conditional diffusion denoising to reconstruct stable, discriminative temporal edge-event representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class-imbalanced node classification on temporal graphs faces challenges where majority-dominated temporal propagation assimilates minority representations, and conventional node/neighborhood information provides insufficient discriminative evidence for minority classes.

Method: Proposes MDTE with two key components: 1) Distribution-Aware Selective Propagation combining LOF-based propagation filtering with cluster-aware low-frequency propagation to preserve informative dependencies while mitigating harmful propagation; 2) Multi-View Discriminative Fusion exploiting feature reconstruction and topology prediction to characterize class-wise differences and extract complementary discriminability signals for denoising guidance.

Result: MDTE achieves best performance on minority-class-oriented metrics across five real-world datasets: improves minority-class recall by up to 23.53 percentage points, minority-class F1 by 8.68 percentage points, and AUPRC by 2.67 percentage points over strongest baselines.

Conclusion: MDTE effectively addresses class imbalance in temporal graphs by reconstructing discriminative temporal edge-event representations through diffusion-based denoising, significantly enhancing minority class performance while mitigating harmful majority-dominated propagation.

Abstract: Class-imbalanced node classification on temporal graphs is challenging because majority-dominated temporal propagation progressively assimilates minority representations, while conventional node and neighborhood information provides insufficient discriminative evidence for minority classes. To address these issues, we propose MDTE, a minority-aware diffusion framework that reconstructs stable and discriminative temporal edge-event representations through conditional diffusion denoising. Specifically, MDTE introduces Distribution-Aware Selective Propagation, which combines Local Outlier Factor (LOF)-based propagation filtering with cluster-aware low-frequency propagation. The module preserves informative neighborhood dependencies while mitigating harmful propagation and majority-class information assimilation.
