It further develops Multi-View Discriminative Fusion, which exploits feature reconstruction and topology prediction to characterize class-wise differences in distribution learning and extracts complementary discriminability signals to guide denoising. Experiments on five real-world datasets demonstrate that MDTE consistently achieves the best performance on minority-class-oriented metrics, improving minority-class recall by up to 23.53 percentage points, minority-class F1 by 8.68 percentage points, and AUPRC by 2.67 percentage points over the strongest baselines.

</details>


### [198] [Effective Learning Rate Governs Loss Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24814)
*Zihan Liu,Ruiheng Zheng,Shaobo Zhang,Changxin Tian,Kunlong Chen,Zhiqiang Zhang,Lei Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Effective Learning Rate (ELR = LR/norm) collapses loss trajectories across diverse training configurations when matched, establishing ELR as fundamental coordinate linking learning rate scheduling, norm control, and loss dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover fundamental principles governing loss dynamics in language model pretraining, particularly understanding how learning rate (LR) and parameter norm interact to affect training trajectories.

Method: Systematic experiments across optimizers, architectures, datasets, and model scales to measure collapse precision when ELR is matched; ablations on normalization design and timescale of LR-norm variation; controlled interventions on weight decay and Hyperball; development of ELR-based functional scaling laws.

Result: When ELR is matched across runs, loss trajectories collapse with mean errors ~few × 10^-3, below seed-to-seed variation; normalization design and timescale of LR-norm variation determine collapse precision; weight decay and Hyperball affect dynamics through induced ELR schedules; ELR-based functional scaling laws transfer across norm-control methods and explain delayed acceleration.

Conclusion: ELR serves as a common coordinate that fundamentally links learning rate scheduling, norm control, and loss dynamics in language model pretraining, enabling unified understanding and transferable scaling laws.

Abstract: We uncover ELR collapse in language model pretraining: learning rate (LR) and parameter norm govern loss dynamics primarily through their ratio, the effective learning rate (ELR). When ELR is matched across runs, their loss trajectories collapse throughout training despite substantially different LRs and parameter norms. Across optimizers, architectures, datasets, and model scales, mean collapse errors are typically a few x 10^-3, below the seed-to-seed variation measured in a representative configuration. Systematic ablations identify normalization design and the timescale of LR-norm variation as key determinants of collapse precision. Controlled interventions further show that weight decay and Hyperball shape loss dynamics primarily through the ELR schedules they induce. Replacing LR with ELR enables a fitted functional scaling law (FSL) to transfer across norm-control methods. The resulting ELR-based FSL also explains delayed acceleration, a recurring effect of norm control. Together, these results establish ELR as a common coordinate linking LR scheduling, norm control, and loss dynamics.

</details>


### [199] [A Geometric Theory of Robust Fairness Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24818)
*Binita Maity*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neighborhood-based fairness audits are vulnerable to small feature perturbations that can change local neighborhoods and fairness assessments, requiring new robustness analysis framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread use of neighborhood-based fairness audits to evaluate individual fairness by comparing predictions among similar individuals, little is known about the robustness of the auditing procedure itself. The audits rely on nearest neighbor relationships, making them potentially sensitive to feature perturbations.

Method: Developed a geometric framework for analyzing robustness of neighborhood-based fairness audits under bounded perturbations. Established sufficient conditions for neighborhood invariance, quantified how neighborhood replacement propagates to audit instability, and introduced audit volatility as a measure of expected sensitivity under repeated perturbations.

Result: The proposed framework explains observed stability of neighborhood-based fairness audits. Experiments on benchmark datasets support the theoretical analysis and show the framework effectively captures audit robustness characteristics.

Conclusion: Neighborhood-based fairness audits can produce different fairness assessments even with unchanged model predictions due to sensitivity to feature perturbations. The developed geometric framework provides tools to analyze and understand the robustness of these important fairness evaluation methods.

Abstract: Neighborhood-based fairness audits evaluate individual fairness by comparing predictions among similar individuals in feature space. Despite their widespread use, little is known about the robustness of the auditing procedure itself. Because these audits rely on nearest neighbor relationships, small perturbations in feature space can alter local neighborhoods and produce different fairness assessments even when model predictions remain unchanged. We develop a geometric framework for analyzing the robustness of neighborhood-based fairness audits under bounded perturbations. Our analysis establishes sufficient conditions for neighborhood invariance, quantifies how neighborhood replacement propagates to audit instability, and introduces audit volatility, a measure of the expected sensitivity of fairness audits under repeated perturbations. Experiments on benchmark datasets support the theoretical analysis and show that the proposed framework explains the observed stability of neighborhood-based fairness audits.

</details>


### [200] [BioKERN: Biological Kernel Regularization for Histology-to-Transcriptomics Neighborhood Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24823)
*Seungik Cho,Betul Orcan-Ekmekci*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioKERN is a multimodal spatial representation learning framework that explicitly incorporates biological neighborhood structure through learnable inductive biases, improving biological-neighborhood retrieval over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing histology-transcriptomics methods focus too much on instance-level matching, which can miss important biological neighborhood structure when non-paired spots share molecular or spatial context. Spatial biology requires representations that preserve biological neighborhood relationships rather than only exact cross-modal correspondences.

Method: BioKERN constructs a training-time biological kernel that combines transcriptomic similarity and spatial proximity, then uses this kernel to provide graded neighborhood supervision and regularize embedding geometry. The framework uses a fixed, model-independent biological neighborhood definition shared by all methods for evaluation.

Result: Across Mouse Brain Visium and Human Liver GSE240429 datasets, BioKERN consistently improves biological-neighborhood retrieval over BLEEP in both single- and multi-scale settings. Controlled experiments show most improvement comes from biological-kernel regularization rather than increased model capacity.

Conclusion: Explicit biological geometry serves as an interpretable inductive bias for multimodal learning in spatial biology, supporting the value of incorporating biological structure as a learnable component in representation learning frameworks.

Abstract: Spatially resolved biology requires representations that preserve biological neighborhood structure rather than only exact cross-modal correspondences. Existing histology--transcriptomics objectives can emphasize instance-level matching even when non-paired spots share molecular or spatial context. We introduce BioKERN, a multimodal spatial representation-learning framework that incorporates biological structure as an explicit, learnable inductive bias. BioKERN constructs a training-time biological kernel by combining transcriptomic similarity and spatial proximity, then uses it to provide graded neighborhood supervision and regularize embedding geometry. Evaluation uses a fixed, model-independent biological neighborhood definition shared by all methods. Across Mouse Brain Visium and Human Liver GSE240429, BioKERN consistently improves biological-neighborhood retrieval over BLEEP in both single- and multi-scale settings. Controlled shared-architecture experiments show that most of the improvement arises from biological-kernel regularization rather than increased model capacity. These results support explicit biological geometry as an interpretable inductive bias for multimodal learning in spatial biology.

</details>


### [201] [Bellman Calibration for Marginalized Importance Weighting in Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24858)
*Lars van der Laan,Nathan Kallus*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Isotonic Bellman calibration is a post-processing method that corrects occupancy-ratio estimates in offline policy evaluation by applying monotone transformations to reduce occupancy-balance violations while preserving ranking information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing estimators for marginalized importance weighting (like minimax, primal-dual, and fitted fixed-point methods) leave residual occupancy-balance violations due to approximation errors, regularization, or incomplete optimization. These violations are hard to diagnose and reduce because there's no direct supervised validation loss for hyperparameter tuning and model selection.

Method: Introduces isotonic Bellman calibration, a one-dimensional, model-agnostic post-processing method that applies fitted occupancy-ratio evaluation (FORE) over a class of nondecreasing transformations to correct the scale and shape of initial occupancy-ratio estimates. The method preserves ranking information while reducing occupancy-balance violations.

Result: The method achieves Bellman calibration as a conditional fixed-point property equivalent to occupancy-balance against every test function. The paper establishes finite-sample calibration guarantees and a KL oracle inequality relative to the best monotone transformation of the initial estimate.

Conclusion: Isotonic Bellman calibration achieves small calibration error and KL risk within statistical error of the best monotone correction, with theoretical guarantees for downstream target-occupancy functionals including policy-value estimation, providing a robust solution to address occupancy-balance violations in offline policy evaluation.

Abstract: Marginalized importance weighting evaluates a target policy by reweighting offline state-action samples with its discounted occupancy ratio, characterized by an adjoint Bellman equation. Existing minimax, primal-dual, and fitted fixed-point estimators can leave residual occupancy-balance violations because of function-class approximation, regularization, or incomplete optimization. These violations are difficult to diagnose and reduce because the objectives generally lack a direct supervised validation loss for hyperparameter tuning, model selection, and early stopping. We introduce isotonic Bellman calibration, a one-dimensional, model-agnostic post-processing method that reduces these violations while preserving the ranking information in any initial occupancy-ratio estimate. The method corrects the estimate's scale and shape by applying fitted occupancy-ratio evaluation (FORE) over a one-dimensional class of nondecreasing transformations. We characterize Bellman calibration as a conditional fixed-point property equivalent to occupancy-balance against every test function of the calibrated ratio. More generally, we derive a calibration-refinement bound showing that any fitted ratio with small calibration error performs nearly as well as the best post-processing based on its fitted values. For isotonic Bellman calibration, we establish finite-sample calibration guarantees and a KL oracle inequality relative to the best monotone transformation of the initial estimate. Consequently, isotonic Bellman calibration achieves small calibration error and KL risk within statistical error of the best monotone correction, with guarantees for downstream target-occupancy functionals, including policy-value estimation.

</details>


### [202] [Improving Cross-Problem Vehicle Routing with Locally Augmented Preferences and Representation Disentanglement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24859)
*Arthur Corrêa,Paulo Nascimento,Samuel Moniz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: POLAR (Preference Optimization with Locally Augmented Refinement) and PLE (Progressive Layered Extraction) encoder together improve multi-task VRP solving by addressing training limitations and representation entanglement in existing neural solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-task VRP solvers have two main limitations: training challenges (RL suffers from reward-scale disparities and shrinking advantages; preference optimization stagnates with near-identical solutions) and architectural issues (fully shared encoders entangle constraint-dependent representations across heterogeneous variants, limiting generalization).

Method: Two model-agnostic contributions: 1) POLAR training algorithm applies local search refinement to best decoded tours before forming preference pairs for more informative pairwise margins; 2) PLE encoder uses gating mechanism to route each layer through shared expert + task-specific experts, progressively separating common routing structure from constraint-specific encodings.

Result: Significant performance improvements: 21.3% reduction in average gap to reference solutions on 16 in-distribution variants relative to strongest published baseline; outperforms prior neural methods on 27 out of 32 unseen variants. Ablation studies confirm efficacy across multiple backbone architectures.

Conclusion: POLAR and PLE together elevate state-of-the-art for neural multi-task VRP solvers by addressing both training supervision limitations and architectural representation entanglement, enabling better cross-problem generalization across diverse VRP variants.

Abstract: Multi-task vehicle routing problem (VRP) solvers seek to handle multiple VRP variants within a single unified model, avoiding the need to train a separate model for every variant. In spite of recent progress, current approaches remain limited on two fronts. On the training side, reinforcement learning suffers from reward-scale disparities and shrinking advantage signals as policies improve, whereas preference optimization stagnates once sampled tours become near-identical and thus fundamentally limited by the quality of the policy's own generated solutions, leaving both paradigms with weak supervision as training progresses. On the architecture side, existing fully shared encoders entangle constraint-dependent representations across heterogeneous variants, which limits generalization. We address these gaps with two model-agnostic contributions. First, we propose Preference Optimization with Locally Augmented Refinement (POLAR), a novel training algorithm that applies a local search refinement pass to the best decoded tour before forming preference pairs, yielding much more informative pairwise margins. Second, a Progressive Layered Extraction (PLE) encoder routes each encoder layer through one shared expert and a set of task-specific experts via a gating mechanism, progressively separating common routing structure from constraint-specific encodings. Through extensive experiments on various VRP variants, we show that POLAR and PLE together elevate the current state-of-the-art among neural multi-task solvers. We reduce the average gap to reference solutions by 21.3% relative to the strongest published baseline on 16 in-distribution variants, and outperform prior neural methods on 27 out of 32 unseen variants. Ablation studies confirm the efficacy of each contribution, showing that both improve cross-problem generalization across multiple backbone model architectures.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [203] [Markets, Not Planners: Decentralized Orchestration of LLM Agents with Private Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23867)
*Xiao Liu,Haoyang Li,Songwei Li,Hongbo Fang,Fengli Xu,Feng Shi,James Evans*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: AgentLance is a decentralized labor-market-inspired system for orchestrating LLM agents where agents bid on tasks using private costs, with VCG-style payments and hierarchical delegation, outperforming centralized approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent orchestration is centralized, creating bottlenecks as agent pools grow, requiring private information (like execution costs), and being vulnerable to manipulation where a single inserted preference can nearly double a favored agent's task share.

Method: AgentLance creates a repeated labor market where agents bid on tasks using private costs and self-maintained strategy notes, an allocator selects winners from bids and public reputation records, uses VCG-style payment rules to reward cost-aware bidding, and handles complex tasks through hierarchical delegation where winning agents can decompose work and subcontract.

Result: Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, knowledge-intensive QA, and agentic tasks, AgentLance matches agents to their specializations, shifts work toward cheaper agents as cost sensitivity rises, and consistently outperforms single-model, centralized-orchestration, and market baselines. Further gains were achieved by diagnosing and correcting market failures like inaccurate cost self-estimation and sub-optimal bidding.

Conclusion: AgentLance demonstrates a more efficient approach to LLM agent orchestration through decentralized market mechanisms, charting a path toward more efficient agent economies by addressing limitations of centralized systems.

Abstract: As LLM agents proliferate, built by different parties and with different capabilities and costs, orchestrating them is more like assembling labor across the economy than a computer calling a subroutine. Existing orchestration is typically centralized, with a single planner assigning every task, but this creates a bottleneck as agent pools grow, requires private information (e.g., agents' execution costs), and can easily be manipulated, such that a single inserted preference nearly doubles a favored agent's task share under a centralized LLM allocator. We introduce AgentLance, a repeated labor market in which agents bid on tasks using their private costs and self-maintained strategy notes, an allocator selects winners from bids and public reputation records, and a VCG-style payment rule rewards cost-aware bidding. Complex tasks are handled by hierarchical delegation: winning agents can decompose work and subcontract it through the same mechanism. Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, knowledge-intensive QA, and agentic tasks, AgentLance matches agents to their specializations, shifts work toward cheaper agents as cost sensitivity rises, and consistently outperforms single-model, centralized-orchestration, and market baselines. Diagnosing market failures, including inaccurate cost self-estimation and sub-optimal bidding, then correcting them in controlled experiments yields further gains, charting a path toward more efficient agent economies.

</details>


### [204] [Agentopia on a Consumer GPU: A Reduced-Scale Long-Horizon Port with an 8B Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24215)
*Luo Huan*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper implements a reduced-scale version of Agentopia multi-agent social simulation on consumer hardware (RTX 5070 Ti), adapting it for efficient operation with quantized models and introducing structural changes to manage memory constraints while maintaining stable agent behavior over long simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous LLM-based multi-agent social simulations like Agentopia were evaluated with 100 agents over 10 years using large models, but the behavior of reduced-scale deployments on consumer hardware remains unclear. The paper aims to bridge this gap by implementing a more accessible version that runs on standard consumer-grade GPUs.

Method: Implemented a reduced-scale Agentopia port on NVIDIA RTX 5070 Ti (12GB VRAM) using Qwen3-8B-AWQ (4-bit quantized model). Introduced three structural adaptations: (1) system-managed layered memory compression, (2) four activity blocks per simulated day instead of continuous time, and (3) explicit physical- and mental-health state variables. Conducted three independent stochastic runs to evaluate performance.

Result: Two runs completed 52 weeks and one completed 50 weeks before hitting context limits, totaling 154 system-weeks (770 agent-weeks). No agents died and no health warnings were triggered. Activity records with NO_RESPONSE fields occurred at 10.15-10.29% across runs. Additional experiments showed that four daily time blocks produced 2.72 times more finalized records with lower lexical duplication but higher missing-field rates compared to continuous time.

Conclusion: The reduced-scale implementation successfully demonstrates stable long-term agent behavior on consumer hardware with structural adaptations for memory management. While the comparisons don't support causal claims about agent behavior, the system provides a viable framework for more accessible multi-agent social simulations. The authors release their implementation changes and configurations but withhold raw data due to provenance concerns.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent social simulation has demonstrated compelling results, but Agentopia was evaluated with 100 agents over 10 simulated years using Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, leaving the behavior of reduced-scale deployments on consumer hardware unclear. In this paper, we implement and evaluate a reduced-scale Agentopia port on a single NVIDIA RTX 5070 Ti(12 GB VRAM) using Qwen3-8B-AWQ, a 4-bit quantized model. We introduce three structural adaptations for this setting: (1) system-managed layered memory compression, (2) four activity blocks per simulated day, and (3) explicit physical- and mental-health state variables. Across three independent stochastic runs, two runs completed 52 weeks and the third completed 50 weeks before reaching the context limit, totaling 154 system-weeks (770 agent-weeks). No agent died,and no threshold-based health warning was logged; activity records containing at least one NO_RESPONSE field occurred at rates of 10.15-10.29% across runs. A 52-week memory-off run tied L2/L3 artifact production to layered memory; a separate 10-week comparison associated four daily time blocks with 2.72 times more finalized records and lower lexical duplication, but a higher missing-field rate. These comparisons do not support causal behavioral claims. We release validated configurations, derived audits, analysis scripts, aggregate figure data, and our implementation changes in a public fork; raw runs and initial persona data are excluded because their redistribution provenance is not fully resolved.

</details>


### [205] [Test-Time Collaborative Classification over Multi-Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24787)
*Ping Hu,Mert Kayaalp,Ali H. Sayed*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Distributed binary classification framework where independently trained heterogeneous agents coordinate at test time through limited communication to form collective predictions, with theoretical error guarantees under communication constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing challenges of multi-agent system heterogeneity in joint training while leveraging cooperative inference as a powerful mechanism for distributed decision making over networks.

Method: Independent training of agents (potentially differing in architecture, feature space, or modality) followed by cooperative inference at test time through exchange of local decision statistics using distributed learning protocols with limited communication.

Result: Established classification error guarantees under sufficient, finite-round, and finite-precision communication, with PAC-style generalization bounds. Experimental results reveal trade-offs between independent training costs and collective prediction benefits.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates the price of independent training versus the benefit of collective prediction in distributed decision making with data-learned models, capturing effects of heterogeneity, network topology, combination policies, and communication constraints.

Abstract: The increasing heterogeneity of multi-agent systems poses significant challenges for jointly training a global model across agents. At the same time, cooperative inference between agents has long been recognized as a powerful mechanism for distributed decision making over networks. Motivated by these observations, we propose a collaboration framework for distributed binary classification over multi-agent networks, where a set of independently trained agents, potentially differing in architecture, feature space, or modality, coordinate their actions during test time to form collective predictions. This coordination is achieved by exchanging local decision statistics through a distributed learning protocol. We develop a theoretical and experimental study of this independent training and cooperative inference paradigm, and examine its performance under different communication budgets and distributed learning rules. We establish classification error guarantees under sufficient, finite-round, and finite-precision communication, together with PAC-style generalization bounds. These results capture the influence of model heterogeneity, network topology, combination policy, and communication constraints on prediction accuracy. Taken together with the experimental results, they reveal both the price of independent training and the benefit of collective prediction for the proposed distributed decision making framework with models learned from data.

</details>
